The case is still hashed into both streams, its subject calls are **not made**, and the suite row counts the case in `unreachable_by_type` and the calls it would have made in `unreachable_calls`. The call check becomes `calls + unreachable_calls == expected_calls`, so a skipped call is always accounted for and never passes as made. Because a lane could otherwise declare everything unreachable and call nothing, three things bind the count: it is zero for every type whose row below holds the full range (every `U`/`I` type in every lane, by bit pattern), so only `Nat` and composites containing it can be unreachable; the manifest checker recomputes it from the corpus and the binding's `values` line without running the subject; and a suite with a non-zero count is labelled `type-limited` and can never be `full` (section 9.3). The result check is exact: an integral value of the wrong representation (a float `1.0` for `U8`, a boxed object for a `long`) is `type-mismatch`.

| Lane | `U64` / `I64` at the boundary | narrow types (`U8..U32`, `I8..I32`) | `Nat` |
|---|---|---|---|
| rust, c, cpp, go, csharp, fsharp, swift | native 64-bit, unsigned / signed | native width | `u64`-sized where the host type holds it |
| kotlin | `ULong` / `Long` | `UByte..UInt` / `Byte..Int` | `ULong` |
| haskell | `Word64` / `Int64` | `Word8..Word32` / `Int8..Int32` | `Natural` or `Word64` (values past `Word64` are unreachable by type) |
| ocaml | `Int64` with unsigned operations (`Int64.unsigned_compare`, `unsigned_div`); `int` (63-bit) is **refused** | `int` is allowed only for widths of at most 32 bits | `Int64` |
| python, ruby, elixir | big integers, **range-checked** (not masked) at the boundary; Python results checked with `type(x) is int`, so a `bool` is refused; Elixir with `is_integer/1` and `===`, so `1.0` is refused; Ruby with `Integer` exactly | same | same |
| typescript, dart (web) | `bigint` / `BigInt`; `number` is **refused** | `number` is allowed at the boundary only, and only when integral, in range and not `-0` (`Object.is`); the evaluator stays exact | `bigint` |
| java | `long` carrying the bit pattern; unsigned compare/div/rem through `Long.compareUnsigned`, `Long.divideUnsigned`, `Long.remainderUnsigned`; `java.math.BigInteger` in the evaluator | `U8` as `byte` (bit pattern) or `int` (range-checked); `U16` as `short` or `int`; `U32` as `int` (bit pattern) or `long` (range-checked) | `long` (a value above `2^63-1` is unreachable by type) or `BigInteger` |
| lua | Lua 5.3+ integers; `U64` is the 64-bit pattern, compared with `math.ult`; the runner **refuses to start** if `math.type` or `math.ult` is absent (Lua 5.1, 5.2, LuaJIT) | integers in range; a float result is `type-mismatch` even if integral | integer `0..2^63-1` (larger is unreachable by type) |
| perl | native `UV`/`IV` only if `$Config{ivsize} >= 8 && $Config{uvsize} >= 8`, else `Math::BigInt`; **`use integer` is never used**; a result carrying a float flag (`NOK`) is `type-mismatch` | integer scalars in range | same as `U64` |

**Why Java, Lua, Perl and JavaScript need a table at all.** Their native integer type is signed 64-bit (Java, Lua), a double (JavaScript `number`) or either depending on the build (Perl). The corpus contains `u64_edge` values from `2^63` upward precisely so a lane that silently holds `U64` in a signed or floating type fails an enumerated case, not a code review. The runner's start-up checks (`math.type`, `ivsize`, `typeof 1n`) turn a wrong toolchain into exit 2 instead of a wrong verdict.

### 7.3 Bytes, lists, records and variants

`Bytes` and `Bytes<N>` use a **closed list of representations per lane**, checked by exact type (VER04i sections 5.1 and 5.2): `bytes` (Python), `Uint8Array` with an exact prototype and a non-detached buffer (TypeScript), `byte[]` (Java, C#, Kotlin), `[]byte` (Go), `uint8_t *` plus a length (C), a byte string or binary where the language has no byte array (Lua: a string; Perl: a byte string for which `utf8::downgrade` succeeds, so **wide characters are rejected**; Elixir: a binary). `Bytes<N>` additionally asserts the length equals `N` on the way in and out (a wrong-length result is a failure, never padded).

`List<T>` binds to the lane's sequence type named in `values` (`List=`) (reserved with `ir.list`: no lane binds or enumerates a list before the amendment, [D63]) and a tuple to the host form named by `Tuple=` (a host tuple, a fixed-length array, or multiple return values where the language has them); a component or element type with no host mapping is `BindingInvalid`, and a returned tuple of the wrong arity is `type-mismatch`. Records and variants bind through `type` lines: `type Point host=Point fields=x,y` gives the exact host field names in the corpus field order, and `type Decoded host=Decoded ctors=Short,Done` gives the constructor names. Positional matching is never used for constructors; the name is the portable key (VER04j section 4.9). A lane with no sum types binds a result as a tagged struct, which is a binding concern.

### 7.4 Aliasing and freshness

1. Every mutable argument is a **fresh copy for each call**, and no two arguments of one call share storage. (Aliasing is legal in C, Java, C# and Go; the adapter is what makes it impossible.)
2. After the call, every argument that is **not** `inout` must equal its pre-call copy. A difference is the new failure id **`arg-mutated`**.
3. A `fresh` result must not share storage with any argument: the runner mutates the result and re-reads the arguments, then mutates an argument and re-reads the result (VER04i section 5 rule 4). `fresh=immutable` is accepted only where the type is immutable by construction (Elixir binaries, Python `bytes`). An absent `fresh=` on a `fresh` op means `probe`, and for a composite result `immutable` is required of the container and of every component (section 16.19 R83).
4. An `inout` post-state is read from **the very object that was passed**, after the call, never from a copy taken before (VER04k section 4.6 rule 2). A fixed-length form must keep its length; a changed length is `type-mismatch`.

### 7.5 Error mapping

Mapping is **closed and matched exactly**. A host error discriminator is one of: a raised class (a built-in, or, new in v2, a class exported by the bound module and resolved at start-up), a Go sentinel (`errors.Is` on the exact value), a C status code, an Elixir tag, a `Left` constructor, or `panics` (only with a single-case `error=`).

* A raised class matches only when the raised object's class **is** the mapped class (`type(e) is cls`; `Object.getPrototypeOf(e) === cls.prototype`); a subclass, a duck-typed object and a thrown non-`Error` are `unexpected-error`. A bare `Error`/`Exception`/`BaseException` mapping is refused, so a binding cannot swallow arbitrary failures.
* `err_any=CASE` requires an error type with **exactly one case** (VER04j section 14.4): `Err(_)` does not say which error it was, so with two or more cases a wrong error would pass vacuously.
* For a multi-case error the runner **refuses a binding in which two cases of one op share a discriminator**; otherwise a wrong error would map to the same case.
* Anything unmapped is `unexpected-error`; a model `Err` answered with a value is `missing-error`.

Two shipped ports already force this: TypeScript `gf256` throws a plain `Error` (which the binding refuses) and Go `gf256` panics. Slice S8 gives TypeScript a dedicated error class and binds Go with `panics` plus a single-case `error=`.

### 7.6 `inout` and `ensures` in a lane

The corpus follows VER04k section 4.9: the result is encoded as its declared type and `ops.<op>` names `inout` and `post_slot`. The runner's adapter, **written once per lane**, (a) keeps the oracle's pre-state and gives the subject a fresh copy, (b) reads the post-state back from the object it passed, (c) assembles the Warrant result by the slot rule (S1 to S4), and (d) destructures the host return exactly: an extra or missing return component is `type-mismatch`.

The binding names a **host form per `inout` parameter**:

| Form | Meaning | Rust forms of VER04k section 4.6 it covers |
|---|---|---|
| `mutable` | a fixed-length mutable byte array, mutated in place | `slice`, `array` |
| `growable` | a growable sequence (Java `ByteArrayOutputStream`/`List<Byte>`, Go `*[]byte`, C buffer plus `size_t *len`) | `vec` |
| `cell` | a one-element holder, for languages with no reference to an integer or boolean | `int:<ht>`, `bool` |
| `returned` | the export returns the post-state as (part of) its result; immutable languages (Haskell, Elixir, OCaml, Python `bytes`) | `returned` |

The record forms (`struct:`, `slice-of:struct:`, `vec-of:struct:`, `codec:`) are reserved: a corpus using them is `not-live` (level `ir.inout.records`) until a later slice; what a binding that names one meets is [D67] (section 16.19). A form must fit the type of its parameter: `mutable` and `growable` are forms of `Bytes` and `Bytes<N>` only, `cell` of `Bool`, `Nat` and the fixed-width integers only, and `returned` fits every type (section 16.19 R92). **`ensures` rows** are IR. The runner's call wrapper evaluates them after every call whose arguments satisfy `admit`; a false row fails the case with the note `postcondition-violated: <op> row <i>`, and an unevaluable row fails with `postcondition-unevaluable`. The compiler emits the synthesized `warrant/<m>/exhaustive/<op>/post` suites as **predicate** suites (section 4.3), plus `postcondition_rows` and `monitored_calls`, which the lane manifest must reproduce.

`monitored_calls` must not depend on the subject, or a lane could not be checked against it. Whether a call is monitored depends on its arguments satisfying `admit`, and the arguments of an outer call in `add(add(a,b),c)` are subject results. So the corpus commits, per op and per depth (`{"slim": n, "full": n}`), the count of calls of that op whose arguments are **subject-independent** and admitted; the lane must reproduce the number for the depth it ran. Calls with subject-dependent arguments are monitored in exactly the same way (a false row still fails the case) and are reported in `monitored_dynamic`, which is informational and is never compared.

A row may use any built-in, including WX-D ones (VER04k section 4.2): `zeroize`'s `all(result, is_zero)` needs `ir.seq`. An op whose rows need a level the lane lacks, or a reserved level, has its `/post` suite and its monitoring recorded `not-live`; the rows are never partly evaluated.

**Honest limits.** A `returned` binding does not observe in-place mutation, and the report says so (`inout_form`, section 9.1). No lane gives evidence that a wipe survives the optimiser: `zeroize` shows the post-state is zero, not that the compiler kept the store (VER04i section 11).

## 8. Faults and sabotage controls

### 8.1 A fault is data

VER04i section 4.2 made each fault a per-lane wrapper with a name-keyed table (`faults.ts`, the Python equivalent). v2 moves the table into the corpus:

```json
{"fault": "generator-shift", "op": "multiply", "kind": "post",
 "expr": <pure IR over the op's params and result>, "requires": ["ir.core"]}
```

There are exactly three kinds:

| Kind | Effect |
|---|---|
| `pre` | rewrites parameters before the call: a `rewrites` list of `{"param": "a", "expr": ...}`, each expression over **all** the op's parameters as they were passed in, and all rewrites applied **simultaneously** (one rewrite never sees another's output). The row has `rewrites` instead of `expr`; a fault that rewrites every `Bytes` or every `U64` parameter (section 8.2) has one entry per such parameter [D11a] |
| `post` | rewrites the result: `expr` over params and `result` |
| `replace` | supplies the result without trusting the call's value (the call is still made and counted) |

`result` is the one reserved name for the call's result, in a fault expression and in an `ensures` row alike (section 6); an op with a parameter named `result` is a generation error (`W0701`) [D11a]. (The first draft wrote `r` here; two spellings would have been two reserved names.) A lane implements these three wrappers **once** and deletes its per-fault table. Fault expressions use only tags of their declared level and **never `subject_op`**.

### 8.2 Generic and adopter-specific faults

The portable names of VER04i section 4.2 become IR templates instantiated by signature; a few examples (full list in `corpus.json.faults`):

| Fault | Kind | IR (sketch) | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| `invert` | post | `["not", result]` | core |
| `missing-error` | replace | `if is_err(result) then Ok(first Bytes arg) else result`; **every payload from S11 [D50]**, exact forms in the `missing-error` block below the table | core (tuple or data by payload) |
| `swap-choice` | pre on `choice` | `["not", choice]` | core |
| `off-by-one` | post | `["add", result, ["lit", {type: T, value: "1"}]]` (wraps, section 3; `T` is the result type, `Nat` included) | core |
| `truncate-last` | post | `if len(result) > 0 then take(result, len(result) - 1) else result` | core |
| `ignore-last-byte` | pre on each `Bytes` param | `concat(take(p, len(p) - 1), b"00")` guarded by `len(p) > 0` | core |
| `xor-lsb` | post | `["bit_xor", ["var","result"], ["lit", {"type": T, "value": "1"}]]` (`T` the result type) | fixed |
| `ignore-high-bit` | pre on each `U64` param | `["bit_and", ["var", p], ["lit", {"type":"U64","value":"9223372036854775807"}]]` | fixed |
| `flip-result-bit` **[D27]** | post | exact JSON in the block below the table | fixed (+ tuple inside `Ok`) |
| `drop-last-result-byte` **[D27]** | post | exact JSON in the block below the table | core (+ tuple inside `Ok`) |
| `ok-as-err`, `drop-consumed`, `swap-components` | post | composite rewrites of VER04j section 4.8 over `Result` and tuples, written with `match` on `Result` and the tuple literal | tuple [D35] |
| `off-by-one-consumed` **[D36]** | post | the second component `n` of a tuple result, or of the tuple inside an `Ok`, becomes `n + 1` when `n` is even and `n - 1` when it is odd (VER04j section 4.8); exact JSON in the second block below the table | fixed + tuple |
| `field-zero` **[D49]** | post | the first `Nat` or fixed-width field of a record result, or of the record inside an `Ok`, becomes `0` (VER04j section 4.8); exact JSON in the `field-zero` block below the table | data (+ tuple inside `Ok`) |
| `wrong-ctor` | post | a composite rewrite of VER04j section 4.8 over variants. **Deferred to its first adopter [D49]**: not in the registry and no JSON; a profile that names it is refused (code `fault`) | data |
| `saturate-add`, `saturate-sub` **[D51]** | replace | the saturating sum or difference of the first two parameters, whatever the call returned (VER04j section 2.7); exact JSON in the last block below the table | fixed |
| `shift-mask` **[D51]** | pre on the last parameter | the last parameter `p` becomes `p mod w`, `w` the width of the first parameter's type (VER04j section 3.7); exact JSON in the last block below the table | fixed |

**`ignore-high-bit` stays `ir.fixed` [D9].** ct-compare's `eq_u64` control uses it, and ct-compare is otherwise an `ir.core` document, so until slice S10 teaches the generator, the oracle and the lanes `ir.fixed`, that one control is recorded **`not-live`** in every lane (it is counted in `not_live`, never skipped and never an `uncaught-fault`), and the lane cannot be `release-ready` for ct-compare while it is (section 4.1). An `ir.core` rewriting of the same fault (`if p >= 2^63 then p - 2^63 else p`) was considered and declined by the owner: the table keeps one definition per fault, in the level whose built-in it names. Because a generator that has not implemented `ir.fixed` cannot evaluate this fault, it emits the fault row as data (`requires: ["ir.fixed"]`) and the control row with `requires: ["ir.fixed"]`, every candidate claim in `required_claims` and `slim_witness` entries `{"claim": C, "deferred": "ir.fixed"}` (section 5.6; these are not `unproven`, since `eq_u64_agrees` has a stand-in and only the evaluator is missing), and leaves the witness search to S10. The fail-closed rule that an unimplemented level refuses a *suite* (section 13, "Slice S3 in detail") does not apply to a fault whose level only gates a control, because no **suite or chunk digest** depends on the fault. The same is not true of the slim digests in general: a witness chunk feeds `slim_chunks` and so `slim_inputs_sha256`. For ct-compare nothing changes, because every suite has `m <= 16` and so is `full` in a slim run (section 5.6), but when S10 adds the witness search this control row changes (its `slim_witness` entries) and so can the slim sets of any suite with `m > 16`.

**`flip-result-bit` and `drop-last-result-byte`: exact forms [D27, D35; normative from S10c-1 (oracle) and S10c-2 (generator)].** `R` is `["var","result"]`, `N0` is `["lit",{"type":"Nat","value":"0"}]` and `N1` is the same with `"1"` (the `Nat` literal form of the committed ct-compare faults), `U1` is `["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"1"}]`, `U0` the same with `"0"`, and `B01`, `B00` are `["lit",{"type":"Bytes","value":"01"}]` and `...,"value":"00"}]` (a `Bytes` literal is lowercase hex, section 2.3; the empty-result values are the Rust harness's `01` and `00`, which the x25519 adopter PR confirms against the bindings before its fixture is committed). Let `GT` be `["gt",["len",R],N0]`.

| Fault and result type | `expr` (kind `post`) | `requires` |
|---|---|---|
| `flip-result-bit`, fixed-width result `T` | `["bit_xor", R, ["lit",{"type":"T","value":"1"}]]` (the `xor-lsb` form; `T` is the type's name) | `["ir.fixed"]` |
| `flip-result-bit`, `Bytes` **or `Bytes<N>`** (one template; the `if` wrapper is emitted for `Bytes<N>` too, whatever `N`) | `["if", GT, ["concat", ["be_bytes", ["bit_xor", ["unwrap_or", ["at", R, N0], U0], U1]], ["drop", R, N1]], B01]` | `["ir.fixed"]` |
| `drop-last-result-byte`, `Bytes` or `Bytes<N>` (a fixed-width result is not a template: the generator code `fault`) | `["if", GT, ["take", R, ["sub", ["len", R], N1]], B00]` | `["ir.core"]` |
| either, result `Result<X,E>` with `X` as above | `["match", R, [[["ok",["bind","ok_payload"]], ["ok", F]], [["err_any"], R]]]` where `F` is the `expr` of the row above with `["var","ok_payload"]` in place of `R` throughout | the union of the row's level and `ir.tuple` |

Typing: `concat(be_bytes(..), drop(..))` has the type `Bytes` (dynamic) and so does the `if`; for a `Bytes<N>` result the faulted value is therefore typed `Bytes` (inside `["ok", F]`, for a `Result<Bytes<N>,E>`, the payload of the faulted `Ok` is likewise `Bytes`) and a consumer compares and streams it as `Bytes` (the flip keeps its length; the drop deliberately does not, which is the fault). `be_bytes` of a `U8` is `Bytes<1>` (Appendix A.1); the collision rule applies only to the `Result` fault forms that introduce the `ok_payload` binder: there, an op parameter named `ok_payload` (a fault expression sees only the op's parameters, `result` and `ok_payload`; claim binders are never in scope) is a generator `fault` error, as one named `result` is `W0701` (author refinement, S10t-1 review). A result `Result<X,E>` with `X` a tuple, a variant or anything not in the table has no template (the generator code `fault`, nothing skipped). Because `match` on `Result` is `ir.tuple` [D35], these rows are **live** from the S10c flip and never `deferred` (the plan's deferral to S11 is superseded, section 16.10 D27). A fault row's `requires` may therefore hold more than one level (section 16.6 item 3 said one; the sorted union of R9 applies). The registry keeps `xor-lsb` and `truncate-last`; the two new names are the Rust bindings' spellings, which section 8.4 requires the profile to equal.

**`off-by-one-consumed`: exact form [D36; normative from S10o-1 (oracle) and S10o-2 (generator), section 13].** This is the fault of VER04j section 4.8 ("right value, wrong bytes consumed") as the Rust harness applies it (`fault_off_by_one_consumed` in an adopter's generated `warrant_evidence.rs`; VER04j section 14, item 23): the **second** component `n` of the result tuple becomes `n + 1` when `n` is even and `n - 1` when `n` is odd, so the pair `2k, 2k+1` is exchanged, both directions occur, and the value never leaves its type (`0` goes to `1`; the largest value of every `Nat` and fixed-width type is odd and goes down; a signed minimum is even and goes up). It is a `post` fault and is a different name from the core `off-by-one` (`result + 1` on a scalar result); both stay in the registry.

*Applies to* exactly the result types `drop-consumed` applies to: a tuple `(T1, T2, .., Tk)`, or `Result<(T1, T2, .., Tk), E>`, whose second component `T2` is `Nat` or a fixed-width type (the wasm-leb128 decoders are `Result<(U64,Nat),Leb128Error>` and `Result<(I64,Nat),Leb128Error>`). Any other result (a non-numeric second component, a scalar, `Bytes`, a `Result` whose payload is not a tuple, an `Option`, a record or a variant) has no template: the generator code `fault`, nothing skipped. (Until S11a an `Option` result never reaches the template in a real document: [D23] refused any `Option` in a signature at lowering, in both lowerers, with the level `ir.data` (generator code `level`); only the oracle's bare `fault_row`, as used by the `off-by-one-consumed-v1` parity dump, reached the `fault` code. From S11a and S11b an `Option` in a signature is lowered ([D48], section 16.14) and such a result reaches the `fault` code in both tools.)

`R`, `N1` are as above; `I0` is `["lit",{"type":"Int","value":"0"}]` and `I2` the same with `"2"`. `S` is the tuple the template reads: `R` for a bare tuple result and `["var","ok_payload"]` inside an `Ok`. `C` is `["project", S, 1]`, the second component (the IR has no `let`, so `C` is written out at each use).

| Part | JSON |
|---|---|
| `X`, the new second component, `T2` = `Nat` | `["if", ["eq", ["unwrap_or", ["rem", C, I2], I0], I0], ["add", C, N1], ["sub", C, N1]]` |
| `X`, `T2` a fixed-width type `T` | `["bit_xor", C, ["lit",{"type":"T","value":"1"}]]` (`T` is the type's name) |
| `F`, the faulted tuple | `["tuple", [["project", S, 0], X, ["project", S, 2], .., ["project", S, k-1]]]` (every component but the second is projected unchanged, in order) |
| `expr`, bare tuple result | `F` with `S` = `R` |
| `expr`, result `Result<(T1, .., Tk), E>` | `["match", R, [[["ok",["bind","ok_payload"]], ["ok", F]], [["err_any"], R]]]` with `S` = `["var","ok_payload"]` (the `Ok` form of the block above) |

`requires` is `["ir.fixed","ir.tuple"]` in all four combinations: `rem`, `unwrap_or` and `bit_xor` are `ir.fixed`, the tuple literal, `project` and `match` on `Result` are `ir.tuple`, and, as for `flip-result-bit`, `ir.core` is not listed although the `Nat` form uses `if`, `eq`, `add` and `sub` (section 16.10 R9). For both wasm-leb128 result types the row's `expr` is this JSON value (written compactly here; the canonical writer decides the layout of a dump or a corpus):

```json
["match",["var","result"],[[["ok",["bind","ok_payload"]],["ok",["tuple",[["project",["var","ok_payload"],0],["if",["eq",["unwrap_or",["rem",["project",["var","ok_payload"],1],["lit",{"type":"Int","value":"2"}]],["lit",{"type":"Int","value":"0"}]],["lit",{"type":"Int","value":"0"}]],["add",["project",["var","ok_payload"],1],["lit",{"type":"Nat","value":"1"}]],["sub",["project",["var","ok_payload"],1],["lit",{"type":"Nat","value":"1"}]]]]]]],[["err_any"],["var","result"]]]]
```

*An `Err` result is returned unchanged* (the `err_any` arm returns `R`), as the harness does; the fault changes **every** `Ok` value and no `Err` value, so a claim whose stand-in expects only `Err` is proven blind (section 5.6), as for `drop-consumed`.

*Typing.* `rem` of a `Nat` and an `Int` is `Option<Int>` (Appendix A.1, section 16.10 R7), consumed directly by `unwrap_or` ([D23]) whose default `I0` is an `Int` literal, so no operand relies on widening; the divisor is the literal `2`, so the `Option` is never `None`, the default is never taken, and the floor remainder is `0` or `1`. `add` and `sub` on a `Nat` operand are exact and have the type **`Int`** (section 3.2; the core `off-by-one` on a `Nat` result is typed the same way by both tools). `X` is therefore statically `Int` in a component the op declares `Nat`, `F` is `(T1,Int,..)`, and the two arms of the `match`, `Result<(T1,Int,..),?>` and the op's `Result<(T1,Nat,..),E>`, have the common type `Result<(T1,Int,..),E>` (a `Nat` component widens to `Int`; the `Result` of a bare `ok` takes the other arm's error type). **No narrowing is written and none is needed.** The IR has no `Int`-to-`Nat` conversion (the target of `try` and `wrap` is fixed-width, and `to_nat` takes an unsigned fixed-width operand), and a fault expression's static type is not required to equal the op's result type (the `Bytes<N>` forms above are typed `Bytes`). What matters is the **value**, and it is always a `Nat` value: `sub` is evaluated only when `n` is odd, so `n >= 1` and `n - 1 >= 0`; `add` only when `n` is even, and a `Nat` value is in `0..=2^64-1` (section 2.3), whose largest member is odd, so `n + 1 <= 2^64-1`. A consumer holds, compares and (if it ever must) streams the faulted component as the `Nat` it is; no `ir-overflow` can occur and nothing wraps (`wraps` counts neither a `Nat`/`Int` node nor `bit_xor`, [D31]). For a fixed-width `T2` the form is `flip-result-bit`'s: `bit_xor` of `T, T` is `T`, and the faulted payload has exactly the result's type.

*Exact against the Rust harness on every value.* For `Nat` the harness computes `if n % 2 == 0 { n + 1 } else { n - 1 }` on a non-negative integer, which is the expression above. For a fixed-width component it tests `n % 2 == 0` on the value and adds or subtracts `1` on the `w`-bit pattern with wrapping; an even pattern has bit 0 clear, so `+ 1` sets it without a carry, and an odd one has it set, so `- 1` clears it without a borrow: the result is the pattern with bit 0 flipped, for negative values too, and the wrapping never happens. `bit_xor` with `1` is that function exactly. The two places the harness goes further are outside what a v2 fault row can express, so no value differs: it also rewrites a tuple inside a `Some` (an `Option` result is `ir.data` and has no template here), and it leaves a tuple with a non-numeric second component unchanged at run time (VER04j refuses that binding at generation, `W0701`; v2 refuses it with the code `fault`).

*Scope and names.* The expression sees the op's parameters, `result`, and, in the `Ok` form, `ok_payload`; claim binders are never in scope. An op parameter named `ok_payload` is a generator `fault` error for the `Ok` form (the bare-tuple form introduces no binder and does not refuse it), and one named `result` is `W0701`, exactly as for the `Ok` forms of `flip-result-bit` and `drop-last-result-byte`. *Liveness.* The fault gates are open for `ir.fixed` and `ir.tuple` (the S10c flip), so the row is **live** in both tools and never `deferred`; a lane records the control `not-live` until its runner declares both levels (section 4.1). *Cost* (section 4.2 rule 6; a check for implementers, not a new limit): on a pair, the `Nat` form costs 16 bare and 19 in the `Ok` form, the fixed-width form 7 and 10. **This section's JSON is normative** (unlike the faults of section 16.10 R11, whose dump is): the dump S10o-1 commits must reproduce it, and a difference is an erratum to this section before any fixture names the fault.

**`missing-error`: one rule for every payload [D50; normative from S11f-1/S11f-2 for a scalar or `Bytes` payload and from S11c-1/S11c-2 for a payload that needs `ir.data`, section 13].** `missing-error` is a `replace` fault on any op whose result is `Result<X,E>`; a result that is not a `Result` has no template (the generator code `fault`, nothing skipped). With `R` = `["var","result"]` the row's `expr` is

```text
["if", ["is_err", R], ["ok", P], R]
```

where `P` = `build(X)`, defined on the payload type and, through tuples and records, on their component and field types (the generalisation of section 16.10 R3; section 16.14 R31):

| Type `X` | `build(X)` |
|---|---|
| a tuple `(T1,..,Tn)` | `["tuple", [build(T1), .., build(Tn)]]` (R3, unchanged) |
| a record `N` with field types `T1..Tn` in declaration order | `["ctor", "N", [build(T1), .., build(Tn)]]` |
| a **leaf**: `Bool`, `Nat`, a fixed-width type, `Bytes`, `Bytes<N>`, `Option<T>` or a variant | `["var", p]` for the first parameter `p`, in parameter order, whose type is **exactly** `X`; if the op has none, the **zero** of `X` |
| zero of `Bool` | `["lit", false]` |
| zero of `Nat` or of a fixed-width type `T` | `["lit",{"type":"Nat","value":"0"}]`, `["lit",{"type":"T","value":"0"}]` |
| zero of `Bytes`, of `Bytes<N>` with a literal `N` | `["lit",{"type":"Bytes","value":""}]`, the same with `2N` zero digits as the value (a named width has no zero literal: code `fault`). The literal has the type it is the zero **of**: plain `Bytes` for the first, not `Bytes<0>` (section 16.17 R67) |
| zero of `Option<T>` | `["none", "T"]` (Appendix A.2) |
| zero of a variant; any type that is or contains a list | none: no template (code `fault`) until an adopter needs one (a variant's default value is the same decision `wrong-ctor` needs, [D49]; lists are `ir.list`) |

A tuple or a record is always **built**, never taken whole from a parameter of its type; that is R3 as the tools have implemented it since S10t, so no committed dump moves. **One exception, kept for byte parity with every committed row:** when the payload `X` itself (not a component or a field) is `Bytes` or `Bytes<N>`, `P` is `["var", p]` for the first parameter whose type is `Bytes` **or `Bytes<M>` for any `M`** (the "first `Bytes` arg" of the table above, the rule both tools have had since S3c: a `Bytes<4>` parameter is taken for a `Result<Bytes,E>` result), and the zero is used only when the op has no such parameter (new: such an op was refused before).

`requires` is `["ir.core"]` when `P` is a `var` or a `lit`; otherwise it is `ir.tuple` if `P` contains a `tuple` node together with `ir.data` if `P` contains a `ctor` or a `none` node, sorted, and `ir.core` is not listed (as for the tuple form since S10t-1); the levels of the op's own types are added as for every fault row (section 16.10 R9 as amended). The static type of `P` is assignable to `X`, and when `X` is a tuple or a record it **is** `X` (section 16.17 R67: a zero literal is typed at the type `build` was asked for, so the empty literal written for a component declared plain `Bytes` is a `Bytes` and the built tuple has the declared tuple type exactly, which an invariant tuple needs) (every leaf is a parameter declared with exactly its type or a literal of it; at a stand-in's static widths a `Bytes` parameter is typed `Bytes<N>`, as in the tuple form since S10t), so the `if` is well typed exactly as the existing forms are. *Cost* (a check, not a limit): `4 + cost(P)`, so 5 for a leaf payload and `5 + n` for a record of `n` leaf fields.

For the two S11 adopters (the rows the S11f-1 and S11c-1 dumps must reproduce; names are the specs'):

| Op and result | `P` | Template levels |
|---|---|---|
| `and_gate(a: U8, b: U8) -> Result<U8,GateError>` (and the other ten one- and two-input gates, `mux2` with `d0`, `mux4` with `d0`) | `["var","a"]` | `ir.core` |
| `and_n(inputs: Bytes) -> Result<U8,GateError>` (also `or_n`, `xor_n`, `mux_n`) | `["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"0"}]` | `ir.core` |
| `demux(data: U8, sel: Bytes, n_outputs: Nat) -> Result<Bytes,GateError>` | `["var","sel"]` (the existing form; `decoder` and `encoder` take `inputs`) | `ir.core` |
| `priority_encoder(inputs: Bytes) -> Result<(Bytes,U8),GateError>` | `["tuple",[["var","inputs"],["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"0"}]]]` (the existing R3 form) | `ir.tuple` |
| `tri_state(data: U8, enable: U8) -> Result<Option<U8>,GateError>` | `["none","U8"]` | `ir.data` |
| `ripple_carry_adder(a: Bytes, b: Bytes) -> Result<RippleCarryResult,ArithError>` | `["ctor","RippleCarryResult",[["var","a"],["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"0"}],["lit",false]]]` | `ir.data` |
| `ripple_carry_adder_with_carry(a: Bytes, b: Bytes, carry_in: U8)`, the same result | `["ctor","RippleCarryResult",[["var","a"],["var","carry_in"],["lit",false]]]` | `ir.data` |
| `alu(a: Bytes, b: Bytes, op: AluOp) -> Result<AluResult,ArithError>` | `["ctor","AluResult",[["var","a"],["lit",false],["lit",false],["lit",false],["lit",false]]]` | `ir.data` |

*Not value-identical to the Rust harness, by decision [D50], as [D37] decided for tuples.* The harness returns the **first element of the payload's domain** on a contained panic: `Ok(0)` for `Result<U8,E>` (where this template gives `Ok(a)`), `Ok(None)` for `tri_state` (the same value as here), and the record of each field's first domain value (`RippleCarryResult(b"", 0, false)`, `AluResult(b"", false, false, false, false)`) where this template passes the first operand through. Both turn **every** `Err` into an `Ok` and leave every `Ok` alone, so the claims that catch one catch the other: the ones that demand a rejection. The difference is recorded in each adopter's fixture README.

**`field-zero`: exact form [D49; normative from S11c-1 (oracle) and S11c-2 (generator), section 13].** This is the fault of VER04j section 4.8 as the Rust harness applies it (`fault_field_zero` in an adopter's generated `warrant_evidence.rs`): the **first numeric field** of a record result, in declaration order, becomes `0`, inside an `Ok` for a `Result`. It is a `post` fault.

*Applies to* a result that is a record `N`, or `Result<N,E>` with `N` a record, where `N` has at least one field whose type is `Nat` or a fixed-width type; `k` is the 0-based index of the first such field and `T` its type. Any other result has no template (code `fault`, nothing skipped): a record with no numeric field (arithmetic's `AluResult`), a variant, a tuple, a scalar, `Bytes`, an `Option` (of a record too) and a `Result` whose payload is not a record.

`R` is `["var","result"]`; `S` is the record the template reads, `R` for a bare record result and `["var","ok_payload"]` inside an `Ok`; `Z` is `["lit",{"type":"T","value":"0"}]`.

| Part | JSON |
|---|---|
| `F`, the faulted record of `n` fields | `["ctor", "N", [["field", S, 0], .., Z, .., ["field", S, n-1]]]` (`Z` in position `k`; every other field is projected unchanged, in order) |
| `expr`, bare record result | `F` with `S` = `R` |
| `expr`, result `Result<N,E>` | `["match", R, [[["ok",["bind","ok_payload"]], ["ok", F]], [["err_any"], R]]]` with `S` = `["var","ok_payload"]` (the `Ok` form of the blocks above) |

`requires` is `["ir.data"]` for a bare record and `["ir.data","ir.tuple"]` in the `Ok` form (`ctor` and `field` are `ir.data`, `match` on a `Result` is `ir.tuple`; `ir.core` is not listed although `lit` and `ok` are core tags, as above). For both arithmetic adders (`RippleCarryResult(sum: Bytes, carry_out: U8, overflow: Bool)`, `k` = 1) the row's `expr` is this JSON value (compact; the canonical writer decides the layout):

```json
["match",["var","result"],[[["ok",["bind","ok_payload"]],["ok",["ctor","RippleCarryResult",[["field",["var","ok_payload"],0],["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"0"}],["field",["var","ok_payload"],2]]]]],[["err_any"],["var","result"]]]]
```

*Typing.* `["field", S, i]` has the declared type of field `i` and `Z` the type of field `k`, so `F` has exactly the type `N` and the row's static type is the op's result type; nothing widens. *Exact against the Rust harness on every value.* The harness finds the first field whose value is an integer (`V::Int` or `V::Fixed`; a field's declared type decides which, and `Int` is never a field type, section 2.1), replaces it with the zero of the same kind, leaves every other field alone, does so inside an `Ok`, and returns an `Err` unchanged: the expression above. The places the harness goes further are outside what a v2 row expresses: it also rewrites a record inside a `Some` (no template here until an adopter binds one), and VER04j refuses at generation the record with no numeric field that v2 refuses with `fault`. *An `Err` result is returned unchanged*, so a claim whose stand-in expects only `Err` is proven blind (section 5.6). *Scope and names* are those of the other `Ok` forms: an op parameter named `ok_payload` is a `fault` error for the `Ok` form only, one named `result` is `W0701`. *Cost* (a check): `2n` bare and `2n + 3` in the `Ok` form (6 and 9 for `RippleCarryResult`). *Liveness.* Each tool evaluates `ir.data` faults from its own S11c PR ([D52]: no flip); the row is never `deferred` in a committed corpus. **This block's JSON is normative**, with one proviso the other blocks do not have: `ctor` and `field` are Appendix A.2 tags, provisional until S11a's committed dump ([D47]); if that dump changes a tag shape, this block changes with it in the same erratum.

**`saturate-add`, `saturate-sub` and `shift-mask`: exact forms [D51; normative from S11f-1 (oracle) and S11f-2 (generator), section 13].** These are three of the scalar faults of VER04j sections 2.7 and 3.7 as the Rust harness applies them (`V1_FAULTS` in `rust/warrant-grammar/src/evidence.rs`: `Replace("p0.saturating_add(p1)")`, `Replace("p0.saturating_sub(p1)")` and `PreLast("p % {w}")`; in logic-gates' generated `warrant_evidence.rs` the arms `Fault::SaturateAdd => p0.saturating_add(p1)`, `Fault::SaturateSub => p0.saturating_sub(p1)` and the line `let p0 = if fault == Fault::ShiftMask { let p = p0; p % 8 } else { p0 };`). logic-gates binds them on 21 of its 51 fault lines. They need no `ir.data` and are independent of S11a to S11c.

*`saturate-add` and `saturate-sub` apply to* an op with at least two parameters whose **first two** parameters `p0`, `p1` (by position; further parameters are ignored, as for `mux2`) have one and the same fixed-width type `T`, and whose result is `T` or `Result<T,E>`. Anything else has no template (code `fault`): a `Nat` or non-integer first or second parameter, two different widths, a result of another type (`Bool`, `Nat`, `Option<T>`, `Bytes`, a composite). The kind is **`replace`**. With `A` = `["var", p0]`, `B` = `["var", p1]`, `MAX` = `["max_value","T"]`, `MIN` = `["min_value","T"]` and `NEG` = `["lt", B, ["lit",{"type":"T","value":"0"}]]` (`T` is the type's name throughout):

| Fault and `T` | `V`, the saturated value |
|---|---|
| `saturate-add`, `T` unsigned | `["unwrap_or", ["checked_add", A, B], MAX]` |
| `saturate-add`, `T` signed | `["unwrap_or", ["checked_add", A, B], ["if", NEG, MIN, MAX]]` |
| `saturate-sub`, `T` unsigned | `["unwrap_or", ["checked_sub", A, B], MIN]` |
| `saturate-sub`, `T` signed | `["unwrap_or", ["checked_sub", A, B], ["if", NEG, MAX, MIN]]` |

| Result type | `expr` |
|---|---|
| `T` | `V` |
| `Result<T,E>` | `["ok", V]` |

The template's levels are `["ir.fixed"]` in all eight combinations (`checked_add`, `checked_sub`, `unwrap_or`, `max_value` and `min_value` are `ir.fixed`; `ir.core` is not listed although `ok`, `if`, `lt` and `lit` are core tags, as above: the levels are the ones the template declares, not the union over its tags), and the row's `requires` adds the levels of the op's own types as for every fault row (section 16.10 R9), which is nothing for an op whose parameters and result are scalars, so `["ir.fixed"]` for every logic-gates row. For logic-gates' nine two-input gates (`T` = `U8`, parameters `a`, `b`) the two rows' `expr` are these JSON values (`mux2`'s `saturate-add` is the first with `d0`, `d1`):

```json
["ok",["unwrap_or",["checked_add",["var","a"],["var","b"]],["max_value","U8"]]]
["ok",["unwrap_or",["checked_sub",["var","a"],["var","b"]],["min_value","U8"]]]
```

*Exact against the Rust harness on every input.* `saturating_add` and `saturating_sub` return the exact sum or difference when it is in `T` and the nearer bound of `T` otherwise. `checked_add`/`checked_sub` are `Some` of the exact value iff it is in `T` (Appendix A.1), so `unwrap_or` returns it whenever nothing overflows, and its default is the bound: an unsigned sum can only pass `MAX` and an unsigned difference can only pass `0`, which is `MIN`; a signed sum overflows only when both operands have one sign, upwards iff `p1 > 0` and downwards iff `p1 < 0`, so the bound is `MIN` when `p1 < 0` and `MAX` otherwise (`p1 = 0` never overflows, so its choice is never used); a signed difference `p0 - p1` overflows upwards only when `p1 < 0` and downwards only when `p1 > 0`, so the bound is `MAX` when `p1 < 0` and `MIN` otherwise. The default operand is always evaluated (`unwrap_or` does not short-circuit, Appendix A.1) and is total. Under these faults the harness **does not call the export at all**: the arm replaces the call, nothing can panic, and the adapter returns `Ok` of the value on every input, including the inputs the real op rejects; the v2 row is `["ok", V]` without a test of `result`, and by section 8.1 a `replace` fault still makes and counts the call and ignores its value. So the faulted result is the same value on every input. The equality is of the **Warrant** types: the harness computes on the host types of the binding, which are the Warrant types unless an `args=` entry says otherwise (none of the ten logic-gates ops that bind the two saturating faults has one); section 16.14 R33 records the condition.

*Typing.* `V` has the type `T`. `["ok", V]` has the type `Result<T,?>`: the expression fixes no error type, and none is needed, because a fault expression's static type is not required to equal the op's (the `Bytes<N>` forms above) and the value is always an `Ok`. *Cost* (a check): `V` is 5 for an unsigned `T` and 9 for a signed one; the `Result` form adds 1. *Blindness.* The row never reads `result`, so on a stand-in claim it is blind exactly where the claim's expected value already equals `Ok` of the saturated value on every case.

*`shift-mask` applies to* an op whose **first** parameter has a fixed-width type of width `w` bits (8, 16, 32 or 64) and whose **last** parameter `p` (the same parameter when the op has one) has a fixed-width type `L`, signed or unsigned. Anything else has no template (code `fault`): a `Nat` first or last parameter (the harness computes those as `u32`; no adopter binds one), a non-integer one. The kind is **`pre`** with the single rewrite `{"param": p, "expr": X}`:

| `L` | `X` |
|---|---|
| unsigned | `["unwrap_or", ["rem", ["var", p], ["lit",{"type":"L","value":"w"}]], ["lit",{"type":"L","value":"0"}]]` |
| signed | `["unwrap_or", ["rem_trunc", ["var", p], ["lit",{"type":"L","value":"w"}]], ["lit",{"type":"L","value":"0"}]]` |

(`L` is the type's name and `w` the decimal width, `"8"`, `"16"`, `"32"` or `"64"`.) The template's level is `["ir.fixed"]`, and the row's `requires` adds the levels of the op's own types, as above. For logic-gates' `not_gate(a: U8)` and `nand_not(a: U8)` the row's `rewrites` is this JSON value:

```json
[{"param":"a","expr":["unwrap_or",["rem",["var","a"],["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"8"}]],["lit",{"type":"U8","value":"0"}]]}]
```

*Exact against the Rust harness on every value.* The harness rewrites the last argument to `p % w` before the call, in the argument's own integer type, with `w` the width of the **first** argument's type, and then calls the export, containing a panic as it does without the fault. Rust's `%` on an unsigned type is the remainder and on a signed type the **truncating** remainder (the sign of the dividend): `rem` on an unsigned pair and `rem_trunc` on a signed pair are those functions (Appendix A.1; `rem` refuses a signed fixed-width pair, which is why the signed form names `rem_trunc`). The divisor literal always fits `L` (`w <= 64` and the smallest signed maximum is 127) and is never `0` or `-1`, so the `Option` is never `None` and the default `0` is never taken. A `pre` fault's rewritten parameter then reaches the real op, as in the harness. `X` has exactly the type `L`. *Cost* (a check): 5. The same host-type condition as above applies (R33).

*Scope, names and liveness of the three.* The expressions see the op's parameters only (the `replace` forms do not read `result`) and introduce no binder, so there is no `ok_payload` refusal; a parameter named `result` is `W0701` as everywhere. The fault gates are open for `ir.fixed` since the S10c flip, so the rows are **live** in both tools from the PR that adds them and are never `deferred`. **This block's JSON is normative**: the dump S11f-1 commits must reproduce it, and a difference is an erratum to this section before any fixture names the faults. Checked against `warrant-oracle` 0.5.0 before this erratum was written (section 16.14 R33).

**Adopter-specific faults** (`alt-poly`, `exp-mod-256` in gf256) reference **fault-only models** emitted under `fault_models`: pure model bodies that may exist *only* for a fault and may never appear in a claim, so the corpus cannot be made to depend on them.

**WX-G faults** are all `post` rewrites over the result and the pre-state parameters: `stale-post` (result is the pre-state), `skip-last-write`, `drop-last-push`, `clobber-prefix`, `cursor-off-by-one`, with the applicability table of VER04k section 4.8. Two of those conditions name a host form (`drop-last-push` and `clobber-prefix` need a growable buffer, `cursor-off-by-one` an integer cell), which the language-neutral corpus cannot know. The corpus therefore lists the control with `applies_to_forms: [...]` in section 7.6 vocabulary; a lane whose `inout_form` for that parameter is not listed records the control as `not-applicable` with the form, and the manifest checker verifies that against the op row's `inout_form`. A lane cannot mark any other control not-applicable.

### 8.3 Controls, scoping and catching

VER04i section 4 is otherwise unchanged and applies to v2:

* Controls are per `(target suite kind, op, fault)`. Each perturbs **exactly one op**; a control passes iff the suite run with that single-op fault fails with a registered failure id. The scoping exists because a fault applied to every operation can cancel inside a relational claim: XOR-ing the low bit of every result turns `mul(a,b) == mul(b,a)` into `mul(a,b)^1 == mul(b,a)^1`, which is still true. This matters *more* in v2, where relational predicates are common.
* Each control row lists `required_claims`, the claims (by suite id) not proven blind (section 5.6), and `blind_claims`, the claims proven unable to witness the fault; the manifest's `caught_by` must be a non-empty subset of `required_claims`. An unrelated failure cannot credit a control, and a blind claim never appears in `required_claims`. A control no claim can catch is `uncaught-fault` and fails the lane unless the corpus's `accepted_blind_faults` names its control id (a fault is accepted by an `accept_blind <op> <fault>` line of `corpus.profile`, section 8.4, [D15]). A **not-live** control (section 4.1) is the one exception: the lane does not run it, its manifest row has `caught_by: []` and the state `not-live` with its `level`, and it is neither caught nor `uncaught-fault`; it does not fail the lane, but it counts in `not_live` and so blocks `release-ready`. A not-live row with a non-empty `caught_by`, or a control recorded `not-live` whose `requires` levels the lane declares, is rejected by the manifest checker. The control row is `{"id": "warrant/<module>/negctl/exhaustive/<op>/<fault>", "op", "fault", "target_kind", "requires", "required_claims", "blind_claims", "slim_witness"}` plus `applies_to_forms` for the WX-G faults of section 8.2 (a key that is not applicable is omitted, section 1.4). `requires` is the level of the fault's expression, and is what a lane that lacks it uses to record the control `not-live` (section 4.1).

**Accepted-blind controls must be visible [normative, S3d review].** An accepted control appears in `corpus.json` only through the top-level `accepted_blind_faults` list (its control row has an empty `required_claims`), so every consumer must surface it, never fold it into a pass: (a) a lane manifest records it with the distinct control state `accepted-blind` and `caught_by: []`, never `uncaught-fault` and never caught; (b) the report counts such controls and shows the count like `not_live`; (c) the report validator (S9) rejects an `accepted_blind_faults` id that has no control row, or whose control row has a non-empty `required_claims`, and rejects a control with empty `required_claims` whose id is not listed. **A non-empty `accepted_blind_faults` blocks `release-ready` [D17]**, like a not-live control (sections 4.1 and 9.1).

* A faulted or filtered run **never writes a manifest**. The call counter (`calls == expected_calls`) and the fact that a faulted run must reach the real export remain the guards against a runner that never calls the library (VER04i section 4.4).
* In a `slim` run controls run against the `slim` chunk set and stop at the first failing case.

### 8.4 `corpus.profile`

Fault selection and the knobs of this document live in a **neutral line file**, `code/specs/fixtures/<module>-warrant-v2/corpus.profile`, read by the compiler and by the oracle's independent reader and by nothing else:

```text
budget 16777216
chunk_size 65536
slim_stride auto
max_ir_cost 32768
fault multiply generator-shift
fault multiply alt-poly
fault zeroize stale-post
```

`max_ir_cost` is optional (default 2,000, at most 65,536; section 4.2 rule 6).

`omit_example <id>` **[D22, D34]** removes one example from `corpus.json` `examples`, **but only when the tool can decide that the example is inexpressible** for one of two named reasons. `<id>` is the example's full id as `examples[].id` spells it (`warrant/<module>/example/<name>`). The profile line carries **only the id**; the author never asserts the reason, the tool computes it, and records it in the `omitted_examples` row `{"id": ..., "reason": ...}` (section 1.4), sorted by id. The closed reason vocabulary:

* `not-op-eq-pure`: the example is not of the shape `op(args) == pure` (section 1.4 `examples`; the generator's refusal for any other shape is `W0701`). Example: lzw's two `match`-shaped bomb examples.
* `value-over-limit`: a value the example needs (an argument, or the pure side) exceeds `MAX_IR_VALUE` (section 4.2) or the 4,096-byte `Bytes` value limit of section 2.3, found by evaluating the example's argument and pure expressions with the size checked **before** the allocation (as rule 6 requires). Example: lzw's `bomb512`/`bomb1024` arguments of about 142,848 and 285,696 bytes.

**Shape (author refinement, S10-1 review).** `op(args) == pure` is defined exactly as the generator's `lower_example` (`W0701`) defines it: **exactly one subject call, its arguments free of subject calls, on one side of an equality, the other side pure** (no subject call). A body that is not an equality, has no subject call, has subject calls on both sides, has a subject call that is not the whole of its side, or has a subject call inside an argument (`echo(echo(x)) == y`) is `not-op-eq-pure`. **First error (author refinement, S10-1 review, ruling 2):** "first error" in the order below means evaluation **stops** at the first error: a size error in an earlier argument qualifies even if a later argument would fail with a different error. **Levels (ruling 3):** an omitted example is still lowered and must be within the tool's implemented levels; a level refusal refuses the document even when the example has an `omit_example` line. **Evaluation order (ruling 1):** arguments, then the pure side, then the [D19] check, then the [D18] check; the generator's S10-1 PR adopts this order. If both apply the reason is `not-op-eq-pure` (the shape is tested first; author refinement, section 16.10 R4). **Normative order of the `value-over-limit` decision [author refinement, section 16.10 R4; S10-1, both tools, which must follow it identically even though the generator evaluates per argument and the oracle evaluates all arguments first]:** (1) evaluate the arguments left to right, then the pure side; (2) any evaluation error **other than** a size error refuses generation; (3) a size error qualifies as `value-over-limit` **only if it is the first error** met in that order; (4) if the evaluation completes, run the D18 and D19 checks first and refuse on failure; (5) only after that does a final value over the 4,096-byte limit of section 2.3 qualify. An example that fails (2) or (4) is never rescued by the line. A size error raised while evaluating a callee's `admit` (the D19 check) refuses; only a size error in the arguments or the pure side qualifies (author refinement, CV2-F1). **Nothing else qualifies.** A v0 wrap (section 3.3 rule 2, [D18]), a call outside the callee's `admit` ([D19]) and any other check failure still refuse generation for that example: the line never rescues them, and an omitted example is simply not evaluated, not written to `cases.tfv`, not stream-encoded and not checked against an `admit`. Error cases, in the style of `accept_blind` below (generator code `profile`, oracle code `corpus`): a malformed line (wrong token count, an empty token) is an error; an `<id>` that names no example of the spec is an error; a second `omit_example` line with the same id is an error; a line of whitespace alone is an error like any other; and **a line for an example that is emittable is an error** (an omission that excuses nothing is a stale profile, [D15] refinement 2), with the message naming the id and never echoing example values. A document with zero examples left is accepted. An omitted example does **not** block `release-ready`; every report shows the count beside `not_live` and the S9 validator prints the ids [D34] (implemented by the lane runners, S6 and S7, and S9; no check enforces it before). The parsing and the reason are implemented in slice S10-1 (section 13), oracle first and then the generator (which adopted the evaluation order above for every example, and defers its non-`op == pure` `W0701` for an example that has an `omit_example` line); the two agree byte for byte on `tests/fixtures/omit-example-v2`.

`accept_blind <op> <fault>` **[D15]** accepts a fault that every candidate claim is blind to (section 5.6). It must repeat a `fault` line of the same file, may not repeat itself, and names a fault that really is all-blind: a line for a fault some claim can see (witnessed, unproven or deferred) is a profile error, because an acceptance that excuses nothing is a stale profile. The accepted faults are recorded in `accepted_blind_faults` as **control ids** (`warrant/<module>/negctl/exhaustive/<op>/<fault>`), sorted by bytes (section 16.8). Only an empty line is skipped: a line of whitespace alone is a profile error.

The compiler checks that its fault names equal the Rust bindings' `faults=` lists for the same module, so the two cannot drift; the oracle checks the same file against `corpus.json`.

## 9. Manifest additions

### 9.1 Schema `warrant-lane-evidence-v2`

The v1 manifest of VER04i section 8, as a new closed key set, plus:

| Where | Field | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| top level | `depth` | `"full"` iff every live suite ran every chunk, else `"slim"`; the depth of the *run*, set by the runner from what it executed, never copied from the binding or from a command-line flag. It says nothing about `not-live` suites, so every place that prints `depth` prints `not_live` beside it |
| top level | `levels`, `not_live` | the IR levels implemented, and the count of suites **and controls** recorded `not-live` (section 4.1) |
| suite row | `depth` | `"full"` or `"slim"` for this suite (a suite with `m <= 16` is `full` in any run) |
| suite row | `chunks_run`, `chunks_total`, `cases_run` | what actually executed |
| suite row | `chunks` | for each executed chunk, its recomputed `inputs_sha256`/`expected_sha256` (the sidecar-free place the checker compares) |
| suite row | `oracle` | `reference` or `relational` |
| suite row | `domain_label` | `full`, `bounded-set` or `sample` (VER04i section 8 sidecar, moved into the row: LP-012) |
| suite row | `unreachable_by_type`, `unreachable_calls` | cases a host type could not hold, and the subject calls not made for them (section 7.2) |
| suite row | `wraps` | informational (section 3.3) |
| top level | `conformance_sha256` | digest of **the conformance cases the lane ran**, defined below the table [D61]; never the digest of `index.json` (section 11; amended by section 16.17, the earlier text said "digest of the conformance fixture's `index.json` the runner passed") |
| top level | `conformance_run` | an object: category name to the count of that category's cases the lane ran, with a key only for a category that has at least one; defined below the table [D61] (section 16.17 R66) |
| op row | `inout_form` | the host form used per `inout` parameter, so a `returned` binding shows as such |
| `ensures` ops | `monitored_calls`, `postcondition_rows`, `monitored_dynamic` | the first two reproduced from the corpus for the depth run; the third informational (section 7.6) |
| control row | `applicable` | `true`, `not-applicable` with the host form (section 8.2), or `not-live` with `level`, the level the corpus control row's `requires` names and the lane lacks (sections 4.1, 8.2); a `not-live` control has an empty `caught_by` (section 8.3) |

**`release-ready` [D17].** A lane is `release-ready` for an adopter only when it has zero not-live suites, zero not-live controls (`not_live` is 0) **and zero accepted-blind controls** (the report's count of controls in the state `accepted-blind`, section 8.3). The report shows that count beside `not_live` wherever it prints `depth`. This slice states the rule; the lane runners (S6, S7) and the report validator (S9) enforce it.

**A corpus that needs more than the lane's levels [D64; section 16.18 R69].** A lane never refuses a corpus because a part of it needs a level the lane lacks. Reading the corpus, it classifies every model, op, suite and control by the levels of what it reaches. A suite or control whose reach is inside the lane's `levels` is live: it is compiled, validated and run as section 14 requires. One whose reach is not is recorded `not-live` and counted in `not_live` (section 4.1). A model or an op that needs a missing level is marked not-live in the loaded corpus and is not compiled; it makes not-live every suite and control that reaches it, and nothing else; the checks that read tag names alone still apply to it (section 16.18 R69 (c), tier 2). So a lane at `ir.core` runs the `ir.core` suites of a mixed corpus and reports the others as not-live. The manifest key set does not change: `not_live` still counts suites and controls, and a model or an op that needs a missing level has no row; the not-live suite and control rows that reach it name the deferring level [D65]. Everything that is not a question of level is still checked for the whole corpus before the first case: the digests, the key sets, the limits of section 14, and an unknown tag or level name anywhere; and a model or op of an implemented level is compiled and validated in full whether or not a live suite reaches it (the three tiers of section 16.18 R69 (c)).

**What a lane pins of the conformance kit [D61; section 16.17 R62 to R66].** Let `L` be the manifest's `levels`: it names every level the runner implements, `ir.core` included, and is closed under the implications of section 4.1; the subset test below is on names and applies no implication. A case of the fixture family of section 11 is **run** when it is a case of one of the four v1 categories (`json`, `tagged`, `containment`, `label-digest`: their cases carry no `requires` and every lane runs all of them) or a case of a `v2-*` category whose `requires` is a subset of `L`. Every other case is **skipped** and is no part of the manifest (a category that is neither a v1 category nor a `v2-*` one is refused by the index check of section 11 before any selection). Then:

* `conformance_sha256` is the lowercase-hex SHA-256 of the bytes made by taking the run cases in ascending order of `id` and concatenating, for each, the **canonical document of the case object**: the whole object as it stands in the `cases` array of its category file, every key included, written by the canonical writer of VER04i section 9.1 (keys sorted by code point, two-space indent, booleans and null as the literals, one final LF). Nothing else is hashed: no header, no separator other than each document's own final LF, no category name (the `id` starts with it), no row of `index.json` and no skipped case (why the case and why this form: section 16.17 R62, R63).
* `conformance_run` maps each category that has at least one run case to the count of its run cases. A category with none has no key, and skipped cases are not counted anywhere in the manifest.
* Ids are compared by code point, on the whole id string and not by category and then slug (the two orders differ as soon as one category name is a prefix of another, because `-` and `.` sort below `/`). An id is unique in the family and is printable ASCII (U+0021 to U+007E); a computation that meets any other id, or the same id twice, fails, so a lane's default string comparison is enough. The kit's own checker gains the same rule in S5d: the structural check of `code/scripts/warrant_corpus_conformance.py` requires every case id to be printable ASCII (until then it requires only that the file's bytes are ASCII, which an escaped `\u00e9` in an id passes).
* The runner computes both values from the cases it executed, after all of them passed (Gate [D7], section 11). The writing process itself runs the selected cases (or the runner's self-test entry that the lane's test suite also drives) and hashes those; it does not read a result left behind by an earlier test run. A checker recomputes both from the committed fixture and the manifest's `levels` alone and requires equality (section 11).
* A lane that implements every level the kit's cases require skips nothing, so its digest covers every case of the kit and moves with every kit change. Two lanes with the same `levels` have the same two values for the same kit.

Kit growth at a level outside `L` therefore changes nothing in the manifest; a lane re-pins when it adopts a level, because `L` changes (section 16.17 R65). *Check values*, computed twice independently from the kit as S5b committed it (`cc4823a59d3`): the 461 cases of the four v1 categories alone give `75fc5945e21b3e4773146700bba114f5a7c6d0df9c57932ecb287b8c28a65145`, which cannot move while those four files are not edited (section 11) and is a test vector, not a manifest value; with `levels` = `["ir.core"]` all 1,195 cases are run, `conformance_run` is `{"containment":141,"json":231,"label-digest":13,"tagged":76,"v2-arith":129,"v2-chunk":66,"v2-domain":154,"v2-fault":36,"v2-ir":221,"v2-value":128}` and the digest is `f5dda5b463cb812e7d1458b2f424f4e588f97b12cbb267170a72e0e78b6f39f8` (it moves when S5d adds `v2-binding` cases a lane at `ir.core` runs).

The coverage-labels sidecar `domain-labels.json` of VER04i section 8 is **retired for v2**: its content is in the row, so one file cannot drift from the other.

### 9.2 What each depth may assert

| Depth | `inputs_sha256`/`expected_sha256` of the suite | Report may say |
|---|---|---|
| `full` | equals the corpus suite digests (and, for v1-header suites, the v1 whole-suite digests) | the label of section 9.3 |
| `slim` | equals the corpus `slim_inputs_sha256` and `slim_expected_sha256` over the chosen chunks; the suite digests cannot be recomputed | `slim(n of m chunks)`; never `full`, never `exhaustive` |

### 9.3 Honest labels [D6]

The three domain labels survive per suite and per lane: **`full`** (every binder enumerates its whole type: `bool`, `u8_all`, `fx_full`, `Bytes<0>`, `Bytes<1>`), **`bounded-set`** (a fixed strict subset chosen as the type's edge cases: `u64_edge`, `fx_edge`, `bytes_d1`, `nat_count`, and any composite or list over them) and **`sample`** (a small draw from a huge space: `bytes_n(N>=2)`, which is a few dozen of `256^N` values). **A demoted binder has no label of its own ([D72], section 16.21):** the label of a demoted suite follows its leaves like any other suite's, demotion replaces a leaf by `fx_edge`, and `fx_edge` is `bounded-set`, so a demoted suite is `bounded-set` unless another of its leaves is a `sample`. (This sentence replaces the clause "and every demoted binder", which stood in the `sample` list and contradicted `fx_edge` in the `bounded-set` list.) The VER04i sidecar called `bytes_n(N>=2)` a `bounded-set`; VER04j section 14.4 later named it `sample(SIZE)` in the Rust manifest, and v2 follows the later and stricter word, so the ct-compare `eq_fixed` suites move from `bounded-set` to `sample` when it migrates (the case counts do not change, only the heading they are printed under). A suite with `unreachable_by_type > 0` carries the extra tag `type-limited`. A suite is as weak as its weakest binder. A **slim run, or a run with any case the host type cannot hold, can never be labelled `full`**; the effective strength of a suite is the minimum of its domain label, its depth and its oracle kind (`relational` is a separate tag, not a strength, but a relational suite never counts as an independent reference). Report headlines are computed **per label**, e.g. `full: 65,537; bounded-set: 506,572; slim(16 of 256): 1,048,576`; the word "exhaustive" is never a total (VER04i section 8, LP-012).

## 10. The independent oracle [D3]

### 10.1 Obligation

Today's oracle re-reads the `.warrant` source with its own reader and shares no code with the compiler (VER04i section 2.8). **That independence MUST survive**: an oracle that only re-evaluated the corpus IR would miss every lowering bug, which is the bug class v2 grows the most. The oracle is a **full independent `.warrant` source reader**, shares no code, data file or generated table with the Rust generator, and **gates each adopter**: an adopter's v2 corpus cannot merge until the oracle reads that adopter's spec and its three checks pass.

### 10.2 Shape

A stdlib-only package at **`code/packages/python/warrant-oracle/`** (distribution `coding-adventures-warrant-oracle`, import package `coding_adventures_warrant_oracle`) with five parts, each independently testable: the reader (`lexer`, `parser`, `ast`: source text to AST; fail-closed), `lower` (AST to IR), `ir_eval` (the IR evaluator with the section 3 rule), `domains` and `suites` (descriptor trees, `size`/`nth`, enumeration and chunking), `stream` (the section 2.4 encoders and SHA-256 framing). It is a package and not a directory under `code/scripts/` (the path this section named before the amendment of section 16.1) because the build tool discovers a `BUILD` file only under `code/packages/` and `code/programs/`: under `code/scripts/` its tests, its lint and the gate itself would not run in CI, and an oracle that CI does not run gates nothing. Living under `packages/` gives it no licence to depend on anything: it has no runtime dependency, imports no other package of this repository, and its `BUILD` runs the gate of section 1.2. The old `code/scripts/warrant_corpus_oracle.py` is the v1 oracle and stays until S13.

### 10.3 The three checks

1. **Lowering.** The oracle's own lowering of the spec equals the IR in `corpus.json`, compared as canonical JSON (the v1 canonical writer of VER04i section 9.1). Two independent implementations can only agree byte for byte if lowering is **syntax-directed and unoptimised**: one IR node per source construct, operands in source order, no constant folding, no reassociation, no common-subexpression sharing, no inlining of models, desugarings (`a != b`, `x.0`, refinements into `admit`) exactly as section 4 and Appendix A name them. Any normalisation the generator performs is a spec change here first. A generator that lowers `a - b` as `b - a` is caught here and nowhere else.
2. **Enumeration and evaluation.** The oracle's own enumeration and evaluation reproduce, for every suite and every chunk it verifies: `cases`, `discarded`, `first_admitted`, `calls`, `inputs_sha256`, and for `reference` suites `expected_sha256`, plus the suite digests, `slim_chunks`, `slim_inputs_sha256` and `slim_expected_sha256`, `expected_calls`, `wraps` and `ir_cost` (and the corpus `max_ir_cost` against `corpus.profile`).
3. **Faults and controls.** It re-derives every fault instantiation, `fault_models`, control row and `required_claims`, `blind_claims` and `slim_witness` set from the spec, the op signatures and `corpus.profile`, and compares them with `corpus.json`.

**Status (S4 part 2).** `verify-v2 <corpus-dir> --warrant P --profile P [--bindings P]` implements all three checks over a whole fixture directory (`corpus.json`, `chunks/*.txt`, `cases.tfv`), byte for byte where this document fixes bytes and structurally where section 1.4 is provisional; section 16.7 records what it decided where this document is silent.

### 10.4 Scaling rule and cost

The reader grows with the feature levels (section 4.1): it refuses, with a named error, every construct of a level it does not implement in a suite, op or model; **an unknown construct is never skipped**. The one exception is a *fault row* (and its control) of a level the oracle lacks: check 3 must still re-derive it, so the oracle treats such a row as **data**, instantiating the template and comparing it without evaluating it (and expecting `deferred` witness entries, section 5.6). A naive Python pass over 51 million gf256 cases is, by estimate (not measured), far too slow for PR CI. Two measures are specified:

* **Memoisation.** Pure model calls are memoised on scalar arguments in a bounded table. gf256's models are functions of two `U8` values, so a three-binder claim becomes table lookups. The table is a cache only: a hit and a recomputation MUST agree, and a test samples both.
* **Parallel chunk verification.** A chunk is the unit of work, so verification parallelises across processes and CI jobs with no shared state.

**What is verified when.** "Gates each adopter" [D3] means no chunk digest reaches `main` unverified:

| Trigger | Oracle work |
|---|---|
| a PR that changes a spec, a `corpus.profile`, or any file of a v2 fixture directory | **every chunk of every suite** of that corpus, as a required check (sharded across jobs by chunk if one job is too slow), plus checks 1 and 3 |
| every other PR | checks 1 and 3; an **admission-only pass** over every chunk (no model evaluation, no hashing) that reproduces every `cases` and `first_admitted`; full digests for the `slim` chunks and for every chunk of suites with at most 2^20 product ordinals |
| nightly | every chunk of every suite, again |

The suite digests of section 5.4 are a framing over the chunk digests in the chunk file; the oracle always recomputes that framing, and it is only as verified as the chunk digests beneath it, which is why the first row is not optional. The cost of the first row for gf256 is not measured; if it does not fit CI even when sharded, that is a blocker for S8 to report, not a reason to merge an unverified corpus.

### 10.5 Trust boundary

The oracle reads the spec file from a PR and is therefore a parser of hostile input (section 14). It is also not infallible: a wrong claim is faithfully verified against (VER04i section 11, item 2) and the oracle only shrinks that gap.

## 11. Conformance: `warrant-corpus-conformance-v1` and the v2 cases

**Fixture family.** Every lane runner must pass `warrant-corpus-conformance-v1` (VER04i section 9.1, WD-100; on `main`, with Python, TypeScript and Go runners): the strict JSON reader, canonical writer, `tagged` values and stream, containment and label digest categories. v2 **adds categories to the same fixture family** with the same layout (`index.json`, one JSON file per category, `{"schema": "warrant-corpus-conformance-v1", "category": C, "cases": [...]}`, `* -text`, per-category case counts compared against `index.json`, bytes as lowercase hex). **The four existing category files (`json.json`, `tagged.json`, `containment.json`, `label-digest.json`) are not edited, byte for byte** (acceptance of S5b). `index.json` does change: it gains one row per v2 category, after the four v1 rows in their present order and in the fixed order `v2-value`, `v2-arith`, `v2-ir`, `v2-domain`, `v2-chunk`, `v2-fault`, `v2-binding`, and `total_cases` grows. The three v1 lane tests (Python, TypeScript, Go) close the category set of `index.json`, so an added row would turn each of them red; **S5-1 relaxes them before any v2 row lands [D40]**: a lane requires a row for every category it knows, checks the digest and the case count of **every** row against the file, requires `total_cases` to be the sum of the rows, and accepts a row whose category it does not know only if the name starts with `v2-` (any other unknown row fails). Such a row is digest-checked and not run until the runner reads v2. S5-1's own tests assumed that the committed index holds exactly the four v1 rows; **S5-1b removes that assumption before S5b commits the first `v2-*` row [D59]**. This document **specifies** the cases; the fixture files are created by S5b (six categories) and S5d (`v2-binding`) and extended by S10d, S11 and S12. (Amended by section 16.13: the earlier text said the kit was "possibly not yet on `main`" and that "v1 lanes keep passing" because the existing categories are not edited; the first was stale and the second false.)

**The oracle writes the cases (S5a, S5c; R19).** A hand-written case table in the oracle package states each case's inputs and its **intent** (the expected value or the error code); the oracle derives the outcome independently; the two must agree or the build of the fixture fails naming the case id. `make_conformance.py --check` rebuilds every `v2-*.json` in memory and requires the committed bytes to be identical (exit 0 identical, 1 the bytes differ, 2 a table and derivation disagree or any other error); `--write` regenerates. No fixture JSON is edited by hand. **Regeneration is two steps (section 16.13 R26):** `make_conformance.py --write [--fixture DIR]` writes the `v2-*.json` files (`DIR` defaults to the family directory), then `code/scripts/warrant_corpus_conformance.py --write` rewrites `index.json` from its four derived categories plus one row per `v2-*.json` on disk, in the fixed order above. `make_conformance.py` never writes `index.json`; its `--check` also requires `index.json` to hold exactly one row per file it derives, with that file's `cases` and `sha256`, and skips that one check in a directory that has no `index.json` (S5a's scratch run). The oracle's BUILD runs `--check`, the v1 checker `code/scripts/warrant_corpus_conformance.py`, which indexes the `v2-*` files without importing the oracle or any lane, and that checker's tests `code/scripts/tests/test_warrant_corpus_conformance.py`, so either tool alone catches a stale index (S5b).

**Case envelope [D41].** Every v2 case has, besides its category's fields:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `id` | `<category>/<slug>`, unique across the whole family |
| `summary` | one line, ASCII |
| `requires` | the sorted list of the levels a runner must implement **to produce `expect`** (section 16.13 R24). For a case whose `expect` is not `level-missing` or `unknown-tag` it is computed like a suite row's (section 16.10 R9) over what the case's checked expression reaches [D64]: the tree, the types of its `env` and of its literals, and the bodies and signatures of the models and ops in its call closure (section 16.18 R69). A model, an op or a `types` row that the expression does not reach adds nothing. For a case whose `expect` is `level-missing` or `unknown-tag` it is the case's `levels` field: the level of the offending tag is deliberately not included, because the runner that lacks it is the one the case tests. Every S5 case has `["ir.core"]`; a table case whose `requires` so defined is anything else fails the S5 build |
| `expect` | the outcome object of the category |

**A lane runs a case if and only if its `requires` is a subset of the levels the runner implements**, and skips it otherwise. A corpus is handled by the same per-unit rule [D64]: a lane decides `level-missing` suite by suite, and control by control, from what the checked expression reaches, records what it cannot run `not-live` and runs the rest. It never refuses a whole corpus for a level (sections 4.1 and 9.1). Per category, cases run plus cases skipped MUST equal the index row's `cases`, so a case is never skipped silently and a kit that grows by a level does not break a runner that lacks it. The cases a lane runs by this rule, with every case of the four v1 categories, are exactly the cases its manifest pins [D61] (section 9.1). An error outcome is `{"error": E}` with `E` from **the closed set of its category** (`v2-value` and `v2-binding` add `"outcome":"reject"`); a lane maps its own failure to that code in its test harness, and a code outside the set is a kit error.

**New categories.**

| Category | Case fields | The lane runs | Expected | Closed error set |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `v2-value` | `type` (section 2.1 text), `types` (the section 2.2 table), `input_hex` (the JSON value's UTF-8 bytes) | the section 2.3 decoder then the section 2.4 encoder | `{"outcome":"accept","stream_hex":X,"stream_sha256":D}` (the section 2.4 bytes of the one value, with no header or context prefix, and their SHA-256; `stream_hex` omitted when longer than 2,048 characters) or `{"outcome":"reject","error":E}` | `value`, `malformed-json`, `type` |
| `v2-arith` | `type` (the type of **both** operands; a mixed `Nat`/`Int` comparison is a `v2-ir` case), `op` (`add sub mul neg` and the six comparisons), `lhs`, `rhs` (decimal strings of at most 20 digits, `Int` included [D58]; no `rhs` for `neg`), optional `document` (`"v0"`, [D45]; a lane ignores it, and the expected value is the same with or without it) | the one evaluator node over `lit` operands | `{"type":T,"value":S}` (`T` the result type: `Int` for every `add`, `sub`, `mul` and `neg` on `Nat` or `Int`, section 16.15 R42; `Bool` with a JSON boolean for a comparison) or `{"error":E}` | `ir-overflow` |
| `v2-ir` | `levels`, `ir`, `env`, `types`, `models`, `model_order`, `ops`, `subjects`, optional `max_ir_cost`, optional `role` (below) | the IR evaluator | `{"type":T,"value":V,"calls":N}` or `{"error":E}`; `ir-overflow` carries `"class":"corpus"` or `"class":"case"` (the split of section 3.2) | `unknown-tag`, `level-missing`, `ir-overflow`, `impure-guard`, `ir-cost`, `ill-typed`, `model-order`, `subject-forbidden`, `depth` |
| `v2-domain` | `descriptor` (section 5.1 JSON), `type` (the binder type, needed to encode the values), and `index` (one JSON integer) or `range` (the JSON array `[lo, hi]`, read half-open: indices `lo..hi-1`). A later level may add an optional `types` field; absent means `{}` (section 16.15 R44) | `size()` and `nth()` | `{"size":S,"values_hex":[..]}` (`S` a JSON integer; each value's section 2.4 bytes, with no header or context prefix, in index order) or `{"error":E}` | `descriptor`, `index` |
| `v2-chunk` | `corpus`, `suite`, `chunk_file_hex`, `chunk`, optional `also_sequential` (below) | validate the chunk file, then enumerate the one chunk by unranking and stream it | `{"cases","first_admitted","calls","inputs_sha256","expected_sha256"}` (no `expected_sha256` for a predicate suite) or `{"error":E}` | `chunk-file`, `slim-chunks`, `corpus` |
| `v2-fault` | `fault` (one row of section 8.1), `op` (the section 6 row of the op that `fault.op` names; the row itself carries no name), `types` (read whole and first, [D74]), `levels`, `args` (section 2.3 values in parameter order), `result` (the value the call returned).
