* **R24. `requires` of a refusal case.** [D41] adopted `requires` "computed like a suite row's". Read literally, R9 gives a tree that holds a `bit_xor` tag the levels `ir.core` and `ir.fixed`, so the mandatory vector "a tag of an undeclared level" and the `level-missing` case of `v2-fault` could not be `["ir.core"]`, and a core-only runner would skip the very cases that test it. Section 11 therefore defines `requires` as the levels a runner must implement to produce `expect`: R9 for every other case, the case's `levels` for a `level-missing` or `unknown-tag` case. It follows that a runner must tell the two codes apart for a level it does not implement, so it knows the tag names of every defined level; S5b's `level-missing` vectors use `ir.fixed` and `ir.tuple` tags only. **Amended by [D64] (section 16.18 R69):** `requires` and `level-missing` are decided from what the checked expression reaches, types included, and S5e adds `level-missing` vectors that a type causes.
* **R25. What the codes mean.** [D41] closed each category's error set by name. Section 11 now defines each code and the order in which two of them would be reported; the codes are the kit's own, the oracle maps its codes to them in the derivation module, and every case triggers exactly one error. `depth` covers both limits of section 14 (expression nesting and active `model_call` frames). A definition that S5a finds wrong is an erratum to that table before S5b merges (R20).
* **R26. Regeneration, locations and the v1 checker.** Not decided by the owner and needed to build S5a and S5b: regeneration is two steps and only the v1 script writes `index.json` (the oracle's `--check` verifies its rows); the module and table locations and the slug form are those of the S5a row; `v2-arith` has one `type` for both operands and a lane ignores `document`; `v2-fault` reports every argument after a `pre` rewrite; a stream in `v2-value` and `v2-domain` is the bare section 2.4 value. The v1 script rejects any `expect.reason` outside the v1 kit's closed set, which `v2-binding` would trip, so S5d scopes that check to the v1 categories. S5b wires the script's own tests into the oracle's BUILD as well as the script.


### 16.14 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (S11, ir.data)

Slice S11-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records the owner's decisions on the S11 plan (`ir.data`, with the adopters logic-gates and arithmetic). Planning S11 with the tools as merged (`warrant-corpus-gen` 0.6.0, `warrant-oracle` 0.5.0) found that the level had types, values, stream tags, TFV forms, descriptors and a cost rule but **no IR**: no Appendix A.2, no JSON for `field-zero`, no `missing-error` on any payload but `Bytes` and a tuple, and three fault names in the logic-gates bindings (`saturate-add`, `saturate-sub`, `shift-mask`: 21 of its 51 fault lines) that the registry does not hold, so no profile was acceptable to both tools (section 8.4), the position `off-by-one-consumed` was in before [D36]. The normative sections (2.2, 4.1, 5.3, 8.2, 13, 16.2, Appendix A.1 and the new Appendix A.2) and [D23], R1, R3, R8 and R9 of section 16.10 were amended in the same change; each new rule names the slice that implements it. **Owner decisions** are [D46] to [D57]; the author's readings where the decisions are silent are **R27** to **R39** and are erratum candidates, not rulings. *(This section was written beside the errata of sections 16.12 and 16.13 and merged after them, so its numbers continue theirs.)*

**[D46] Lists leave `ir.data` for a new reserved level, `ir.list` (option B of the plan's Q1).** `ir.data` is records, variants (with and without payload), `Option` as a real type, the constructors, field access and `match` on any scrutinee. The list type and values, the list literal, `len`/`concat`/`take`/`drop`/`at` on a list, the `list` descriptor and section 16.2 items 1 and 2 move to `ir.list`, which is **reserved**: the name is closed, nothing of it is implemented, and both tools refuse a document that needs it (section 4.1). Why: no adopter with `evidence.bindings` has a list (logic-gates needs `Option` only; arithmetic needs records, one payload-free variant and one `match` on it), and a level is a closed set a lane must implement whole, so keeping lists in `ir.data` would have left eighteen lanes owing list semantics that no corpus exercises. The rejected options were implementing lists now (it needs the byte-string order of section 16.2 settled and list vectors, with no adopter to check the result) and keeping them in `ir.data` on paper while both tools refuse them (a level whose content no tool implements). The precedent is [D20], which carved `ir.tuple` out for the same reason. Section 4.1; implemented as a refusal in S11a (oracle) and S11b (generator).

**[D47] The Appendix A.2 shapes, as proposed (the plan's Q3).** `["some", e]`, a **typed** `["none", "T"]`, `["ctor", "Name", [e1, .., en]]`, `["field", e, i]`, and `match` on any scrutinee. (The data patterns `["some", p]`, `["none"]`, `["ctor", "Name", [p..]]` and `["bool", v]` are the plan's G4, an author proposal the owner was not asked about: R30.) A distinct `field` tag instead of a reused `project`, so that the tag-to-level table stays static and `match` stays the only type-directed tag; the type argument on `none` because a consumer that types IR bottom-up has no other source for it (the [D24] convention of a static type argument in position 1). **Provisional until S11a's committed lowering dump reads them**, the R2 precedent: if the dump differs, the appendix and the `field-zero` block of section 8.2 change in one erratum before S11b. Implemented in S11a and S11b (lowering, typing, `requires`, cost) and S11c-1 and S11c-2 (evaluation, values).

**[D48] `Option` is a real type at `ir.data`, and the direct producer-to-`unwrap_or` use keeps adding no `ir.data` (the plan's Q4; amends [D23]).** Why the second half: the alternative, every `Option`-typed node is `ir.data`, would add `ir.data` to the `requires` of every suite that uses `unwrap_or` and so move lzw, wasm-leb128, x25519 and the fault rows of ct-compare, forcing a regeneration and a version bump for no gain in what a lane must implement. R29 states the invariant exactly. Implemented in S11a and S11b.

**[D49] The data faults of S11 are `field-zero` only; `wrong-ctor` waits for its first adopter (option A of the plan's Q5).** Section 8.2 has `field-zero`'s exact form (R32 has the readings behind it). Why: arithmetic binds `field-zero` and nobody binds `wrong-ctor`, whose replacement constructor needs a default value for each field, a second place a v2 form would differ from the harness ([D37]) and better settled against a real binding. Until then `wrong-ctor` is not in the registry and a profile that names it is refused (code `fault`), nothing skipped. The rejected option was specifying both now.

**[D50] `missing-error` is generalised to every payload by one rule (option A of the plan's Q6).** The first parameter of exactly the type, else the type's zero, `none` for an `Option`; tuples and records are built from their components (section 8.2; R31). It is deliberately **not** value-identical to the Rust harness, which returns the first element of the payload's domain (`Ok(0)` for `Result<U8,E>` against `Ok(a)` here); both turn every `Err` into an `Ok` and leave every `Ok` alone, so the same claims catch both, and the difference is recorded as [D37] recorded it for tuples. The rejected option was matching the harness, which leaves the registry with two rules, since the `Bytes` and tuple forms keep theirs. Implemented in S11f-1/S11f-2 (scalar and `Bytes` payloads) and S11c-1/S11c-2 (record and `Option` payloads).

**[D51] `saturate-add`, `saturate-sub` and `shift-mask` join the v2 fault registry (option A of the plan's Q7).** Section 8.2 has their exact, normative forms, each equal to the harness rule on every input. Why: the [D36] reasoning. The bindings stay the single source of fault names; the rejected options were removing the faults from the logic-gates bindings (which deletes merged Rust negative controls, four per fault line, to fit a new tool) and a profile line that declares a bindings fault not portable (a new corpus key, and weaker). **Sequence** (section 13): this erratum; the oracle (S11f-1: registry, templates, a parity dump in a **new** fixture directory); the generator (S11f-2: byte parity with that dump); then the logic-gates adopter with all 51 bindings faults. S11f-1 and S11f-2 also carry the scalar-payload `missing-error` of [D50], and are independent of S11a to S11c.

**[D52] No atomic flip and no version bump (option A of the plan's Q8).** Each tool opens its `ir.data` fault-evaluation gate in its own evaluator PR (S11c-1, then S11c-2), and neither `warrant-corpus-gen` (0.6.0) nor `warrant-oracle` (0.5.0) is bumped by any S11 slice. This is sound because no committed fixture holds an `ir.data` suite or a `{"deferred":"ir.data"}` witness ([D27]: none exists after S10) and the generator refuses any document with a data type whole until S11c-2, so no emitted byte of an existing fixture depends on the gate; and no committed fixture names the three faults of [D51] or has a `missing-error` on a scalar payload. **The consequence, accepted:** the first data fixtures record `warrant-corpus-gen@0.6.0`, and their `--expect-generator` pins say the same, although a 0.6.0 build from before S11c-2 refuses the documents; the recorded version identifies the crate release line, not the capability, as it already does for the wasm-leb128 fixture and `off-by-one-consumed` ([D36]). The rejected option was one atomic PR after S11c-2 and S11f-2 that bumps both tools and regenerates the seven committed corpora and every `--expect-generator` pin. R37 has the mechanics.

**[D53] CV2-F10 is closed by S11a and S11b, with no separate oracle PR (option B of the plan's Q2).** The difference CV2-F10 names (the oracle counts record and variant types in `requires` and lowers, the generator refuses them at `ir.data`, section 16.10 R13(d)) ends when both tools lower them. **Whatever stays unimplemented after S11 is refused, not counted:** from S11a the oracle refuses a document that needs `ir.list` (today it enumerates a `List` binder, counts `ir.data` and evaluates the suite, which is the behaviour CV2-F10 questions), as the generator does.

**[D54] The `demoted` path text for composites is the oracle's (the plan's Q10).** `.k` for a tuple component or a record field, `.Ctor.k` for a variant field, `.some`, `.ok` and `.elem`, after the binder index. Section 5.3 states it; it extends section 16.10 R14(b), which gave `.k` and `.ok`. Implemented in S11c-2 (the generator; the oracle already writes it).

**[D55] A pattern that binds an error value stays refused and is outside every level (the plan's Q11).** `Err(e) -> Err(e)` needs an error value as an IR value, which no level has. Section 4.1 says so. Two hash-functions claims stay blocked on it; hash-functions is not an S11 adopter. R28 gives the name the refusal carries.

**[D56] No VER29 `assume` rows for arithmetic yet (the plan's Q12).** Both of arithmetic's `assume` blocks are `none`, both tools refuse any other row because the shape of `assumes` is open (section 16.4 item 4), and the VER29 dependency reader is Rust-only (section 12). The arithmetic adopter therefore emits `assumes: []` and is **not** "the first VER29 lane link": that sentence of section 12 and the old S11 row of section 13 are superseded, and both are amended here. The `assumes` shape gets its own erratum when a spec first has a contract row.

**[D57] Unproven controls are accepted and recorded; CV2-F12 is reworded and sequenced (option A of the plan's Q13).** Nearly every claim of both adopters passes computed arguments (`b2u(a)`, `bv8(a)`), and the stand-in rule needs plain binders (section 16.6 item 5), so nearly every control of both will be `unproven`. That is allowed (section 16.6 item 7: blind plus unproven passes) and is what x25519 has; each adopter's fixture README records the counts of witnessed, blind and unproven candidates per control, and (author addition, R38) a control whose **every** candidate is blind is reported in the PR, never silenced by an `accept_blind` line added without saying so ([D17]). **CV2-F12 is reworded** from literal arguments to **pure computed arguments** (R36 has the text for the backlog). **Sequencing constraint:** CV2-F12 changes witness rows, so it moves every faults dump and every fixture that has an `unproven` candidate; it lands **wholly before S11c-1 or wholly after the arithmetic adopter**, and (author addition, R38) never between an oracle PR and its generator mirror (the S11f pair included).

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R27. Where `ir.list` sits, and what refuses it.** `ir.list` **implies `ir.data`** (and so `ir.tuple`) and does not imply `ir.fixed`: a list `at` returns an `Option`, a list's elements may be records, variants or options, and nothing in it needs a fixed-width built-in. `ir.seq`'s relation to it is left to the amendment that defines `ir.seq`. Its content is listed in section 4.1; the list rows of sections 2.1, 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 5.1 and 7.3 stay as written and are that reserved content, not rules any tool implements. **Refusal:** a document in which a list type appears anywhere (a signature, a binder, a `types` field, a model), or that uses a list literal, a list operand of `len concat take drop at`, or one of the built-ins of R28, is refused whole by both tools (generator code `level`, oracle code `level`), with the level name **`ir.list`**. *(Section 16.16 R57:)* the list operand of `len concat take drop at` is that refusal and **not a typing error**, however the operand got its list type; so is a call to an op whose signature is refused for `ir.list`, wherever the op is declared (the call is not an unknown name); and an IR tree that holds the tag `list` is refused by an IR reader as the level `ir.list`, not as an unknown tag. Nothing of it is counted in a `requires`, so the string `ir.list` never appears in a corpus. The descriptor kind `list` and the value forms stay known to both tools' readers only as far as they already are; a consumer that meets them in a corpus rejects the corpus.
* **R28. The five source built-ins no level names, and the two feature names.** Both lowerers refuse `fixed slice to_list to_bytes replicate` under the name `ir.data`, and section 4.1 listed none of them. `slice`, `to_list`, `to_bytes` and `replicate` (the WX-D1 sequence built-ins of VER04k) are **assigned to `ir.list`**, by name and whatever their operand (`slice` of a `Bytes` too), so the tag-to-level table stays static; they are refused with `ir.list`. `fixed<N>(b)` (a `Bytes` to `Option<Bytes<N>>`) is **outside every level**: it has no tag in Appendix A.1 or A.2, adding one is a decision for its first adopter (the 35 names of `ir.fixed` are closed, [D24]), and it is refused with the feature name **`fixed-cast`**. The error-value use of [D55] is refused with the feature name **`err-value`**. Feature names stand in the level position of the refusal exactly as `inout`, `ensures`, `vectors` and `assume` do (section 13, the fail-closed rule). `range` is named `ir.seq`, which is where section 4.1 has always put it (both tools said `ir.data`). After S11b the name `ir.data` is therefore never a **lowering** refusal of either tool (evaluation and `generate` keep it until S11c, R37); S11a and S11b change the names, and the censuses of section 16.10 R13(d) are re-pinned with them (hash-functions' 21 `ir.data` claims divide into `ok`, `err-value` and whatever else the new names show). **All of this item is a reading and an erratum candidate, not an owner decision:** the owner decided that the error-value bind stays refused and is outside every level ([D55]) and that lists are `ir.list` ([D46]); the names `err-value` and `fixed-cast`, the name `ir.seq` for `range`, and the assignment of `slice to_list to_bytes replicate` to `ir.list` are the author's. Renaming one is an erratum to this item before S11a commits its refusals dump, and an erratum with a regenerated dump and census after.
* **R29. The exact invariant of [D48].** (a) `Option<T>` may appear wherever a type may: an op or model signature, a binder, a `types` field, an example value, a stream, and as the static type of any IR node. (b) The **intermediate use** is a node of one of the nine producers (`try checked_add checked_sub checked_mul div rem div_trunc rem_trunc at`) that **is the first operand of an `unwrap_or`**, directly. (c) The tags `unwrap_or` and the nine producers are `ir.fixed` and bring `ir.fixed` only. (d) The type rule of R9 counts `ir.data` for every `Option` type **except the static type of a producer node in the intermediate use**. So `unwrap_or(at(b, i), 0u8)` requires `ir.fixed` and no more, exactly as before; `unwrap_or(o, d)` with `o` a binder of type `Option<U8>` requires `ir.fixed` and `ir.data`; `checked_add(a, b) == Some(x)`, `Ok(rem(a, b))` and `unwrap_or(if c then at(b, i) else at(b, 0), d)` require both, because the producer is not the first operand of an `unwrap_or` or the first operand is not a producer. *(The last example read `.. else None, d)` until section 16.16 R52: the first operand of `unwrap_or` has no expected type, so a bare `None` there is a typing error.)* (e) R8's consumer rule is thereby **relaxed, not removed**: `unwrap_or` accepts any `Option`-typed first operand, and an `Option` may be an operand of `eq`/`ne`, a branch of `if`, an argument, a payload and a scrutinee. (f) **No committed byte moves:** before this erratum every `Option`-typed node of every lowered unit was a producer in the intermediate use (anything else was refused, [D23], R8), so every existing `requires` and `levels` is computed to the same value by the amended rule; S11a's acceptance is that the seven committed corpora verify unchanged, and its fixture has a control row of `unwrap_or(producer)` claims that pins it. (g) `Option<Int>` and `Option<Nat>` of an exact type remain IR-internal like `Int` (section 2.1): they may be the type of a node and of a `none`, never of a signature, binder or value.
* **R30. Appendix A.2 where [D47] is silent.** The four data patterns (`some`, `none`, `ctor`, `bool`) are the plan's G4 and are a reading, not part of the decision; `field` applies to a record only (a variant's fields are reached through `match`, VER04j section 4.3); `ctor`'s name is a record name or a constructor name, which one namespace makes unambiguous; `none` carries the canonical type name of its payload, `Int` allowed as for `lit`; the `bool` pattern exists because VER04j has `true`/`false` patterns and no other literal pattern; a `match` adds `ir.data` whenever its scrutinee is not a `Result` **or any of its patterns is a data pattern**, which matters only for `bool` (the other three can match only a value whose type already brings `ir.data`); the corpus errors are those of Appendix A.1 over the larger pattern set, plus an unknown or wrongly typed constructor, a wrong field count and a `field` index out of range; equality is structural, ordering is undefined; every tag costs 1 plus its operands. All of it is the plan's section 1.4 or the author's reading of VER04j sections 4.2 to 4.5, and all of it is provisional with [D47].
* **R31. `missing-error`, where [D50] is silent.** (a) *Tuples and records are built, leaves are looked up.* The plan took a whole tuple or record from a parameter of exactly its type first; R3 as written and as implemented always builds the tuple, and a record is treated the same way, so no S10t dump moves and [D37]'s "no tool and no fixture changes" still holds. (b) *The top-level `Bytes` rule keeps its looser match* (any `Bytes<M>` parameter), read from both tools (`faults.py::_missing_error`, `faults.rs`), because tightening it to "exactly" could move a committed row; everywhere else the match is exact, as R3 already was for components. (c) *New acceptances:* an op with a `Bytes` payload and no `Bytes` parameter, and every scalar payload, were refused and now have a template; each tool's own unit tests that pinned those refusals change in S11f, and no committed parity dump pins one (checked: no `*refusals*` dump of the oracle names `missing-error`). **Corrected by section 16.20 R95:** that was true when written; S11f-1 then committed two such rows in `scalar-faults-v1`, and S11c-1 removes the one that [D50] makes false [D71]. (d) *A variant leaf with no parameter of its type, and anything with a list, has no template.* (e) *Template levels* follow the tuple form's precedent: the highest levels written, without `ir.core`.
* **R32. `field-zero`, where [D49] is silent.** Records only; the first `Nat` or fixed-width field in declaration order; the `Ok` form of the existing blocks; no template for a record under `Some`, for a variant (the harness would zero a field of whichever constructor the value has) or for a record with no numeric field. The equality with the harness is argued in section 8.2 and is on values, so there is no R3-style difference to record. `field-flip` and `field-flip-last` (in the shared generator since VOS-039c) are not in the v2 registry and no S11 adopter binds them.
* **R33. The forms of `saturate-add`, `saturate-sub` and `shift-mask`, where [D51] is silent.** (a) *Read from the harness, not from the plan:* `V1_FAULTS` and `scalar_adapter_v1` in `rust/warrant-grammar/src/evidence.rs` and the generated `rust/logic-gates/tests/generated/warrant_evidence.rs` (`op_and_gate`, `op_mux2`, `op_not_gate`, `op_nand_not`). Two facts there shaped the forms: under a `Replace` fault the export is **not called**, so the saturating forms return `Ok` unconditionally and are kind `replace`; and `{w}` is the width of the **first** argument's type while the rewritten argument is the **last**. (b) *Signed forms are specified although no adopter binds them* (logic-gates is all `U8`): the registry instantiates by signature, the signed rule is two more table rows, and it is exact (`rem_trunc` for Rust's signed `%`; the bound chosen by the sign of `p1`), the [D36] precedent of R15(d). (c) *`Nat` parameters have no template:* the harness computes them as `u32`, a host type the corpus cannot see, and a `Nat` rewrite built from `rem` would be typed `Int`. (d) *The host-type condition.* The harness computes on the binding's host argument types; they are the Warrant types unless the binding has `args=`, which none of the twelve logic-gates ops that bind these faults has. Where a binding does give another integer type for the parameters a fault reads, the v2 form (on the Warrant type) and the harness (on the host type) can differ; the adopter PR that first has such a binding must say so, as R6 asked for the empty-result forms. (e) *`min_value` rather than a `0` literal* for the unsigned `saturate-sub` bound, so the four rows differ only in the tag and the bound. (f) *The template level is `["ir.fixed"]`*, declared by the template (the union over its tags would list `ir.core` too), with the op's type levels added by R9, and the rows are live. (g) **Checked against `warrant-oracle` 0.5.0 before this erratum was written** (its typed IR compiler, `Program.compile`, which already implements `ir.fixed`): the section 8.2 JSON compiles there with the types `U8`/`I8` bare and `Result<U8,?>`/`Result<I8,?>` in the `Ok` form and the costs 5, 9, 6 and 10; evaluated on **all 65,536 pairs** of `U8` and all 65,536 pairs of `I8` it equals the saturating sum and difference clamped to the type; the four `shift-mask` forms tried (`U8` by 8, `U8` by 32, `I8` by 16, `I8` by 64) compile with the type of the parameter and cost 5 and equal Rust's `p % w` on all 256 values each; and the scalar `missing-error` forms `Ok(a)` and `Ok(0u8)` compile with the type `Result<U8,?>` and cost 5, turning an `Err` into the `Ok` and returning an `Ok` unchanged. The oracle's existing template gave the `demux` and `priority_encoder` rows of the section 8.2 table and refused `and_gate`, as stated. **Not checked, because 0.5.0 does not lower `ir.data`:** every form with `ctor`, `field` or `none`.
* **R34. Variant limits: the corpus limits stay (VER04r 64 and 8 against VER04j 256 and 32).** VER04j section 4.2 allows a variant 256 constructors and a record or constructor 32 fields; section 2.2 rule 3 allows 64 constructors and 8 fields per constructor (32 per record). The stricter numbers are kept as **corpus limits**: every lane binds every constructor by name (section 7.3) and enumerates every constructor's field product, 64 is the limit an `error` type already has, 8 is the tuple arity, and no adopter is near either (arithmetic's variant has six payload-free constructors). A document inside the language limits and over the corpus limits is refused whole (generator code `limit`, oracle code `limit`), checked on the declarations before any expression is lowered; raising a limit is an erratum to section 2.2 when an adopter needs it. *(Section 16.16 R56:)* such a document exists for the two variant limits only; the record limit of 32 is the language's own, and a record of 33 fields or a variant of one constructor is a parse error (section 2.2).
* **R35. Refusal order for parity (extends R12).** So that both tools name the same refusal for a document that has several: (1) the declaration limits of section 2.2 (`limit`) come first, in declaration order; (2) within one unit (a type declaration, an op signature, a model, an op refinement, a claim, an example), a level or feature refusal (`ir.list`, `ir.seq`, `err-value`, `fixed-cast`) is named in preference to a type error of the same unit, and of several the first in source order is named (pre-order, left to right). A corpus error is not displaced: between a level or feature refusal and a corpus error, the one met first in lowering order is named (pre-order; a `match`'s patterns before its arm bodies) (section 16.16 R54); (3) within one `match`, every pattern is checked before any arm body, in arm order, and within a pattern outside in: an unknown constructor, a constructor or literal that does not fit the scrutinee's type, a wrong field count, then the duplicate-bind and shadowing rules; (4) among the whole-`match` corpus errors, unreachable arms (in arm order) before fall-through, as R12 says, now over every scrutinee type; (5) units are processed as: type declarations, operation signatures, models, operation refinements, then claims and examples, each in declaration order. The first error that is not a level or feature refusal, in that order, is named, unless a unit already processed and declared on an earlier line holds a level or feature refusal, which is named instead; with no such error, the refusal of the unit declared on the earliest line is named (section 16.16 R53, which replaces "units are reported in declaration order"). A type declaration and an op signature are units too, so a list type in a `record` or `variant` declaration or in a signature is named at that declaration. The difference section 16.10 R13(a) records (the generator's checker can name an earlier arm-body type error where the oracle names a pattern error) is not closed by this and stays a difference in which error is named, never in whether the document is refused. S11a's committed refusals dump pins the order and S11b reproduces it.
* **R36. Names, the backlog, and text this erratum does not edit.** The slices are S11-0, S11-0b, S11a, S11b, S11c-1, S11c-2, S11f-1 and S11f-2 ("f" for the faults that are not `ir.data`), then the two adopters, then S11d, S11e and S11g behind S5 to S7. The VER-BACKLOG rows are a **separate docs PR** (S11-0b), as for S10-0: it adds the S11 progress row, closes CV2-F10 by [D53] once S11b merges, notes that S11a also closes CV2-F8 if the oracle then refuses a `bind` named like a constructor, and rewords CV2-F12 to: *"Extend the stand-in rule (VER04r 16.6 item 5) to pure computed arguments, literals included: for a `post` or `replace` fault the stand-in needs only the argument values, which are pure when no argument holds a subject call, so a `reference` claim whose subject side is `op(e1..ek)` with every `ei` pure has a stand-in; a `pre` fault keeps the plain-binder rule, because it must re-evaluate `expected` at one rewritten binder. Lands wholly before S11c-1 or wholly after the arithmetic adopter ([D57])."* The S5, S6, S7, S10d, S10e and S10f rows of section 13 are the S5-0 erratum's (section 16.13) and are not edited. After that erratum merged, this one placed its cross-references in the text S5-0 owns, each a minimal edit: the status line (S11 is specified, not implemented); section 11's disposition table (the `ir.data` vectors are S11d's, lists wait for `ir.list`) and its `v2-ir` sentence on the closed tag sets (Appendix A.2 joins A and A.1, so an `unknown-tag` vector may not use `some`, `none`, `ctor`, `field`, `list` or an `ir.seq` name; S5a's tables must respect this before S5b commits bytes); section 12 (arithmetic is not the first VER29 lane link, [D56]); "Order and size"; and R20 (the reserved `ir.list` is a level name the S5c validator knows). The cost table of section 4.2 rule 6 still labels `match` "(`ir.data`)"; since [D35] a `match` is `ir.tuple` or `ir.data` by its scrutinee and the cost is the same for both.
* **R37. What "no flip" means in each PR.** S11a and S11b lower, type and cost `ir.data` and keep `generate` and evaluation fail-closed (`level ir.data` at evaluation, the S10a/S10b pattern), which is the one place the name `ir.data` is still a refusal, until S11c. S11c-1 opens the oracle's fault gate (`IMPLEMENTED` gains `ir.data`) and from then the oracle **rejects** a `{"deferred":"ir.data"}` entry; S11c-2 opens the generator's. Between the two no corpus can hold such an entry, because the generator still refuses the document. The generator's `KNOWN_FAULTS` goes from 14 to 17 in S11f-2 (the three names of [D51]) and gains `field-zero` in S11c-2, 18 when both have landed, in whichever order; the oracle's `REGISTRY` the same in S11f-1 and S11c-1. Every S11 PR runs the four committed fixtures' `--check` lines and the oracle's `verify-v2` lines unchanged, and a moved byte is a blocker, not a regeneration.
* **R38. Two sentences of [D57] that are the author's.** The owner's answer was: accept and record the unproven controls, reword CV2-F12, and land it wholly before S11c-1 or wholly after the arithmetic adopter. Two things written in that decision go further and are readings: that a control whose every candidate is blind is reported in the adopter PR and not silenced by an unannounced `accept_blind` line (the existing rule of [D17], restated), and that CV2-F12 never lands between an oracle PR and its generator mirror, the S11f pair included (the plan's advice; a faults dump moved between the two halves would turn the mirror red). Section 13 carries the second in the S11c-1, S11f-1 and S11f-2 rows.
* **R39. Typing points Appendix A.2 needed for S11a (found in review).** (a) `["some", e]` is typed exactly `Option<type(e)>`; **corrected by [D60], section 16.16 R51:** `Option` is *not* a composite for VER04j section 4.4's no-lifting rule, an `Option<S>` flows to `Option<T>` when `S` flows to `T` and two `Option` types unify by their payloads, as `Result` does, in every position and not only in `ctor` arguments (this item said `Option` does not lift, which is stricter than the checker it names). (b) A source `None` with no type from its context is a typing error, because the typed `["none", "T"]` has nothing to write; "its context" is the **expected type** of its position and never a sibling branch or arm (section 16.16 R52). (c) `e.i` on a non-tuple and `e.name` on a non-record are typing errors from S11a (they were the level `ir.data`), which decides the `tuple-forms-v1` row `a-projection-of-a-non-tuple`. (d) Type declarations and op signatures are units for the refusal order (R35). All four are the author's reading of VER04j sections 4.3 and 4.4 and of `checker.rs`, and provisional with [D47]: S11a reads the checker before it writes the dump.

**Items of earlier sections settled or moved by this section.** Section 16.2 items 1 and 2 (lists of byte strings) are **not** settled: they move with lists to `ir.list` ([D46]) and stay refused. Section 16.10 R13(d)'s arithmetic difference and the arithmetic part of CV2-F5 close with S11b; CV2-F10 closes with S11a/S11b ([D53]). Still open and unchanged: `assumes` (16.4 item 4, [D56]), `wrong-ctor` ([D49]), `fixed<N>` and the error-value bind (R28).

**Findings in the S11 plan that contradict the decisions or each other, resolved here.** (1) The plan named this section 16.12 with [D39] and R17 onward; those numbers were taken while it was being answered, hence section 16.14, [D46] and R27 onward. (2) The plan's Appendix A.2 table had a `list` tag and list forms of `len concat take drop at`; dropped by [D46]. (3) The plan's `build(X)` took a whole tuple from a parameter, which R3 never did; R31(a). (4) The plan left a signed `shift-mask` without a template; it is exact with `rem_trunc` and is specified (R33(b)). (5) The plan's "no committed byte depends on the gate" is right for corpora and chunk files, and one committed **parity dump** row does name the level: `a-projection-of-a-non-tuple` in the oracle's `tuple-forms-v1` refusals dump (`t.0` on a `Bool`, refused today with `level ir.data`). From S11a that projection is a typing error (code `type`, Appendix A.2), so S11a regenerates that row; the generator's `tuple_parity.rs` reads only the row's code class (not `corpus`, so not `ir`), so it stays green across the pair, but S11a's PR must run it. (6) The plan counted the generator's fault names "14 to 17 (18 with `field-zero`)", which fixes an order between S11f and S11c that nothing requires; R37. (7) The plan kept the name `ir.data` for what stays refused after S11; a tool that implements a level cannot refuse under its name, hence `ir.list` and the two feature names (R27, R28).

### 16.15 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (S5a review)

Slice S5a-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what the review of S5a (the oracle's derivation of the six `ir.core` categories, section 13) needed from this document. The implementer left ten questions where sections 2, 3, 11 and Appendix A did not decide a case's outcome and kept every such case out of the tables. Two needed the owner: [D58] and [D59]. The others are the author's readings R40 to R50 (erratum candidates, not rulings), each checked against the generator (`warrant-corpus-gen` 0.6.0) and the oracle as merged unless it says otherwise. This is the erratum that R20 and R25 of section 16.13 require **before S5b merges**. Sections 2.2, 3.2, 11, 13, 14, [D40] and Appendix A were amended in the same change; sections 2.3 and 5 are unchanged. Each rule names the slice that implements it.

**[D58] Decimal text is at most 20 digits for every `lit`, `Int` included.** Section 2.3 and section 14 stand unchanged: the digit limit of the "every integer type, `Nat`" row applies to the `value` of a `lit` node of type `Int` as well (the sign is not a digit), so an `Int` literal is in `-(10^20-1)..=10^20-1`. Why: it is what the generator can emit (a source literal is a `u64`; a larger exact value exists only as the result of a node), and one rule for all decimal text means a lane needs no second reader. Consequences: **S5a** drops its nine cases with a longer `Int` literal (seven in `v2-arith`, two in `v2-ir`) and tests the edge of the window of section 3.2 **by arithmetic in `v2-ir`**, from literals of at most 20 digits (R48: an `expect` value is not limited). The oracle accepts up to 39 digits for an `Int` literal today (`decode_int_text`, `ir_eval.py`), which is a divergence from this section: backlog item **CV2-F17** tightens the oracle to refuse an `Int` literal of more than 20 digits (oracle only, with a test at 20 and 21 digits; no emitted byte moves, since the generator cannot write such a literal). CV2-F17 does not block S5a or S5b, because after S5a no kit case depends on the difference. Rejected: widening section 2.3 to 39 digits for `Int` (a second digit limit in eighteen readers, for text no generator writes).

**[D59] The exception of [D40] is used once more: one test-only PR, S5-1b, fixes the three lanes' S5-1 tests.** The conformance-index tests that S5-1 added to `python/warrant-corpus`, `typescript/warrant-corpus` and `go/warrant-corpus` assume that the committed `index.json` holds exactly the four v1 rows, so they would turn red when S5b commits real `v2-*` rows: the defect [D40] was meant to remove, moved into its own tests. S5-1b edits the three lanes' tests together so that they derive every count from the committed index or use invented `v2-` names (R49). It touches no runner source, so no manifest's `runner.src_sha256` moves, and **it lands before S5b**. This is the second and last use of the exception to "no single PR carries more than one of {generator, oracle, fixture, one lane runner, one adopter}" (section 13); like the first, it is no precedent for a PR that changes runner source. Rejected: three PRs, one per lane (the reasoning of [D40]); folding the test edits into S5b (a fixture PR would then carry three lane packages).

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R40. Depth (section 2.2 rule 1, section 14, the `type` and `descriptor` codes).** The two limits of 16 count different things, and both tools already agree on each. The depth of a **type expression** counts the constructors enclosing a name: a bare name is depth 0 and each `(`, `List<`, `Option<`, `Result<` adds one, so 16 nested wrappers are accepted and 17 refused (the generator's `resolve` in `lower.rs`, the oracle's `_resolve` in `lower.py` and `parse_type` in `conformance.py` once S5a lands, each starting at 0 and refusing above 16). A **descriptor tree** counts nodes on a path: a leaf alone is one level and 16 levels are accepted (`describe` and `descriptor_size` in both tools start at 1). A binder type of depth 16 therefore has a 17-level descriptor: the type is legal and the generator refuses the binder (`limit`), which in the kit is a `descriptor` refusal, not a `type` one. The oracle's `descriptor_for` counts from 0 where the generator's `describe` counts from 1; whether the oracle refuses such a binder was not checked for this erratum and is checked, and aligned if needed, under CV2-F17. **S5a:** the tables gain the boundary on both sides (16 wrappers accepted and 17 refused for a type; 16 levels accepted and 17 refused for a descriptor) beside its present cases, which stayed clear of the boundary, and the code and docstring of `parse_type` are corrected.
* **R41. A bare `ok` or `err` (Appendix A).** The IR node carries no `Result` type. A bare `ok(e)` has an undetermined error type and a bare `err` an undetermined payload; each unifies with any `Result` whose other component matches (the generator fixes both from the context when it lowers; a consumer of the IR has no context and unifies). The case of an `err` must be declared by an error type in `types`; otherwise the tree is `ill-typed`. An undetermined component is legal in an intermediate (`is_err` of a bare `ok`, `eq` of two bare `err`), and **a kit case never has a result type with an undetermined component**: a table row whose derived `expect.type` has one fails the build. **S5a.** The oracle's evaluator does not enforce this today; S5a's derivation does, in its static pre-pass.
* **R42. `Nat` arithmetic has type `Int` (section 3.2, `v2-arith`).** `+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` on `Nat` operands all have type `Int`; `T` is `Int` for every `Nat` or `Int` node. Both tools do this (the generator's binary and unary lowering give `Int` whenever the common type is not fixed-width; the oracle's exact arithmetic and `neg` return `Int`), and section 8.2 already says it of `off-by-one-consumed`. The earlier row named only `Nat - Nat`. No tool changes; **S5a** keeps its cases.
* **R43. The `v2-chunk` kit is two documents (section 11).** One at the default `chunk_size` and one at 1,024, because a 2^24 suite at 1,024 would exceed `MAX_CHUNKS`, and a lane must be shown a recorded `chunk_size` that is not the default (section 5.4). Neither profile declares a fault, so R21 of section 16.13 stands for both. **S5a** (already so).
* **R44. `v2-domain` has no `types` field at `ir.core`.** No `ir.core` descriptor names a record or a variant, and the error cases of a `result` descriptor are in the descriptor. A later level may add an optional `types` field; absent means `{}`, so no S5b byte moves when S11d adds it.
* **R45. Five outcomes pinned, and what is never a vector (the code table of section 11).** (a) A suite row whose `chunks` is not `ceil(product / chunk_size)` is `corpus`: the row disagrees with itself before the file is read. (b) A row whose `discarded` is not `P - sum(cases)` is `chunk-file`: it is the cross-check of section 5.5 and needs the file. (c) A `Bytes<N>` whose width has a leading zero is `type` (section 2.1: `N` is a decimal literal, written canonically as in section 2.3). (d) A `subject_op` that names an op missing from `ops` is `ill-typed`, as is an `err` whose case is undeclared (R41). (e) A `max_ir_cost` outside `2,000..=65,536` in the `corpus` part of a `v2-chunk` case is `corpus`; the same value in a `v2-ir` case stays `ir-cost`. (f) **Schema violations of the case itself stay out of the kit for good**: an `index`, a `range` bound, a `chunk`, a `chunk_size` or a `slim_chunks` entry that is negative or not a JSON integer is a kit error, not a lane outcome. "An invalid `chunk_size`" in the `corpus` row of the table therefore means a non-negative JSON integer that is not a power of two in `2^10..=2^16`. **S5a** adds a case for each of (a) to (e) and makes `make_conformance.py` refuse a row of kind (f). **Amended by section 16.23 R126:** a `fault` field that is not a closed object of section 8.1 (an extra or missing key, a `kind` outside the three, an undefined level name in `requires`) is such a violation as well: a kit error, with no code.
* **R46. `v2-chunk` validation does not check the slim digests or the base set.** A lane validating a `v2-chunk` case compares neither `slim_inputs_sha256` nor `slim_expected_sha256` with anything and does not recompute the base set of section 5.6; it checks `slim_chunks` only as the `slim-chunks` row of the table says. Why this reading and not the opposite: section 5.5 lists what a consumer cross-checks the chunk file against (`chunks_sha256`, the chunk count, the two suite digests, the sums) and the slim digests are not among them; section 5.6 has the lane never recompute the list and gives the recomputation of the list, base set and witnesses to the oracle; section 9.2 makes the slim digests what a **slim run** must reproduce, which the lane compares at the end of that run and the manifest checker compares again (section 11, *Accounting*, where the base-set vector already sits); and a `v2-chunk` case enumerates one chunk, so it is not such a run. The lanes as S5-1 left them do not read a `v2-*` file at all, so no lane behaviour is changed. Consequence: **the kit's `slim_chunks` tampers need not re-seal the two digests**, a valid case may carry a `slim_chunks` list that the digests do not match, and a lane that compares them inside this validation fails such a case and is wrong. A tampered slim digest is not a `v2-chunk` vector; it is a run-level negative of S6b and S7b and of the manifest checker (S9). **S5a** (the derivation already behaves so; its README states the rule).
* **R47. `off-by-one` at the largest `Nat` is excluded.** On a `Nat` result the template of section 8.2 is exact and has type `Int` (R42), so a result of `2^64-1` gives `2^64`, which is no `Nat` value (section 2.3) and which section 8.2 does not define (unlike `off-by-one-consumed`, whose value never leaves its type). The kit holds no such case; `2^64-2`, which reaches the largest `Nat`, is the edge it tests. Defining the outcome is an erratum to section 8.2 for the first adopter that can reach it. **S5a** (already so).
* **R48. What [D58] does not limit.** The 20-digit limit is on text a tool **decodes**: a `lit` value, an `env` value, the `lhs` and `rhs` of `v2-arith`. The `value` of an `expect` of type `Int` in `v2-arith` and `v2-ir` is text the lane's harness **writes** from its own result and compares: canonical decimal (no `+`, no leading zero, `-` only for a non-zero value) of any value in the window of section 3.2, up to 39 digits. That is how the window edge is tested by arithmetic. **S5a.**
* **R49. What S5-1b changes (the rule of [D59]).** In each of the three lanes: (a) no test equates the number of rows or `total_cases` of the committed index with a literal or with the count of v1 categories (a lower bound is fine); an expected count is derived from the committed index. (b) A test that adds a row to a scratch copy either uses an invented name that starts with `v2-` and is none of the seven of section 11 (`v2-x`), or first removes the committed `v2-*` rows and their files from the copy, so that a real name is never added twice. (c) The checks S5-1 introduced are kept, not weakened: every known category required, every row digest-checked and counted, `total_cases` the sum, an unknown row accepted only with the `v2-` prefix. The acceptance is that of the S5-1b row of section 13.
* **R50. Names and the backlog.** The S5 PRs are now S5-0, S5-1, S5a-0, S5-1b, S5a to S5d and S5g, in the order of their rows: S5a-0 before S5a merges; S5-1b and S5a before S5b. CV2-F17 (the oracle's `Int` literal limit, [D58], and the descriptor-depth check of R40) is a new VER-BACKLOG item; its row and those of S5a-0 and S5-1b are added by the separate docs PR, as for S5-0. Left undecided and still out of the kit: a `level-missing` caused by a **type** and not a tag (R24 of section 16.13 has the S5b vectors use tags only). **Settled by [D64] (section 16.18 R69):** a type of a missing level that the checked expression reaches is `level-missing`, and S5e adds the vectors.

### 16.16 Owner decision of 2026-11-20 (S11a review)

Slice S11a-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what an independent review of S11a (the oracle's `ir.data` lowering, typing and dumps, section 13; its branch is not on `main`) needed from this document. One item needed the owner: [D60]. The others are the author's readings **R51** to **R61** (erratum candidates, not rulings), each checked against `checker.rs` and `parser.rs` of `rust/warrant-grammar` as merged and against the oracle's `lower.py` on the S11a branch unless it says otherwise. Sections 2.2 and 13, R13 of section 16.10, R27, R29, R34, R35 and R39 of section 16.14 and Appendix A.2 were amended in the same change. S11a implements every rule here before its dumps are committed, and S11b reproduces them.

**[D60] `Option` follows the checker (option A).** `["some", e]` has the type `Option<type(e)>`. An `Option<S>` flows to `Option<T>` when `S` flows to `T`, and two `Option` types unify by their payloads, as `Result` does. Tuples, records and variants do not lift. R39(a) of section 16.14 and the typing paragraph of Appendix A.2, which made `Option` a composite with no lifting, are corrected. Why: Appendix A.2 names `checker.rs` normative for exactly this, the checker lifts (`Type::is_assignable_to` and `Type::common_type`, used for a model body, call and constructor arguments, `if` branches and `==`), VER04j section 14.1 item 17 says "`Option<Nat>` still widens to `Option<Int>` as in v0", and the generator runs the checker before it lowers, so it accepts every such document: an oracle that refused them could never be matched by S11b. Rejected: keep `Option` strict in both tools and drop the "normative" clause, which would have the generator's lowerer refuse documents its own front end accepts and the adopters' authors write.

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R51. The exact relation of [D60].** *Flows* (the checker's `is_assignable_to`): a type flows to itself; `Nat` to `Int`; `Bytes<N>` to `Bytes`; `Option<S>` to `Option<T>` when `S` flows to `T`; `Result<S,E>` to `Result<T,E>` when `S` flows to `T` (the same `E`). Nothing else: a tuple, a record, a variant and a list are invariant, also under an `Option` (`Option<(Nat,Nat)>` does not flow to `Option<(Int,Int)>`), and the rule is recursive (`Option<Option<Nat>>` flows to `Option<Option<Int>>`). *Unify* (`common_type`): equal types; `Nat` and `Int` to `Int`; two `Bytes` types of different or forgotten width to `Bytes`, except a literal width against a named static width, which the checker does not unify; `Option<S>` and `Option<T>` to `Option<U>` when `S` and `T` unify to `U`; `Result<S,E>` and `Result<T,E>` likewise. The relation is the same in every position that asks for it: a `ctor` argument against its field, a model or op argument against its parameter, a model body against its result, the default of `unwrap_or` against the payload, the branches of an `if`, the arms of a `match`, the operands of `eq`/`ne`. An IR reader that types a tree bottom-up uses the same relation, so a `["some", e]` of type `Option<Nat>` is a well-typed operand where an `Option<Int>` is declared; the lift writes no node, and the value is the one section 2.3 gives. **Static width (decided by [D60]: both tools follow the checker).** In the checker a width is part of the type, not a fact kept beside the expression: a bytes literal is `Bytes<len>` whatever is expected (`typecheck_literal`); a `concat` of two operands of literal width is `Bytes<sum>`; an `if` or a `match` whose branches or arms have the same `Bytes<n>` keeps it, and otherwise they unify to `Bytes`; `be_bytes` and `le_bytes` give `Bytes<w/8>`; a binder, parameter, field, projection or call has its declared type; `take`, `drop` and a `concat` with an operand that is plain `Bytes` or has a named static width give plain `Bytes`. `Some(e)` and `Ok(e)` wrap **that** type (`Option<type(e)>`, `Result<type(e),E>` with the `E` of the expected `Result`), and the flow relation above is applied to the wrapped type. So both tools accept exactly this: `Some(e)` where an `Option<Bytes<M>>` is wanted, and `Ok(e)` where a `Result<Bytes<M>,E>` is wanted, **if and only if the type of `e` is `Bytes<M>` by this rule**; and either of them where `Option<Bytes>` or `Result<Bytes,E>` is wanted for every `e` of a bytes type, sized or not. `Some(b"ab")` flows to `Option<Bytes<2>>` and `Option<Bytes>` and not to `Option<Bytes<3>>`; `Ok(b"ab")` flows to `Result<Bytes<2>,E>` and `Result<Bytes,E>`; with `a: Bytes<1>`, `Ok(concat(a, b"0102"))` flows to `Result<Bytes<3>,E>` and `Some(concat(a, b"0102"))` to `Option<Bytes<3>>`, neither to a width of 4; `Ok(take(a, 1))` does not flow to `Result<Bytes<1>,E>`. An `Err(E.CASE)` has the value type its expected `Result` names, so it flows whatever the width. The same types sit under a tuple literal, where nothing lifts: `(b"ab", n)` is a `(Bytes<2>,Nat)` and is not a `(Bytes,Nat)` (the tuple leniency of R61). That is the rule for source; the zero literal a `missing-error` template writes into a tuple is typed by section 16.17 R67. The width decides the flow and is written nowhere: no node is added, a bytes `lit` keeps the written type `Bytes`, and no `result_type` moves. **The oracle before S11a** (`_flows` and `_static_width` of `lower.py`, the same code on `main` and on the S11a branch) forgets these widths in the type, keeps them beside the node and consults them only when the `Bytes` expression itself meets a `Bytes<N>`. A literal directly under `Ok`, `Some` or a tuple literal is typed from the expected type and is accepted; a `concat`, or an `if` or `match` whose branches are not literals, is refused there, because the wrapper's type has already lost the width. That is section 16.10 R13(c) (`Ok(concat(a, b"..."))` refused for `Result<Bytes<N>,E>`): a **pre-existing over-refusal** of a document the checker accepts, which the generator's lowerer copied from the oracle (`tests/tuple_forms.rs` asserts it), and not a rule. R13(c) is corrected: **S11a** makes the oracle carry the width in the type through `Option`, `Result`, `if` and `match`, and **S11b** does the same in the generator's lowerer and turns that assertion round. **No committed byte moves and no committed fixture is affected:** the over-refusal is a typing error, which refuses a whole document, so no committed corpus or lowering dump holds such a unit, and no committed refusals dump has a row for it. **S11a:** `_assignable` and `_unify` gain the `Option` arm; the refusals-dump rows `a-some-is-not-lifted-to-a-wider-option` and `an-option-nat-is-not-an-option-int` become lowering rows.
* **R52. Where a `None` takes its type (R39(b) made exact).** A source `None` is typed only by the **expected type** of its position, and is a typing error (the checker's `W0301`; code `type`) when the position has none or it is not an `Option`. The expected type is handed down, never across. *A position is given one* when it is: a model body (the declared result); an argument of a model, an op or a constructor (the parameter or field type); the second operand of `unwrap_or` (the payload type of the first); the right operand of `==` or `!=` (the type of the left operand, the one case where an operand supplies it, and left to right only). *A position inherits the expected type of the expression it is in* when it is: a parenthesised expression; either branch of an `if`; an arm body of a `match`; the payload of `Some` or `Ok` (the payload of an expected `Option` or `Result`); component `i` of a tuple literal (component `i` of an expected tuple of that arity). *Every other position has none*: the first operand of `unwrap_or`, a `match` scrutinee, the left operand of `==` and `!=`, the base of a projection or field access, an operand of any other built-in (none of them types a `None`). A claim, example and refinement body is expected to be `Bool`, which types no `None`. So `o == None` is typed and `None == o` is not; `Some(None)` is typed where an `Option<Option<T>>` is expected; and **a branch or arm never takes its type from its sibling**: `if c then at(b, i) else None` and `match x with A -> at(b, i) | B -> None` are typing errors wherever no expected type reaches them, the first operand of `unwrap_or` included (`checker.rs`: `unwrap_or` types its first operand with no expected type, and `if` passes its own expected type to both branches). This is why R29(d)'s third example was wrong; it now reads `unwrap_or(if c then at(b, i) else at(b, 0), d)`, which the checker accepts and which needs `ir.fixed` and `ir.data` as the example says. The written type is the expected one: under an expected `Option<Int>`, `if c then Some(n) else None` with `n: Nat` lowers its `None` to `["none", "Int"]` and has the type `Option<Int>` by R51. This reading is about `None` only and changes nothing for a bare integer literal or `Ok`/`Err`. **S11a:** a `None` is no longer typed from the other branch, arm or operand.
* **R53. R35(5): the order across kinds of unit.** R35(5) said "units are reported in declaration order", which does not say what happens between a model and a type declared after it. It now reads: units are processed as type declarations, operation signatures, models, operation refinements, then claims and examples, each in declaration order. The first error that is not a level or feature refusal, in that order, is named, unless a unit already processed and declared on an earlier line holds a level or feature refusal, which is named instead. A document with refusals and no other error is named by the refusal of the unit declared on the earliest line. So a model with a typing error declared before a `record` that holds a list is the typing error, and declared after it is `ir.list`. Two checks belong to no unit and are named where they are met, yielding to nothing: a name, an error case or a test name declared twice (before any unit) and a record or variant that contains itself (after the type declarations). The declaration limits stay first (R35(1)). **S11a:** a typing error of an operation signature yields like any other unit's; at 7b4d483e851 it was raised directly and named over an earlier declaration's `ir.list`.
* **R54. R35(2): a level against a corpus error.** R35(2) prefers a level or feature refusal to a **typing** error of the same unit, whichever comes first in the source. A corpus error is not displaced: between a level or feature refusal and a corpus error, the one met first in lowering order is named (pre-order; a `match`'s patterns before its arm bodies). So a `match` whose second pattern binds a name twice and whose first arm body calls `fold` is the corpus error, and one whose scrutinee calls `fold` is `ir.seq`.
* **R55. Appendix A.2 corpus errors: source against IR.** Of the errors A.2 lists, the Appendix A.1 ones (fall-through, an unreachable arm, the bind rules) are corpus errors at source and in IR alike. The last four (an undeclared `ctor` name or a wrong operand count; a `field` index or operand; a `none` type argument; a pattern that does not fit its value's type) are errors of the IR reader (oracle code `ir`). At source the same mistakes are typing errors (oracle `type`, generator `check`), as for mismatched arm types; a source `None` has no type argument to get wrong.
* **R56. Limits that are also language limits (R34 and section 2.2 made exact).** `parser.rs` allows a record or a constructor 1 to 32 fields (`MAX_FIELDS`, one bound for both) and a variant 2 to 256 constructors (`MAX_CTORS`), and refuses anything outside at parse. Against rule 3: the **constructor count** (64 against 256) and the **fields of a constructor** (8 against 32) are strictly inside, so the `limit` refusal exists for 65 to 256 constructors and for 9 to 32 fields; the **record field count** (32 against 32) is not, so no source reaches the record `limit`. A record of 33 fields, a variant of one constructor, a record of no fields and a constructor with an empty field list are parse errors. **S11a, S11b:** a refusals dump pins no row for them (the S11a branch's `a-record-of-33-fields` and `a-variant-of-one-constructor` are withdrawn; the generator cannot produce them), and keeps the variant rows; `order-limit-before-a-level` is rebuilt from a 9-field constructor (or a 65-constructor variant), not from a 33-field record. Rule 2's limit of 64 error cases is reachable from source too (`parse_error_decl` has no case bound), and its row stays. Rule 3 stands whole for a corpus reader, which checks `types` with no parser in front.
* **R57. `ir.list` is the refusal, not a typing error (R27).** Three cases R27 did not state or stated too briefly: a list operand of `len concat take drop at` is `level ir.list` however the operand came by its type; a call to an op whose signature is refused for `ir.list` is `level ir.list`, also when the op is declared after the caller, and never "an unknown name"; and an IR tree with the tag `list` is refused by an IR reader as the level `ir.list` (the tag is a known tag of a reserved level), never as an unknown tag. The third is checked against the S11a branch; the first two are rules for S11a. A lane refuses the same three, and the `list` descriptor, by [D63] (section 16.18 R68, R76).
* **R58. Declared names that are reserved built-ins (section 2.2).** A record, variant or constructor name that is a reserved built-in of the document's version is refused: the checker seeds its module symbols with `V0_BUILTINS` (and, in a v1 document, the v1 types and functions) before it reads a declaration, so the name is a duplicate symbol (`W0201`). The oracle refuses it as a typing error. This is the declaration-side partner of the bind rule of CV2-F8 (section 16.10 R2) and uses the same version-scoped set. **S11a.** **Amended by section 16.23 R123:** a `types` table carries no document version, so for a table the set is `V0_BUILTINS` and the six v1 type names (R123 says what that refuses); the version-scoped set stays the rule for a source document.
* **R59. The `types` object of the lowering dump (section 13, S11b).** S11a's lowering dump carries a `types` object, with the section 2.2 shape, **only for a document that declares a record or a variant**; it holds the records and variants and no error type (those stay under `errors`), with keys sorted as canonical JSON sorts them. It is part of the bytes S11b reproduces. The dumps of documents with neither (every fixture before `data-forms-v1`) have no such key and do not move.
* **R60. The arithmetic census (section 16.10 R13(d)).** R13(d) gave the oracle 18 `ok` and 45 `ir.data` for arithmetic, the generator 8 and 55. From S11a the oracle's row is **8 `ok` (all `ir.core`) and 55 `ir.data`**, the generator's numbers: all 63 suites lower, and the ten that only counted a record type in `requires`, which the oracle used to evaluate, are refused at evaluation with the other 45 and so are withdrawn from evaluation until S11c-1, as R37 and [D53] have it. The difference R13(d) records therefore closes in the count at S11a and in the refusal's stage at S11b (lowering in the generator until then). The numbers are the S11a branch's census pins.
* **R61. Names and the backlog.** This PR is S11a-0, before S11a merges (section 13). **CV2-F18** is a new VER-BACKLOG item, added by the separate docs PR as for S11-0b: the oracle lifts through tuples (`RT((n, n))` with `n: Nat` into a field of type `(Int,Int)` is accepted, as is any `(Nat,Nat)` flowing to `(Int,Int)`), a leniency older than S11a that the checker refuses (VER04j section 4.4) and that no rule of this document allows; it is fixed with S11a's change to `_assignable` (R51), and `_unify`, which has the same tuple arm, is checked with it. No committed corpus can depend on it, because the generator's front end is the checker.

**Not closed by this section.** How the generator comes to name a level or feature refusal where its front end has a typing error of the same unit (R35(2), R53) is S11b's to implement and is not checked here; section 16.10 R13(a)'s difference stays as it is.

### 16.17 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (O1, kit corrections)

Slice S6-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) closes open item O1 of section 16.13 before S6b builds the first v2 manifest writer, and records one gap the S11a re-review found in the `missing-error` template. Two items needed the owner: [D61], in two parts, and [D62]. **R64**, **R65** and **R67** are the author's readings (erratum candidates, not rulings); **R62**, **R63** and **R66** record the reasons for, and the details of, the form the owner confirmed (details the owner did not state, such as the id character set and the unknown-category rule, remain readings). Sections 8.2, 9.1, 11 and 13, open item O1 of section 16.13, R3 of section 16.10 and R51 of section 16.16 were amended in the same change. R62 to R66 were checked against the kit as S5b committed it (`code/specs/fixtures/warrant-corpus-conformance-v1/` at `cc4823a59d3`) and against `code/scripts/warrant_corpus_conformance.py`; R67 against `faults.py` and `ir_eval.py` of `python/warrant-oracle` on `main` and on the S11a branch (`d2a4177ab6b`).

**[D61] A lane's v2 manifest pins exactly the conformance cases the lane ran (option (b) of O1).** `conformance_sha256` is a digest of what the lane ran at its declared `levels`, and not a digest of the whole `index.json`. The consequence the owner chose it for: kit growth at a level a lane does not implement moves nothing in that lane's committed manifest, and a lane re-pins when it adopts a level. A lane that implements every level gets the digest over everything. Rejected, from O1's own list: **(a)** keep the digest of `index.json` and batch kit growth into regeneration PRs that touch several lanes (a lane's `check` is red between a kit PR and its own next PR, or every kit PR waits for a batch); **(c)** keep the digest of `index.json` and regenerate every lane's manifest in each kit PR (every lane is then regenerated at **every** kit PR, growth at a level the lane lacks included, so the work grows with the number of lanes times adopters times kit PRs; [D62] below allows a regeneration in a kit PR only for a correction to a case a committed manifest pins, and only for the lanes that ran it). **The owner also confirmed the unit and the form** (asked about rows versus cases, 2026-11-20: "Case-level, as drafted"): the digest is the SHA-256 of the run cases, each as canonical JSON, in ascending `id` order, and the manifest carries `conformance_run`, a map from category to the count of cases run. R62, R63 and R66 below give the reasons and the exact bytes; the case unit, the ordered canonical-JSON digest and the `conformance_run` key are decided, not readings. Sections 9.1 and 11 are normative; S6b and S7b implement it and S9 validates it.

**[D62] A kit PR that corrects a pinned case regenerates the lanes that ran it, in the same PR (a standing exception).** A kit PR that corrects a case already pinned by a committed v2 manifest also regenerates, in the same PR, the manifests of exactly the lanes that ran that case; a lane that skipped it is not touched. This is a **standing, listed exception** to "no single PR carries more than one of {generator, oracle, fixture, one lane runner, one adopter}" (section 13, "Order and size", next to the flip [D29], S5-1 [D40] and S5-1b [D59]): the regeneration is mechanical, the PR says which manifests moved and that only `conformance_sha256` and `conformance_run` moved in them, and it edits no runner source and no adopter file other than those manifests. The reason: under [D61] the checker requires the manifest to equal the committed kit, so the lanes that ran a corrected case are red on `main` unless their manifests move with it, and no lane PR can go first. Rejected: **freezing pinned cases**, so that a wrong case could be retired only by adding a new case and deprecating the old one later (a known-wrong vector would stay normative in every lane that pins it for as long as the deprecation takes). [D62] covers corrections to pinned cases only; it is not option (c) of O1, which regenerated every lane at every kit PR.

**Author refinements, and the reasons for the form the owner confirmed.** R62, R63 and R66 belong to [D61] as far as it states them (the case unit, the ordered canonical-JSON digest, the `conformance_run` map); R64, R65 and R67 are not owner decisions.

* **R62. The unit is the case, not a row of `index.json`.** O1 (b) said "the rows the lane ran (the categories and levels it declares)". `index.json` has one row per **category file** (`category`, `path`, `cases`, `sha256` of the whole file), and a level is not a category: S10d, S11d and the `ir.list` fixture PR add cases of higher levels **into** `v2-value.json`, `v2-ir.json`, `v2-domain.json`, `v2-chunk.json` and `v2-fault.json` (section 13), so the `sha256` and `cases` of those rows move for a lane that runs none of the new cases. A digest over index rows would therefore move in every lane at every kit PR, which is what [D61] rules out. The only thing `requires` is written on, and the only thing a lane runs or skips (section 11), is a case; so "the rows the lane ran" is read as **the cases the lane ran**. The four v1 categories have no `requires` and are run whole by every lane, so they are in every lane's digest; their files are never edited (section 11), so they never move it.
* **R63. The byte form, and why this one.** The run cases in ascending `id` order, each as its canonical document (the VER04i section 9.1 writer, with its one final LF), concatenated, then SHA-256 (section 9.1). (1) *Every lane already has both tools and is proven on them in the same run:* the strict reader and the canonical writer are the `json` category, and "SHA-256 of a canonical object" is already what `label-digest` asks; a lane needs no new parser, no byte offsets into a file and no second serialisation. (2) *It is independent of the file:* a case's position, the order of cases and the indentation it has inside `cases` do not enter, so reordering a file or inserting a case of another level beside a run case moves nothing, where hashing byte ranges of the file would need a reader that keeps spans and would move with layout. (3) *It needs nothing new in the kit:* no per-case digest in `index.json` and no new file, so no committed byte of the kit moves, the three v1 lanes' index tests ([D40], [D59]) are untouched and S5d is unaffected. (4) *It is unambiguous:* a canonical document of an object begins with `{` in column 0 and its only other column-0 line is the closing `}`, strings cannot hold a raw LF, so the concatenation splits back into the documents in exactly one way; every committed id is ASCII and unique (the v1 script's structural check refuses a duplicate id), and section 9.1 makes the computation fail on any other, so the order is total and the same under a byte, code-unit or code-point comparison. (5) *It covers the whole case*, `summary` included: an edit to any byte of a run case's content moves the digest, and nobody has to argue which keys matter. Rejected: the index rows of the categories run (R62); one digest per level (a case may require two levels, and `requires` is a set); hashing only `id` and `expect` (an edited input with the old expectation would pass).
* **R64. Who computes it, who checks it, and what cannot be skipped.** The runner hashes the cases it executed, not a selection it recomputes afterwards, as `depth` is set from what ran (section 9.1). The checker derives the selection from `levels` and the committed fixture and takes nothing else from the manifest; a mismatch is one failure, "the conformance cases this lane ran are not the committed ones", and `conformance_run` says in which category to look. The checker verifies the fixture against its own index first, so a category file edited without its index row fails there, for every lane, whatever its levels. A lane cannot shrink its digest by not knowing a category: a `v2-*` category the runner does not know that holds a case with `requires` inside the lane's `levels` stops the manifest write (section 11, Gate [D7]); only a category with no such case is digest-checked and passed over. *Cross-lane check, an acceptance of S7b:* for the same kit and the same `levels`, the TypeScript value equals the Python value byte for byte, and both equal the check values of section 9.1 at the S5b kit.
* **R65. What still moves a manifest, and who regenerates.** (a) *A lane adopts a level* (S10e, S10f and the like): `levels` changes, so both values change; that PR already regenerates the lane's manifests, because the runner source and so `runner.src_sha256` move. This is the re-pin of [D61]. (b) *A kit PR corrects a case that committed manifests ran* (a corrected vector, an edited or removed case): those lanes' `check` would be red on `main`, so **that kit PR regenerates the manifests of exactly the lanes that ran the changed cases**, mechanically, in the same PR, and says which manifests moved and that only these two keys moved. This is **[D62]**, the owner's ruling and a listed exception to the one-per-PR rule (section 13); for an `ir.core` case the lanes concerned are every lane with a committed v2 manifest. *A case added at a level a committed manifest already declares* (a new `ir.core` case after S6c) moves the same manifests in the same way; [D62] as ruled names corrections, and the author's reading, not part of the ruling, is that such an addition is handled as [D62] handles a correction, to be confirmed by the owner with the first kit PR that does it. (c) *Anything else* (a case of a level the lane lacks, a new category at such a level, `total_cases`, a row's `sha256`) moves no manifest.
* **R66. The manifest records counts by category, not a list of cases.** `conformance_run` is recorded because a bare digest cannot say what it covers: a reader of the manifest sees that a lane at `ir.core` ran, say, 221 `v2-ir` cases and not the 300 the index lists, and a failed check names a category. It is derived (the checker recomputes it), so it adds no trust. A category with no run case has no key, and no skipped count is recorded, because either would move with kit growth at a level the lane lacks, against [D61]. A list of case ids is **not** recorded: it is over a thousand strings that `levels` and the committed kit already determine, the checker must not trust it, and the digest already binds every id. The v2 manifest key set of section 9.1, not yet implemented by any lane, gains this one key.
* **R67. `missing-error`: a zero literal has the type it is the zero of (a gap in R3 and [D50], from the S11a re-review).** Section 8.2 writes `["lit",{"type":"Bytes","value":""}]` for a leaf of declared type plain `Bytes` when the op has no parameter of exactly that type. Under R51 a width is part of the type, a bytes literal is `Bytes<len>`, and a tuple is invariant; read that way the literal is a `Bytes<0>`, the tuple built for a payload `(Bytes,Bool)` is a `(Bytes<0>,Bool)`, and `["ok", P]` does not flow to `Result<(Bytes,Bool),E>`, so the template section 8.2 calls well typed would be refused. **The rule:** a zero literal that `build(T)` writes has the type `T`. For plain `Bytes` it is a `Bytes`, the type the `lit` node itself names; for `Bytes<N>` with a literal `N` it is `Bytes<N>`, which is also its length; the scalar zeros already name their type. So `P` has the type `X` **exactly** whenever `X` is a tuple or a record, at every depth of nesting, and section 8.2's "well typed" holds under invariance. *For an IR reader, which has only the tree:* a fault row's `expr` is typed against the op's declared result type as its expected type, handed down as R52 hands one down (the branches of an `if`, the payload of `ok`, component `i` of a `tuple`, argument `i` of a `ctor`); a bytes `lit` in a position whose expected type is plain `Bytes` has the type `Bytes`, and anywhere else it is `Bytes<len>` as R51 has it. Outside a tuple this changes no acceptance, since `Bytes<len>` flows to `Bytes` (a `ctor` argument, the top-level payload); it decides only under a tuple, where nothing flows. **R51 is not reopened for source:** `(b"ab", n)` written in a document is a `(Bytes<2>,Nat)` and is refused where a `(Bytes,Nat)` is wanted, by the checker, before any IR exists, so no lowered tree of an accepted document holds a literal in such a position and the reader's rule is met only in a template. **Against R31:** (a) tuples and records are built and leaves looked up, unchanged; the looser top-level match of (b) is untouched (there the payload is not under a tuple); (c)'s new acceptances are the top-level ones, which flow under R51 without this rule. **No committed byte moves:** the rule is about a type and writes no node; the literal, `requires` and the cost are as section 8.2 has them. **Why nothing fails today:** on `main` and on the S11a branch the oracle types the literal as `Bytes<0>` (`ir_eval.py`, the `lit` arm) and still compiles the template, because the `if` merges its two `Result` branch types without comparing their payloads (`_merge_result`) and nothing compares the template's type with the op's result (`faults.py::_compile_fault` keeps the compiled function and drops the type); on `main` the evaluator's tuple assignability is also still the component-wise leniency of CV2-F18 (R61), which S11a removes, so from S11a the template's type `Result<(Bytes<0>,Bool),?>` no longer flows to `Result<(Bytes,Bool),E>` and only the missing comparison lets it through. No committed corpus, dump or kit file holds such a row (searched at `cc4823a59d3`: no `tuple` node with an empty `Bytes` literal under it in any committed JSON that names `missing-error`). **Enforced in S11c-1** (the oracle's evaluator PR, which types and evaluates the fault templates and opens the fault gate, section 13): whichever change first **compares the type of a fault row's `expr`, or of a tuple inside it, with a declared type** (the payloads of the two `Result` branches of an `if`, a check of the compiled `expr` against the op's result, or a `ctor` argument that is a tuple against its field) carries this rule in the same PR, with three tests: a `missing-error` on an op with result `Result<(Bytes,Bool),E>` and no `Bytes` parameter compiles; the same tree with a one-byte literal in a `(Bytes<2>,Bool)` position is refused; and a `missing-error` on `Result<R,E>`, where the record `R` has a field of type `(Bytes,Bool)` and the op has no `Bytes` parameter, compiles (the oracle's `ctor` typing already checks each argument against its field, so in S11c-1 this template is refused by existing code, whatever the `if` does, unless the rule is in). If such a comparison lands in an earlier oracle PR, the rule and the tests land there. S11a makes the oracle's tuple assignability invariant (R61, CV2-F18) but makes no such comparison for a template (`_merge_result` is unchanged and `_compile_fault` does not compare against the result), so the template still compiles there; S11a adds only the regression test that it does. S11b has nothing to do (the generator's lowerer types source, and a template is not source), and neither has S11c-2 beyond byte parity (the generator's evaluator is untyped, section 16.9 item 3). The lanes meet it through the `v2-fault` vector S10d adds (section 13), for a runner that types the trees it reads.

**Not closed by this section.** The named-width and top-level cases of section 8.2 (a `Bytes<M>` parameter taken for a `Result<Bytes<N>,E>` payload by the looser match of R31(b)) are as they were and were not re-examined. No backlog item is added or changed.

### 16.18 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (S6a review)

Slice S6a-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what the review of S6a (the Python lane's runner library at `ir.core`, section 13; its branch is not on `main`) needed from this document. Three items needed the owner: [D63], [D64] and [D65]. The others are the author's readings **R68** to **R78** (erratum candidates, not rulings). R70 to R73 began as the reviewer's drafts; each was checked against the generator (`warrant-corpus-gen` 0.6.0: `lower.rs`, `ir.rs`, `eval.rs`, `cost.rs`, `suite.rs`) and the oracle (`ir_eval.py`, `domains.py`, `conformance.py`) as merged at `c79cf8564c9`, unless it says otherwise. Sections 3.2, 4.1, 4.2, 5.1, 5.3, 7.3, 9.1, 11, 13, 14 and 16.2, R24 of section 16.13, R50 of section 16.15, R57 of section 16.16 and Appendices A, A.1 and A.2 were amended in the same change. No committed byte moves: no committed corpus holds a list, and the kit as committed holds no `list` descriptor, no `List<T>` type and no case over an op with `width_params`. The new vectors are S5e's (section 13).

**[D63] Lanes and tools refuse the `list` domain descriptor and `List<T>` as the level `ir.list`, until the `ir.list` amendment settles list order.** The `list` row of section 5.1 and every `List<T>` type are refused, naming the level `ir.list`, by the generator, the oracle and every lane. No lane implements a list domain, a list value or a list binding before the amendment that defines `ir.list`, and that amendment is where the order question of section 16.2 items 1 and 2 is settled; until then the two items stay open in section 16.2 and bind nothing.
