Why: the formula of section 5.1 takes a prefix of the element domain `D`, and which values that prefix holds depends on the order of `D`, which is exactly what items 1 and 2 leave undecided (for `List<Bytes<2>>` the corpus order and the Rust harness prelude give different case sets). Rejected: **implementing the formula of section 5.1 in the lanes now.** It would put into every lane an enumeration whose order may change, with no generator or oracle output to check it against (both refuse lists, [D46]) and no kit vector, and it would have to be edited again in every lane when the amendment picks the order. Section 5.1's `list` row, section 7.3 and the `ir.list` row of section 4.1 are amended; R68 has the detail.

**[D64] `level-missing` is decided per suite, from what the checked expression reaches.** A lane decides whether it lacks a level for one suite at a time, from what that suite's checked expressions reach: the suite's environment (its binders and their types), its literals, and the signatures of the models and ops in its call closure, with the bodies of those models. A model or an op that needs a level the lane lacks is **recorded not-live, not refused**, and so is every suite and control that reaches it; the rest of the corpus runs. A lane that implements only `ir.core` therefore still runs the `ir.core` suites of a mixed corpus. The same rule decides `level-missing` and `requires` in the kit, which settles the item R50 of section 16.15 left open, "a `level-missing` caused by a type": a type of a missing level that the checked expression reaches is `level-missing`. Rejected: **refusing the whole corpus** when any part of it needs a missing level. A lane at `ir.core` could then run nothing of an adopter that has one suite or control of another level (ct-compare's own `eq_u64` control needs `ir.fixed`), `not_live` and the rows S10e and S10f of section 13 would describe nothing, and no lane could produce evidence before it implemented every level. Sections 4.1, 4.2 rule 7, 9.1, 11 and 14 are amended; R69 has the detail.

**[D65] A model or an op that needs a missing level gets no manifest row (option (a)).** [D64] has such a model or op "recorded not-live"; the record is the rows that already exist. The not-live suite and control rows that reach it name the deferring level (section 4.1), `not_live` counts suites and controls as before, and the manifest key set of section 9.1 stays closed: no key is added for a model or an op. This closes R69 (i) for ops as well as for models. Why: a model has no id in the evidence and no row of its own anywhere in the manifest, and every consequence of a not-live model or op is already visible in the suites and controls it makes not-live. Rejected: **a separate list of not-live models and ops in the manifest** (a new key in a closed key set, for information the not-live rows and the corpus already determine, which the checker would have to recompute and could not trust). Section 9.1 is amended; R69 (c) and (i) have the detail.

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R68. What [D63] asks of a lane.** (a) *The names are known, the content is not implemented.* A lane recognises the descriptor kind `list`, the type `List<T>` and the IR tag `list` (section 16.16 R57) as names of the reserved level. Its refusal of each is the level refusal naming `ir.list`: never an unknown `d`, an unknown type or an unknown tag. No lane declares `ir.list` in its `levels`, because there is nothing to implement. (b) *The refusal is per unit, by [D64].* A suite, control, model or op that reaches a list is not-live naming `ir.list`, and what does not reach it runs. No corpus the generator writes holds one today, because the generator refuses such a document whole. (c) *The kit.* `v2-domain` has no level code, and a case with a `list` descriptor would carry `ir.list` in its `requires` and be skipped by every lane, so there is no such case; the disposition table of section 11 already gives the list vectors to the fixture PR of `ir.list`. The one vector [D63] gets now is the `v2-ir` case with an `env` entry of type `List<U8>` (R69). (d) *What is kept.* The list text of sections 2.1, 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 5.1 and 7.3 stays as the starting text of the amendment and binds no implementation. (e) *The tools do not change.* The generator and the oracle already refuse a document that needs a list, whole (section 16.14 R27, R28). **S6a, S7a:** no list code; a test that a `list` descriptor and a `List<T>` type are refused as `ir.list`.
* **R69. What [D64] reaches, and what stays whole-corpus.** (a) *Reach.* For a suite: its binders with their types and descriptors, its `admit`, its `body` and, for a `reference` suite, its `expected`; every model in the call closure of those trees, with its body and its parameter and result types; and the parameter and result types of every op they call. For a control: its fault row's expressions and the op's parameter and result types (section 8.2), as the `v2-fault` row of section 11 already had it. For a `v2-ir` case: `ir`, the types of `env`, and the same closure. This is the domain over which section 16.10 R9 computes `requires`, so a suite is live exactly when its `requires` is inside the lane's `levels`; R9 did not need to change. (b) *The level of a construct.* A tag has the level of Appendix A, A.1 or A.2, with the type-directed cases of R76. A type has a level too: `Bool`, `Nat`, `Int`, the fixed-width types, `Bytes`, `Bytes<N>` and `Result<T,E>` are `ir.core` (a `Result` adds the level of `T`); a tuple type is `ir.tuple`; a record, a variant and an `Option<T>` are `ir.data` (the intermediate use of section 4.1 is a tag-level exception and never names a type in an environment, a literal or a signature); `List<T>` is `ir.list`. A type written in a node counts: the type of a `lit`, a static type argument. A runner therefore reads the type syntax of every defined level, as R24 has it know every tag name. (c) *Models and ops.* A model has no `requires` (section 1.4); a lane finds that it needs a missing level by reading its tags and its signature, without compiling it. It marks the model not-live, and a suite or control is not-live when its call closure holds a not-live model or an op whose signature needs a missing level. **Three tiers.** (1) A model or op whose own tags and types are inside the lane's levels is compiled and validated in full whether or not anything reaches it: every code of section 11 applies, as S5b already pins (`v2-ir/subject-forbidden-in-unused-model`, `v2-ir/cost-model-body-2001-refused` and `v2-ir/model-order-from-the-array-not-the-keys` each refuse for a model that `ir` does not reach). (2) A model that needs a missing level is not compiled, and the lane still applies to it the checks that read tag names alone: `unknown-tag`, `subject-forbidden`, `model-order`, the expression depth of section 14 and the chain depth of R77. Its cost and its typing are not checked by that lane, which does not know the node shapes of a level it lacks; the generator, the oracle and every lane that implements the level check them. (3) Only `level-missing` is scoped by reach. The manifest does not change: `not_live` counts suites and controls (section 4.1) and a model or an op has no row [D65]. (d) *What is not a question of level.* An unknown tag, an unknown built-in or an unknown level name is a corpus error wherever it stands, in a not-live model too (section 4.2 rule 7, section 14); so are a `subject_op` in a model body, a `model_call` out of `model_order`, the digests, the key sets and the limits of section 14 that read tags alone, the expression depth and the model-chain depth among them (R77). The cost bound and the typing of a model that needs a missing level are the one exception, tier 2 of (c). This reading goes beyond what the owner stated; the alternative, not reading a not-live model at all, would let a corpus hide an unknown tag behind a level. (e) *A row that disagrees with its reach.* If a lane, compiling a suite whose `requires` is inside its `levels`, meets a construct of a level it lacks, the row is wrong and that is a corpus error, not a not-live suite: the generator said the lane could run it. (f) *The tools do not change.* [D64] is a rule for lanes and for the kit. The generator and the oracle still refuse, whole, a document that needs a level they lack (sections 10.4 and 13), and the oracle still treats a fault row of such a level as data. (g) *The kit.* In `v2-ir`, `level-missing` and `requires` are decided over what `ir` reaches (section 11): a model, an op or a `types` row that `ir` does not reach causes neither. S5e adds three vectors, each with `levels` `["ir.core"]`: an `env` entry of a tuple type (`level-missing`), an `env` entry of type `List<U8>` (`level-missing`, [D63]), and a case whose `models` holds a model with an `ir.fixed` tag that `ir` does not call (a value; its `requires` is `["ir.core"]`). By R24 the `requires` of the two refusing cases is their `levels`. **The oracle's kit derivation does the opposite on all three today** (probed at `c79cf8564c9`): at `levels` `["ir.core"]` an `env` entry of a tuple type and one of type `List<U8>` both derive a value, a model with an `ir.fixed` tag that `ir` does not call derives `level-missing`, and `requires_of` counts the tuple signature of an op that `ir` does not reach. So (f) is about documents only: S5e's oracle PR changes the kit's static pass and `requires_of` (`conformance.py`) to this rule. (h) *R24 and R50.* R24's "S5b's `level-missing` vectors use tags only" stays true of S5b; the type vectors are S5e's. R50's open item is closed. (i) *Closed by [D65]:* a not-live model or op has no manifest row, and the not-live suite and control rows that reach it name the deferring level. **S6a, S7a** implement the rule in the kit harness; **S6b, S7b** in the corpus reader.
* **R70. `subject_op` static widths (Appendix A, section 4.2 rule 3; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** The fourth element of `subject_op` is the list of static widths, one JSON integer in `0..=4096` per entry of the op's `width_params`, in order. A consumer substitutes them into the op's signature before typing the arguments: a parameter or result type `Bytes<N>` whose `N` is a name in `width_params` becomes `Bytes<w>` with the width at that position. A missing or extra entry, an entry that is not such an integer, or a list on an op without `width_params`, is `ill-typed`; so the element is present exactly when the op has `width_params`. *Checked.* The generator's `subject_call` (`lower.rs`) takes one literal width per static of the op's signature, in the signature's order, and refuses a named width at a call site (`W0701`); `Ir::to_json` (`ir.rs`) writes the fourth element as JSON integers and only when the list is not empty; the committed ct-compare corpus has `["subject_op","eq_fixed",[..],[0]]` against an op row with `"width_params":["N"]` and parameters of type `Bytes<N>`. The oracle's `_subject` (`ir_eval.py`) requires the same count, each entry an integer in `0..=4096`, and a list when the op has statics. **One difference:** the oracle accepts an empty fourth element on an op without `width_params`; the generator never writes one, the rule above refuses it, and S5e's oracle PR makes the oracle refuse it. *A fault row has no call site and no widths, and the two readings of that differ.* The oracle's witness search types a fault row at the widths of the suite it searches (`_compile_fault` in `faults.py` substitutes the statics that `_stand_in` takes from the suite's `subject_op`) and does not compare a rewrite's type with its parameter. A lane wraps the op once, with no call site, so it types the row with each named width left unstated, a parameter of type `Bytes<N>` with a named `N` being a plain `Bytes` there, and checks each rewrite against that type (corrected by section 16.19 R94: the check is a common type with that type, not a flow to it). The committed rows are well typed under both: the `ignore-last-byte` row on `eq_fixed` rewrites such a parameter with a `concat` of plain `Bytes` type, which the oracle accepts because it makes no comparison and a lane accepts because the parameter is plain `Bytes`. S5e's oracle PR pins one rule in `v2-fault` or corrects this paragraph. In a `v2-fault` case over such an op the `args` and `result` are decoded as `Bytes` values. *The stand-in.* A model has no `width_params`, so the stand-in model that `subjects` names for an op with `width_params` in a `v2-ir` case has plain `Bytes` in place of each named-width type, and the rule that a stand-in has the op's signature (section 11) is read with that substitution. **Not checked against a derivation:** the kit's op-row reader (`parse_type` in `conformance.py`) reads only a decimal width today, which is why the kit has no such case; S5e's oracle PR teaches it the named width and confirms this paragraph, or corrects it by erratum before the fixture PR. *Vectors (S5e):* `v2-ir`, `v2-chunk` and `v2-fault` each gain at least one case over an op with `width_params`; `v2-ir` also gains a missing width, an extra width, a width of 4,097 and a widths list on an op without `width_params`, each `ill-typed`. No lane has a committed v2 manifest, so [D62] does not apply to these additions.
* **R71. The class of an overflow under an impure guard (sections 3.2 and 4.2 rule 6, the `ir-overflow` row of section 11; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** `class` is `corpus` exactly when the overflowing node is on the pure trace of section 4.4, i.e. the generator evaluates it. A subject-independent node reached only under a guard whose condition contains a `subject_op` is not on the trace, and its overflow is `case`. *What the generator does.* `Program::trace` (`eval.rs`) evaluates a node without a subject beneath it only when its walk reaches that node, and the walk descends into a node that has one as follows: every argument of a `subject_op`; for an `if` with a subject call in a branch, the (pure) condition is evaluated and the taken branch walked; **for an `if` whose branches hold no subject call, only the condition is walked, and the branches are never evaluated**; for `and`, `or` and `implies` with a subject call in the right operand, the left is evaluated and the right walked if it runs; **when the right operand holds no subject call, only the left is walked**; a `match` likewise (the taken arm when an arm calls, else only the scrutinee); every operand of any other node. **A `model_call` is such a node: when one of its arguments holds a subject call the walk descends into the arguments and never evaluates the model's body.** So for `if subject(..) then <pure overflow> else 0` and for `subject(..) and <pure overflow>` the generator does not evaluate the guarded pure node while it generates expectations, and an honest corpus can hold one that overflows whenever the subject's answer lets it run. Calling that a corrupt corpus, as "no `subject_op` beneath the node" did, would be wrong; the oracle's `compile_trace` skips the same operands and its kit derivation already says `case`, so no tool changes. *For a lane, the same rule without a trace:* an overflow is `case` when the overflowing node has a `subject_op` beneath it; or is evaluated inside the body of a model entered, at any call depth, through a `model_call` one of whose arguments contains a `subject_op` (a model body holds no `subject_op` itself, so the first clause never covers it, and the overflow is `case` whether or not it depends on that argument); or lies, at any depth, in a branch of an `if`, the right operand of an `and`, `or` or `implies`, or an arm of a `match` whose condition, left operand or scrutinee contains a `subject_op`; otherwise it is `corpus`. The two statements agree because a pure guard takes the same branch in the lane as in the generator, and a node the walk reaches is evaluated whole under section 4.2 rule 2. The same split holds for a `Bytes` value over `MAX_IR_VALUE`. *Vectors (S5e, `v2-ir`):* the `if` form and the `and` form with a stand-in that makes the guarded node run (`ir-overflow`, `case`); the `if` form with a stand-in that does not (a value, `calls` 1); the twin whose guard is pure (`ir-overflow`, `corpus`); and a model whose body overflows whatever its argument is, called with a subject argument (`ir-overflow`, `case`).
* **R72. `errs` (section 5.1, the `descriptor` row of section 11; the reviewer's draft).** `errs` is the case list of the binder type's error type: 1 to 64 names matching the name grammar (section 2.2 rule 4), without duplicates, in declaration order, and equal to the declared cases when `types` is present. Anything else is `descriptor`. The bounds are those of section 2.2 rule 2. At `ir.core` a `v2-domain` case has no `types` field (section 16.15 R44), so there the list is checked by itself; "equal" is equality of the two lists, order included. **The oracle is too lenient today:** `descriptor_size` (`domains.py`) requires only a list of strings, so the oracle accepts an empty list, more than 64 names and a name outside the grammar, and it has no duplicate check either. The fix is under **CV2-F17** and lands in or before S5e's oracle PR, since the vectors cannot be derived without it. *Vectors (S5e):* in `v2-domain`, each `descriptor`: an empty list, 65 names, a duplicate, a bad name; and in `v2-chunk`, where `types` is present, a suite row whose binder's `errs` differs from the declared cases of its error type (`corpus`: the row is refused for its descriptor).
* **R73. A bytes `lit` (Appendix A, the `ill-typed` row of section 11).** A `lit` whose written type is `Bytes<N>`, or the object form with type `Bool`, is `ill-typed`. A bytes `lit` has the static type `Bytes<len>` (section 16.16 R51). The generator writes the type `Bytes` for every bytes literal and `["lit", true]` or `["lit", false]` for a Bool (`Ir::to_json`), and the oracle's `_lit` (`ir_eval.py`) accepts exactly those forms and types the literal `Bytes<len>`, so both tools already agree. The one exception to the static type is the zero literal of a `missing-error` template in a position whose expected type is plain `Bytes` (section 16.17 R67), which this reading leaves as it is. *Vectors (S5e, `v2-ir`):* a literal of two bytes passed to a `Bytes<2>` parameter (accepted); a `lit` with the written type `Bytes<2>` and a `lit` `{"type":"Bool","value":true}` (each `ill-typed`).
* **R74. The count of `take` and `drop` (Appendix A).** The count is a `Nat` or an `Int`. A fixed-width count (`take(b, 1u8)`) is `ill-typed`: the generator's lowering refuses every other count type ("a take/drop count is a Nat", `lower.rs`), the oracle's IR typer does the same (`_bytes_op`, `ir_eval.py`), and there is no implicit conversion from a fixed-width type (section 3.2). No tool changes. *Vector (S5e, `v2-ir`):* one fixed-width count, `ill-typed`.
* **R75. A suite has 0 to 8 binders (sections 5.3 and 14).** A claim without a `forall` (a ground claim, `claim c: f(b"00", b"00") == true`) has none: its product is 1, it has one chunk and one admitted case whose input tuple is empty, so its input stream is the prefix alone. Both tools accept it: the generator lowers a claim body that is not a quantifier with no binder and tests that "a ground claim with a call is fine" (`lower.rs`), the oracle enumerates "a claim with no binders" as one empty tuple (`suites.py`), and the committed `fixed-eval-v1` and `tuple-forms-v1` evaluation dumps of the oracle hold such suites (`"binders": []`, `product` 1, `cases` 1). The generator refuses more than 8 (`limit`, `suite.rs`). A consumer refuses a row with nine binders, or with a `product` over `MAX_SUITE_PRODUCT = 2^28`, before any case; in the kit both are `v2-chunk` cases with the code `corpus` (the row is refused for a reason that is neither `slim-chunks` nor `chunk-file`). A lane that refuses a row for having no binder is wrong. *Vectors (S5e, `v2-chunk`):* a ground suite (accepted, `cases` 1); nine binders and a product over `2^28` (each `corpus`).
* **R76. The level of `match` is type-directed (section 4.1, Appendices A.1 and A.2).** A `match` whose scrutinee has a static type other than `Result<X,E>`, or that holds a data pattern at any depth, is `ir.data`; a `match` on a `Result` with the patterns of Appendix A.1 alone is `ir.tuple`. `len`, `concat`, `take`, `drop` and `at` on a list operand are `ir.list` (section 16.16 R57), whatever level the tag has on bytes. The JSON is the same in each pair, so a lane that implements `ir.tuple` without `ir.data` must type the scrutinee before it accepts a `match`: it refuses, as `level-missing`, one on a bare tuple, a variant, a record, an `Option` or a `Bool`, and one with a `some`, `none`, `ctor` or `bool` pattern. This restates section 4.1 and Appendix A.2 for a lane; nothing changes in the tools. *Vectors:* **S10d** (`v2-ir`, `levels` `ir.core` and `ir.tuple`): a `match` on a `Result` runs, and a `match` on a bare tuple scrutinee is `level-missing`. **S11d** (`v2-ir`): at the same `levels`, a `match` on a `Result` with a data pattern is `level-missing`; with `ir.data` both run; and `len` on a list operand is `level-missing` with `ir.data` declared. That last vector cannot isolate R57: a list operand needs an `env` entry or a signature of type `List<T>`, which is `level-missing` by R69 before the tag is read; it is kept as a second `ir.list` refusal, at a lane that declares `ir.data`, and not as a test of R57.
* **R77. The 64-frame limit covers every model chain, called or not (section 14, the `depth` row of section 11).** The depth of a model is 1 plus the largest depth of a model its body calls, in any branch, and a model whose depth is over 64 is a corpus error whether or not a suite, a control or the checked expression calls it. The generator computes it so, for every model in declaration order (`ModelCosts::compute`, `cost.rs`). A lane computes it from the `model_call` tags alone, so a not-live model (R69) is counted like any other. **Two implementations are off by one here.** The oracle's kit derivation and the S6a lane as reviewed both count frames from the caller, so each refuses a call of `m65` and accepts a chain of 65 models that nothing calls (probed at `c79cf8564c9`: the derivation gives a value for 65 uncalled models and `depth` only for 66), while by the generator's definition `m65` alone has depth 65 and is refused. The S5b cases `depth-model-frames-64-accepted` and `depth-model-frames-65-refused` call the last model and cannot tell the two readings apart. S5e's oracle PR corrects the derivation, and the vector is therefore **mandatory**. *Vector (S5e, `v2-ir`):* 65 chained models that `ir` does not call, `depth`.
* **R78. Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is S6a-0. **S5e** is one PR, the oracle's kit tables and the regenerated fixture together, as S10d is ([D70], section 16.20, which amends the "two PRs" this reading first had); it lands after S6a-0 and before S6a is pushed, so S6a is written against the kit that holds these vectors. S5e only adds cases and no lane has a committed v2 manifest, so [D62] does not apply; if a lane commits one first, the additions are the "case added at a level a committed manifest already declares" of section 16.17 R65. **CV2-F17** gains the oracle's `errs` leniency (R72) and the empty widths list (R70). The VER-BACKLOG rows for S6a-0, S5e and CV2-F17 are added by the separate docs PR, as for S5-0.

**Not closed by this section.** The order of lists (section 16.2 items 1 and 2), which is the `ir.list` amendment's. Which typing of a fault row over an op with `width_params` the kit pins in `v2-fault`, and the decoding of its arguments, until S5e's oracle PR confirms R70.

### 16.19 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (S5c review)

Slice S5c-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what the review of S5c (the oracle's reference binding validator and the `v2-binding` tables, section 13; its branch is not on `main`) needed from this document. By R20 of section 16.13 the tables S5c commits are normative from the moment **S5c** merges, and the review found that they pin readings this document did not state, several of which every shipped v1 reader contradicts. Four items needed the owner: [D66] to [D69]. The others are the author's readings **R79** to **R93** (erratum candidates, not rulings), one per question of the review in its order (R79 is its question 1, R92 its question 14). **R94** is not from that review: it corrects one sentence of section 16.18 R70 and comes from the re-review of S6a. Most began as the reviewer's drafts; each was checked against the validator (`bindings_v2.py`) and its case table (`cases_binding.py`) as reviewed at `67e87e38e55` with the fixes the review asked for, which were being made while this was written, so where the reviewed code and this section differ the section states what the validator does after those fixes. Sections 7.1, 7.4, 7.6, 11, 13 and 14 and R20 of section 16.13 were amended in the same change, and R70 of section 16.18 gained a pointer to R94. No committed byte moves: `v2-binding.json` is not committed (S5d), no lane parses a v2 binding yet (S6b, S7b), and no v1 reader changes.

**[D66] A binding may declare a reserved level; the declaration is accepted and implements nothing.** A `levels` line may name `ir.seq`, `ir.list` or `ir.inout.records`. The validator knows all seven names of section 4.1 and accepts each, under the lattice rule of R80. Declaring a reserved level implements nothing: every suite, op, control and fault that needs it stays `not-live` in that lane, as in every lane, until the amendment that defines the level. Why: R20 has the validator know every level name, "any reserved level included", and a case that accepts the name stays valid when the level is defined. Rejected: **refusing the declaration with a new reason `level-reserved`** (the case that pins the refusal would have to be turned around when the level is defined, so every lane that ran it would re-pin, section 16.17 [D62]). R80 has the detail.

**[D67] The record `inout` forms belong to the undefined level `ir.inout.records`; the reference validator refuses them and the kit does not pin that.** The forms `struct:`, `slice-of:struct:`, `vec-of:struct:` and `codec:` of section 7.6 are the content of the reserved level `ir.inout.records`, which no amendment has defined. The reference validator keeps refusing a binding that names one, as `BindingInvalid` with the reason `inout-form`. The conformance kit does **not** pin that refusal: the case `inout-form-reserved-record-form` is dropped from the `v2-binding` table, so no lane re-pins when the level is defined. Rejected: **keeping the case** (an `ir.core` case, [D68], whose expected outcome the defining amendment would reverse; by [D61] and [D62] every lane with a committed v2 manifest would then regenerate it). R84 says what a lane may do meanwhile.

**[D68] Every binding case is `ir.core`.** Every `v2-binding` case carries `requires: ["ir.core"]`, whatever record, variant or tuple types its `corpus` declares. The binding line grammar is level-independent: the validator checks names and shapes, not values. Rejected: **labelling the cases whose corpus has a record, a variant or a tuple with `ir.data` or `ir.tuple`** (a lane at `ir.core` would then leave `type` lines and `Tuple=` unpinned although it must parse them, and "types carry levels" is a rule about values a runner produces, which a validator never does); **moving those cases to S11d** (the first lanes would define that part of the kit). R86 has the detail.

**[D69] `seed` has a strict grammar and a bound.** A `seed` is either decimal digit groups separated by single `_`, `[0-9]+(_[0-9]+)*`, or a lowercase `0x` followed by hex digit groups separated by single `_`. Leading zeros are allowed. A seed with more than 20 significant decimal digits, or more than 16 significant hex digits, is `bad-value`, and this is checked **before any conversion**; "significant" means after removing every `_` and the leading zeros. A value of `2^64` or more is `bad-value` as well. This tightens VER04i section 5.1, whose `seed` row says only "`_` allowed"; every existing v1 binding still conforms (R89). Rejected: **relaxing the validator to what the v1 readers accept** (`1__0`, `10_`, `0x_1`: three spellings of nothing a binding needs, each of which a lane's own integer parser may or may not take, which is the disagreement the kit exists to remove); **bounding only the value** (the reference validator as first written converted first, and a decimal seed of 5,000 digits, well inside the 64 KiB cap of section 14, raised an uncaught error instead of `BindingInvalid`). R89 has the detail.

**Open item O2 (not decided here): two dialects claim one file name.** The Rust harness reads `<package>/evidence.bindings` in its own dialect (`rust/warrant-grammar`, `binding.rs`: the directives `package`, `revision`, `spec` and `output`, `struct` and `enum` lines, a bare `fresh`, `faults=` and `static=`), and section 7.1 gives the v2 lane binding the same path. The two are not comparable line for line, and the v2 validator refuses every committed Rust file with `unknown-directive`; the thirteen such files on `main` are untouched and stay valid for the harness. A Rust v2 lane therefore cannot share the file as the two grammars stand. Which of the two moves (a second file name, a merged grammar, or directives each reader ignores in the other's lines) is for the owner **before the Rust lane ports**, and nothing in S5c, S6 or S7 depends on it: the Python, TypeScript and Go lanes have no harness file. Where the two dialects overlap they agree: `error=CASE` is the panic mapping (R81), and `err_any` excludes `error=` and `errors=` (R85).

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R79. The eight one-line directives (section 7.1; question 1).** Each of `lane package module import source seed depth levels` appears **exactly once** and takes exactly one argument. An absent one is `missing-directive`, a second one `duplicate-directive`, a wrong argument count `syntax`, and an argument that is not of its form `bad-value`. Section 7.1 shows `depth` and `levels` without brackets, unlike the optional `op` keys, and all three v1 lane readers already require the other six. **A v1 binding, which has neither `depth` nor `levels`, is not a v2 binding**; the three committed lane files (`python`, `typescript` and `go` `ct-compare`) are accepted once the two lines are added (checked by the review). The forms: `lane` is `[a-z][a-z0-9]*`; `package` is `<lane>/<name>`, where the part before the `/` is the value of the `lane` line (a different lane is `bad-value`) and `<name>` is `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_.-]*`; `module` is `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`; `import` is any one token and is resolved by the lane, not by the validator; `source` is a relative path with no leading `/`, no empty or `..` segment and no `\` or `:`; `seed` is [D69]; `depth` is `full` or `slim`; `levels` is a comma-separated list of level names without a repeat (a repeat is `bad-value`, an empty item `syntax`, the names R80). *The package name.* The reviewed validator allowed `[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]*` only, which refuses package directories that exist: about 200 each in `elixir`, `ruby` and `lua` hold `_` (`elixir/gf256_native`), `swift` has capitals (`swift/Barcode1D`) and `csharp` has dots (`csharp/Mosaic.Flux`). No v1 reader constrains `package`, and the Rust harness's own rule for a package directory name is `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_-]*`. *Vectors (S5c):* one refusal per absent directive; accepted package names with `_`, with capitals and with a dot; a package name that starts with a dot (`bad-value`).
* **R80. Level names and the lattice in a binding (section 4.1, R20; question 2; the detail of [D66]).** A `levels` entry is one of the seven names of section 4.1; any other is `level-unknown`. A level declared without a level it needs is `level-implication`. The needs are checked **directly**, so one missing level is one refusal and not a cascade: `ir.fixed`, `ir.tuple`, `ir.seq` and `ir.inout.records` each need `ir.core`; `ir.data` needs `ir.tuple`; `ir.list` needs `ir.data`. The closure is the lattice of section 4.1 (every level other than `ir.core` needs `ir.core`). For the two reserved levels without content, `ir.seq` and `ir.inout.records`, the kit pins **only** that they need `ir.core`: where `ir.seq` sits is left to its amendment (section 4.1), and a lane must not read more into the table. *What [D66] changes.* The sentence of section 16.18 R68 (a), "no lane declares `ir.list` in its `levels`, because there is nothing to implement", describes what a lane has reason to write and is **not a rule the validator enforces**: a binding that declares a reserved level is valid. The declaration changes nothing a lane runs or records: a suite that needs a reserved level is `not-live` in every lane until the amendment (section 4.1), the list refusals of [D63] stand whatever `levels` says, and no kit case carries a reserved level in its `requires`, so the declaration selects no case in `conformance_run` (section 9.1). Enforcement of "implements nothing" is therefore not the binding validator's; it is what sections 4.1 and 9.3 already require of the runner and of S9. *Vectors (S5c):* all seven names in one `levels` line, in any order, accepted and normalized sorted; an unknown name; `ir.fixed` without `ir.core`; `ir.data` without `ir.tuple`; `ir.list` without `ir.data`; `ir.seq` alone (`level-implication`: it needs `ir.core`).
* **R81. `panics` is the discriminator of `error=CASE` (section 7.5; question 3; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** `error=CASE` is the `panics` mapping: the op's host failure of any kind that the lane calls a panic becomes the one declared case. `panics` is never written as an `errors=` discriminator. `error=` requires an error type with exactly one case, that `CASE` be that case, and a host that has `panics`. In the validator: `panics` in an `errors=` pair, or `error=` on an error type with two or more cases, is `panics-form`; a `CASE` the error type does not declare is `errors-case`; `error=` where the case's `host.discriminators` (R85) does not hold `panics` is `discriminator-unknown`. This matches the Rust harness, where `error=` is the panic-to-error mapping. Section 7.5's "`panics` (only with a single-case `error=`)" already said the first half.
* **R82. `type` lines: `fields=` is positional, `ctors=` is a set of names (section 7.3; question 4; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** A `type` line names a record or a variant of the corpus; any other name, an error type included, is `type-unknown`. It has `host=`, an export name of the lane (one that is not in `host.exports` is `export-unknown`), and exactly one of `fields=` (a record) or `ctors=` (a variant). `fields=` lists **one host field name per corpus field, in the corpus field order**; the names are distinct identifiers and are the host's, so they need not equal the corpus's. `ctors=` lists **exactly the corpus constructor names, in any order**; the name is the portable key (VER04j section 4.9), so the directive carries no information beyond the lane's confirmation that it has every constructor, which is accepted. A missing `host=`, the wrong one of the two keys, a wrong count of fields, a constructor set that is not the corpus's, a repeated name, and an unknown or repeated key are each `type-shape`. Two `type` lines for one name are `duplicate-directive`. A record or variant that an op signature uses, directly or through a field, a payload, a tuple component, an `Option` or a `Result`, and that has no `type` line, is `type-missing`; a `type` line for a declared type that no signature uses is accepted.
* **R83. `fresh=` defaults to `probe`, and what `immutable` requires (sections 7.1 and 7.4; question 5).** On an op the corpus marks `fresh`, an absent `fresh=` means **`probe`**: the runner runs the aliasing probe of section 7.4 rule 3. `fresh=` on any other op, or a value other than `probe` or `immutable`, is `BindingInvalid` (`fresh`). The default is the safe direction, since an absent key never means "trust the export". The consequence: a `fresh` result whose representation cannot be mutated has no probe to run and **must write `fresh=immutable`** (a Python `bytes` result; the v1 Python `ct-compare` binding writes nothing on `select_bytes`, and its v2 form gains the key in S6c). *`immutable`.* It is accepted only if **every host representation that carries the result is in the lane's list of representations immutable by construction** (`host.immutable`, R85). For a `Result` that is the `Ok` type. A value type counts as its `values` representation, or as the `ret=` item where the op line has one (`ret=` replaces the representation of the outermost type only). A record or a variant counts as the host type its `type` line names. A tuple, an `Option` and a list count as the container **and every component**, recursively, because an immutable tuple that holds a mutable byte array can still be written through. A representation the binding does not give is already a refusal of its own (`values-missing`, `type-missing`) and is not judged a second time. The reviewed validator checked the outermost value representation only and nothing for a record or variant result; this reading is the correction. *Vectors (S5c):* `fresh=immutable` on a mutable `Bytes` representation, on a mutable `ret=` override, on a record whose host type is not in the list, and on a tuple with a mutable component (each `fresh`); on a record whose host type is in the list and on a tuple of immutable components (accepted); the default written into `normalized` (R85).
* **R84. What a lane does with a reserved `inout` form (section 7.6; question 6; the detail of [D67]).** Section 7.6 says a **corpus** that uses a record form is `not-live`; it does not say what a **binding** that names one is, and the validator accepts the level name `ir.inout.records` ([D66]) while refusing its forms. Until the amendment that defines the level: (a) the reference behaviour is to refuse the binding, `BindingInvalid` with the reason `inout-form`, and a lane written today should do the same; (b) the kit holds no case for it, so a lane that accepts the line is not caught by the kit, but the rule is (a); (c) **no lane runs a call through a reserved form**, whatever its `levels` line says, because there is no definition to run against. Either way nothing of the level executes. The amendment that defines `ir.inout.records` restates the rule and brings its own cases. A form that is neither one of the four of section 7.6 nor reserved (`inout=buf:inplace`) is `inout-form` in every lane, and that case stays in the kit.
* **R85. The shapes S5c fixes: `host`, `normalized`, the reason set and the order of the checks (section 11, R20; question 7; the reviewer's draft, extended).** R20 delegated these to S5c's committed tables; they are restated here so that a lane can be written from this document. (a) *`corpus`* is an object with exactly the keys `types` and `ops`, read as follows: a type row has `kind` `error` (with `cases`, a list of names), `record` (with `fields`, a list of `[name, type]`) or `variant` (with `ctors`, a list of `[name, [payload types]]`); an op row has `params` and `ret` and may have `width_params`, `inout` and `fresh`, and any other key of an op row (`requires`, `post_slot`) is ignored. It has no module name, so `module` is checked for its form only (R87). (b) *`host`* is an object with exactly four keys, each list holding distinct non-empty names: **`values`**, an object from Warrant value names (`Bool U8 U16 U32 U64 I8 I16 I32 I64 Nat Bytes List Option Tuple`) to the list of host representation names allowed for that name; **`exports`**, the names that exist in the bound module, which serve both `export=` and `type host=`; **`discriminators`**, the error discriminators that exist, with `panics` a member exactly where the host has it; **`immutable`**, the representation names and host type names that are immutable by construction (R83). `Error`, `Exception` and `BaseException` are refused as discriminators (`discriminator-bare`) **even when the list holds them**. A lane's own validator reads the same four lists from its runtime. (c) *`normalized`*, the value of an accepted case, is an object with exactly eleven keys: `lane`, `package`, `module`, `import`, `source` and `depth` as written; `seed` as **decimal text without leading zeros** (`"0"` for zero); `levels` as a list **sorted by code point**; `values`, an object from each Warrant value name written to its host name (`{}` with no `values` line); `types`, an object from each `type` line's name to `{"host":H,"fields":[..]}` (the host field names as written) or `{"host":H,"ctors":[..]}` (the constructor names **in corpus order**, whatever order the line used); and `ops`, an object from each op name to an object with `export` and, **only where the line writes them**, `args` (a list), `ret`, `width`, one of `errors` (an object from case to discriminator), `error` or `err_any` (the case name), and `inout` (an object from parameter to form). The one key present without being written is **`fresh`**, which is `"probe"` on every op the corpus marks `fresh` whose line says nothing (R83). Comments, blank lines and the order of lines, of `levels` entries and of the keys of an `op` line do not survive. A lane compares its normalized binding with `expect.normalized` **by equality**, not by containment, and the kit builder compares the hand-written form with the derived one in the same way. (d) *The reason set* is closed at 39, listed here by the phase of (e) that first reports each: `too-large invalid-utf8 bom cr control-character` (the bytes); `syntax unknown-directive duplicate-directive bad-value op-key` (the lines); `missing-directive level-unknown level-implication values-name values-representation values-missing type-unknown type-shape type-missing op-unknown op-missing export-missing export-unknown args ret width errors-form errors-missing errors-unexpected errors-case errors-incomplete discriminator-shared discriminator-bare discriminator-unknown err-any-multi panics-form inout-param inout-form fresh` (the model; two reasons are reported in two phases: `type-shape` for an unknown or repeated key of a `type` line is a line-phase refusal, and `bad-value` for a `package` whose lane is not the `lane` line's is a model-phase one). The one-line definition of each is the table of the `warrant-oracle` README, which a test holds equal to the validator's `REASONS`; that table is the normative definition (R20). (e) *The order.* The checks run in three phases and a refusal of an earlier phase wins. **The bytes**, in this order, the first one found: over 65,536 bytes (`too-large`; exactly 65,536 is read), not UTF-8, a BOM, a CR, another C0 control or DEL. **The lines**: every line is read for its form (R88), its directive name, a repeated directive, the argument forms of R79, and an unknown or repeated key of a `type` line (`type-shape`) or an `op` line (`op-key`). **The model**: everything that needs the whole file, the `corpus` or the `host`. Every reject case of the kit breaks exactly one rule within its phase, so a lane need not reproduce the order inside a phase. The phase order **is** observable: a case that drops or damages a line leaves rules of the model phase unmet (the absent directive, `values-missing`, `op-missing`, `type-missing`), and the expected reason is the line-phase one. (f) *One reason per kind of list.* An empty item in a comma-separated list is reported under the reason of that list's key and not under one common reason: `syntax` for `levels`, `type-shape` for `fields=` and `ctors=`, `errors-case` for `errors=`, `args` for `args=` and `inout-param` for `inout=`. A key with no value at all (`fields=`) is no `key=value` token and is `syntax`. (g) *The error keys* (section 7.5). At most one of `errors=`, `error=` and `err_any=` on one op (`errors-form`); one of them exactly when the op's result is a `Result` (`errors-missing`, `errors-unexpected`); `errors=` is `CASE:discriminator` pairs split at the first `:`, maps every declared case once (`errors-case` for an undeclared or repeated case or a malformed pair, `errors-incomplete` for an unmapped one), no two cases to one discriminator (`discriminator-shared`), each discriminator a member of `host.discriminators` (`discriminator-unknown`); `err_any=` on an error type with two or more cases is `err-any-multi`, and a `CASE` of `error=` or `err_any=` that is not the declared case is `errors-case`. (h) *`args=` and `ret=`* are optional per-op representations: `args=` has one item per parameter, each a member of `host.values` for that parameter's value name, or the `type` line's `host` for a record or variant (`args`); `ret=` likewise for the result, a `Result` being judged by its `Ok` type (`ret`). An `op` line for an op the corpus lacks is `op-unknown`, a corpus op without a line `op-missing` (the binding is total, VER04i section 5 rule 1), a line without `export=` `export-missing`, an `export=` outside `host.exports` `export-unknown`.
* **R86. What `["ir.core"]` on every binding case asks of a lane (section 11; question 8; the detail of [D68]).** A `v2-binding` case is `["ir.core"]` whatever types its `corpus` names, and the `requires` an op row of that `corpus` carries is not the case's. So the binding validator of **every** lane, from S6b and S7b on, parses and checks `type` lines, `Tuple=`, `Option=` and `List=` entries and record and variant declarations, although the lane's evaluator may implement none of those levels: the validator needs the names and the shapes of the declarations and never a value. This is the reading R24 of section 16.13 already takes for tags (a runner knows the tag names of every level), applied to the binding grammar, and it is the only place where a lane at `ir.core` reads a record or variant declaration for more than its level (section 16.18 R69 has it classify them otherwise). "Every S5 case is `ir.core`" (section 16.13) stays true. Roughly 35 of the cases use a corpus with a record, a variant and a tuple; changing their `requires` later would re-pin every lane, which is why the owner settled it now.
* **R87. Which binding vectors the kit does not carry, and which S5c adds (section 11; question 9).** *Correctly not in the kit:* the four aliasing fixtures (S6 and S7 runner fixtures, per lane, as the disposition table of section 11 has it; `v2-binding` carries only the directive-level cases, which are the `fresh` and `inout` rules of R83 and R92); and "the binding's `module` is the corpus's module", because the case's `corpus` has only `types` and `ops` (R85 (a), [D41]), so that check is each lane's own test. *Added by S5c after the review:* the tuple-component rule of section 7.3 (a component type with no host mapping: `values` without `Bool` against a signature `(Bytes,Bool)`, `values-missing`); the long seed of [D69] (R89); the package names of R79; the line-form cases of R88; the fit of `growable` (R92); and the `fresh=immutable` cases of R83. Every value name a signature needs must have a `values` entry, where "needs" is recursive through tuple components, `Option` and list elements, the `Ok` type of a `Result`, and the fields and payloads of the records and variants the signatures use; `Bytes<N>` needs `Bytes`.
* **R88. Whitespace and line endings are strict (section 7.1, VER04i section 5.1; question 10; the reviewer's draft, extended).** The file is UTF-8 without a BOM, **LF-separated**, with no CR, no other C0 control and no DEL anywhere, a tab included. A line is empty, a comment, or a directive line. A **comment** is a line whose **first character** is `#`; it may hold any other UTF-8 text, and a `#` anywhere else is an ordinary character. A **directive line** is tokens of U+0021 to U+007E separated by **exactly one space**: no leading or trailing space, no run of spaces, no non-ASCII character. The final line needs no LF after it. **Only LF ends a line**: U+2028, U+2029 and U+0085 inside a comment are part of the comment (the C1 controls are not refused), so a reader that splits lines with a Unicode-aware routine (Python's `str.splitlines`, which the v1 Python reader uses) would read text inside a comment as a directive and is wrong. All three v1 lane readers and the Rust harness parser accept CRLF, tabs, runs of spaces, leading and trailing space and indented comments, and the TypeScript reader strips a BOM silently; none of that is a v2 binding. The stricter form is safe in this repository (`* text=auto eol=lf`) and follows the "data-only, canonical" intent of section 7.1: one byte form per binding, so the same file is the same binding in eighteen readers. In the validator a BOM is `bom`, a CR `cr`, another control `control-character`, and a malformed directive line (a space run, a leading or trailing space, an indented comment, a non-ASCII character outside a comment) `syntax`. *Vectors (S5c):* a CR at a line end and inside a comment, a BOM, invalid UTF-8, a tab, a space run, leading and trailing spaces, an indented comment and a non-ASCII character in a directive (each refused); a comment with non-ASCII text, a `#` in the middle of a directive line read as an ordinary character, a comment holding U+2028 followed by the text of an `op` line, the same with U+0085, and a file without a final LF (each accepted).
* **R89. The seed (VER04i section 5.1; question 11; the detail of [D69]).** (a) *Grammar.* `[0-9]+(_[0-9]+)*`, or `0x` and then `[0-9a-fA-F]+(_[0-9a-fA-F]+)*`. The prefix is lowercase only (`0X10` is refused); **the hex digits may be of either case**, which is the author's reading of "hex digit groups" and what the validator and the accepted case `0xFFFF_ffff_FFFF_ffff` do. An `_` stands only between two digits, so `1__0`, `10_`, `_1`, `0x_1` and `0x` are refused, as is a sign. (b) *Bound, then value.* Remove every `_`; for the hex form remove the prefix; remove the leading zeros. More than 20 remaining decimal digits, or more than 16 remaining hex digits, is `bad-value` **without converting the text**. Otherwise the value is the integer of the remaining digits (zero if none remain) and a value of `2^64` or more is `bad-value` (a decimal seed of 20 digits can still be too large; a hex seed of 16 digits cannot). Leading zeros mean nothing however many there are, so a 40-zero decimal seed ending in `7` is the seed 7. Every refusal of the `seed` argument is the one reason `bad-value`. (c) *Why before conversion.* The bound of 20 digits is the "decimal integer text" bound of section 14, applied to the binding and counted after leading zeros are removed (the kit accepts a 41-digit seed with leading zeros). A reader in a language with arbitrary-precision integers otherwise parses whatever a 64 KiB file can hold, and the reference validator did: Python refuses to convert a decimal string of more than 4,300 digits and raises, so a 5,000-digit seed ended the validator with an uncaught error and not with `BindingInvalid`. The v1 Python reader has the same defect and is corrected when it becomes the v2 reader (S6b). (d) *Normalized* as decimal text without leading zeros (R85 (c)), which is the `evidence_seed` form of VER04i section 8. (e) *Every existing v1 binding still conforms.* Checked against `main` at `3d44ca7e8a0`: the 16 committed `evidence.bindings` files (the three lane bindings of `ct-compare` in `python`, `typescript` and `go`, and the 13 Rust harness files, whose dialect is otherwise O2's) each hold exactly one `seed` line, and each is `0x` followed by four groups of four lowercase hex digits separated by single `_`: 16 significant digits at most, inside the bound. (f) *VER04i is not edited by this change.* Its section 5.1 row "a `u64` (decimal or `0x` hex, `_` allowed)" is read with this grammar for a v2 binding; the v1 readers keep what they accept until they are replaced, and the sentence is corrected in VER04i with follow-up F5 of section 16 at the latest. *Vectors (S5c):* the decimal maximum `18446744073709551615` and a hex seed with separators and mixed case (accepted, both normalizing to the same text); leading zeros in each form (accepted); `2^64` in each form, `0x`, `1__0`, `10_`, `-1`, `0X10` and a 5,000-digit decimal seed (each `bad-value`).
* **R90. `values` appears at most once (section 7.1; question 12; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** A second `values` line is `duplicate-directive`; the v1 readers merge several lines, and a v2 reader does not. A `values` line has at least one `Name=host` pair (none is `syntax`). `Name` is one of the fourteen Warrant value names of R85 (b) and is written once (`values-name` otherwise); `host` is a member of that name's list in `host.values` (`values-representation`). The line itself is optional: a corpus whose signatures need no value name is bound without one, and what the signatures need is R87 (`values-missing`). An entry that no signature needs is accepted if it is valid. Likewise there is one `op` line per op and one `type` line per type; a second is `duplicate-directive`.
* **R91. `width=` is written exactly when the op has `width_params` (section 7.1, VER04i section 5 rule 3; question 13; the reviewer's draft, confirmed).** `width=implicit` or `width=runtime` is **required** on an op whose corpus row has `width_params` and **refused** on any other; an absent, an unexpected and an unknown value are all the reason `width`. `static` and `none` are not v2 values: VER04i rule 3 names `static` for Rust and C++, which in the Rust harness dialect is the key `static=` (O2), and `width=none`, which the v1 lane readers accept on an op without widths, is no longer written. This is the binding-side twin of section 16.18 R70, where the fourth element of `subject_op` is present exactly when the op has `width_params`.
* **R92. An `inout` form must fit its parameter (section 7.6; question 14).** `inout=` is `param:form` pairs split at the first `:`. It names **every** `inout` parameter of the corpus op exactly once and no other parameter: a parameter that is not `inout`, one named twice, a malformed pair, and an `inout` parameter left without a form (an absent `inout=` on such an op included) are each `inout-param`. The form is one of the four of section 7.6 and fits the parameter's type: **`mutable` and `growable` are forms of `Bytes` and `Bytes<N>` only; `cell` is a form of `Bool`, `Nat` and the fixed-width integers only; `returned` fits every type.** An unknown form and a form that does not fit are `inout-form`; the reserved record forms are [D67] and R84. The fit follows the table of section 7.6, whose rows describe `mutable` and `growable` as byte sequences and `cell` as a holder "for languages with no reference to an integer or boolean". A tuple, record or variant `inout` parameter therefore binds only as `returned` until `ir.inout.records` is defined. *Vectors (S5c):* `mutable` and `cell` (accepted), `growable` and `returned` (accepted), `cell` for `Bytes`, `mutable` for an integer and `growable` for an integer (each `inout-form`), an unknown form, and the `inout-param` cases above.
* **R93. Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is S5c-0. It lands **before S5c merges**, because R20 makes S5c's tables normative at that merge and not when S5d commits `v2-binding.json`; R50 of section 16.15 listed the S5 PRs without it. S5c carries the review's corrections with it: the seed bound of [D69], the package name of R79, the `immutable` rule of R83, hand-written `normalized` forms compared by equality (R85 (c)), the dropped case of [D67] and the added vectors of R87. S5d is unchanged except that the reason set it checks is the 39 of R85 (d), and its checklist gains one line: the "not committed yet" branch of `make_conformance.py`, which lets `--check` pass while `v2-binding.json` is absent, is removed in S5d, so that a missing file and a missing index row together can never pass. No lane has a committed v2 manifest and `v2-binding.json` is not committed, so [D62] does not apply. The VER-BACKLOG rows for S5c-0 and for O2 are added by the separate docs PR, as for S5-0.

**Correction to section 16.18 R70 (from the re-review of S6a; an author reading, not an owner decision).**

* **R94. A fault expression has a common type with its parameter or result type; it need not flow to it (sections 8.1 and 8.2; section 16.18 R70).** R70 has a lane type a fault row with each named width left unstated and then check "each rewrite against that type". Read as *flows to* (section 16.16 R51), that is too strict, and it refuses a committed row. **The rule:** the static type of a `pre` rewrite and the type of the parameter it rewrites, and the static type of a `post` or `replace` expression and the op's result type, must **unify** (R51's `common_type`): equal types; `Nat` and `Int`; two `Bytes` types of different or forgotten width; and `Option` and `Result` of types that unify, with the same error type. A pair with no common type (a `Bool` for a `Bytes` parameter, a `Bytes` for a `U8` result) is a corpus error, as before. The one relation covers both directions that matter here: a `Bytes` expression against a `Bytes<N>` position, and an `Int` expression against a `Nat` position. *Why not flow.* A fault may change a length by design. The normative `drop-last-result-byte` template of section 8.2, `["if", GT, ["take", R, ["sub", ["len", R], N1]], B00]`, has the type plain `Bytes` on every bytes result: `take` gives plain `Bytes` (R51, static width), and the branches `Bytes` and `Bytes<1>` unify to `Bytes`. Plain `Bytes` does not flow to `Bytes<32>`. The committed x25519 corpus (`code/specs/fixtures/x25519-warrant-v2/corpus.json` on `main` at `3d44ca7e8a0`) has exactly this row: `clamp_scalar` has `"ret": "Bytes<32>"` with a literal width, and its `drop-last-result-byte` fault is the template above, with a live control and five required claims. A lane that demands a flow refuses that corpus; a lane that asks for a common type accepts it, since `Bytes` and `Bytes<32>` unify to `Bytes`. The same holds for the `Ok` forms: the `flip-result-bit` rows on `x25519` and `x25519_base` have the type `Result<Bytes,X25519Error>` against a declared `Result<Bytes<32>,X25519Error>`. R70's own example was not affected (there the parameter is itself plain `Bytes` in a lane), which is why the sentence looked right. *Named widths.* R51 does not unify a literal width with a named static width. That exception cannot arise in a lane, which reads a named width as plain `Bytes` for a fault row (R70), nor in the oracle, which substitutes the suite's literal widths. *What the oracle does.* Neither of its two paths compares the two types at all. The witness search (`_compile_fault`, `faults.py`) types the expression in an environment of the op's parameters and `result` at the suite's widths and uses whatever type comes out; the kit derivation (`derive_fault`, `conformance.py`) compiles the row the same way with no widths, and `v2-fault` has no typing error among its codes (`level-missing` and `subject-forbidden` only, section 11). So the oracle accepts every row a lane accepts under this rule, and also a row with no common type, which the generator's templates never write. That leniency is recorded under **CV2-F17** with the others of section 16.18 R78; it moves no committed byte. This is the "one rule" R70 left for S5e to pin or correct, for the lane side. *The value.* The rule is static. It says nothing new about a faulted value that is not a value of the declared type (the 31 bytes a dropped byte leaves of a `Bytes<32>` result): a `post` or `replace` result goes to the claims as it evaluates, which is the point of the fault, and the lane neither pads nor refuses it. The kit cannot hold such a case today, because the derivation requires its output to read back as a value of the op's declared type. *Vector (S5e, `v2-fault`):* a `post` row over an op whose result is `Bytes<2>` with the expression `["take", R, N2]` (`N2` the `Nat` literal 2): its type is plain `Bytes`, the row is accepted and the result is returned unchanged. A lane that demands a flow refuses the case, and the output is still a value of `Bytes<2>`, so the kit can derive it. **Amended by section 16.20:** tuples (R96), undetermined components (R97) and the branches of an `if` or a `match` (R98). From S11c-1 the oracle compares the two types on both of its paths, so *What the oracle does* above describes the oracle before S11c-1. **Amended by section 16.22 ([D74]):** the sentence above that `v2-fault` has no typing error among its codes is no longer true: `ill-typed` is one (section 11), and a row with no common type is that code (R110).

**Not closed by this section.** Open item O2: which file a Rust v2 lane's binding lives in, for the owner before the Rust lane ports. What a binding that names a reserved `inout` form is once `ir.inout.records` is defined, and the requirements of `ir.seq` and `ir.inout.records` beyond `ir.core`, each of which is its amendment's. The text of VER04i section 5.1 and rule 3 (the `seed` row, `width=static` and `width=none`), which this change reads but does not edit (R89 (f), R91). What a lane does with a `pre` rewrite whose value is not a value of the parameter's declared type, which no committed row produces and R94 does not settle.

### 16.20 Owner decisions of 2026-11-20 (S5e and S11c-1 reviews)

Slice S11c-1-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what two reviews needed from this document: that of S5e (the `ir.core` kit vectors of section 16.18, merged as #19755 at `53c99f6da1e`) and that of S11c-1 (the oracle's `ir.data` evaluation, section 13; its branch is not on `main`). Two items needed the owner: [D70] and [D71]. The others are the author's readings **R95** to **R104** (erratum candidates, not rulings). R95 and R96 began as the S11c-1 reviewer's drafts, and R96's vector and R99's count differ from what the reviewer proposed (each says why). Each reading was checked against the oracle on `main` at `53c99f6da1e` (`conformance.py`, `domains.py`, `ir_eval.py`, the committed `scalar-faults-v1` dumps and the package's `BUILD`), against the S11c-1 branch as reviewed at `a7197d9755b` with the fixes the review asked for, which were being made while this was written (`faults.py`, `tfv.py`, `domains.py`), so where the reviewed code and this section differ the section states what the oracle does after those fixes, and against the generator's `tests/scalar_faults_parity.rs` and `tfv.rs`, the TFV reader (`rust/test-bootstrap`, `tfv.rs`) and the v1 generator's `EvalValue::container_depth` (`rust/warrant-grammar`, `codegen.rs`) on `main`. S6a merged while this was in review (#19762, `2d6320d2e4a`); what R100 says of the lane was read from its kit harness on `main` (`v2/ir.py` and `v2/kit.py` of `python/warrant-corpus`). Sections 2.5, 5.1, 13 and 14 were amended in the same change, and R31 of section 16.14, R78 of section 16.18 and R94 of section 16.19 gained pointers. This erratum moves no committed byte; [D71] has S11c-1 remove one row of one committed parity dump, and nothing else moves.

**[D70] A kit slice ships as one PR (a standing exception).** A slice that adds or changes cases of the v2 categories of `warrant-corpus-conformance-v1` ships the oracle's case tables, with the derivation code those tables need, and the regenerated kit (`v2-*.json`, `index.json`, the README's disposition rows) **together, in one PR**. This is a **standing, listed exception** to "no single PR carries more than one of {generator, oracle, fixture, one lane runner, one adopter}" (section 13, "Order and size"), next to [D62]. It covers **S5e** (which landed so, #19755), **S10d** and **S11d**. Why: since S5b the oracle's `BUILD` runs `make_conformance.py --check` against the committed kit, which rebuilds the six files from the tables and requires the committed bytes to be identical, so an oracle PR that adds a table row without the regenerated files is red, and so is a fixture PR without the rows. The two cannot be split. What the exception covers beyond those words is the author's reading in R104.

**[D71] S11c-1 regenerates `scalar-faults-v1` and the oracle keeps one rule.** S11c-1 removes the row `missing-error/an-option-payload` from `scalar_faults_v1.oracle-refusals.json` and regenerates the dump (**53 rows become 52**), because [D50] gives that payload a template from S11c-1. It removes the oracle's data-blind call, so that `fault_row` has **one rule**. It changes the row count in the generator's `tests/scalar_faults_parity.rs` from 53 to 52 **in the same PR**, the one cross-package edit of S11 and a listed one-time exception to "Order and size". The row `missing-error/a-variant-payload` keeps its bytes. Why: the oracle as first written for S11c-1 refused an `Option` payload whenever it was called without the document's records, only so that the committed row stayed true; the dump then said something false about the shipped oracle, and S11c-2 could not reproduce both that dump and `data-eval-v1` with one `instantiate` unless it copied the second mode. The details are R95.

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R95. `scalar-faults-v1` pinned a refusal that [D50] ends (the detail of [D71]; a correction to section 16.14 R31 (c); the reviewer's draft, without its alternative).** R31 (c) said no committed refusals dump of the oracle names `missing-error`. That was true when written; S11f-1 then committed `missing-error/an-option-payload` (`Result<Option<U8>,E>`) and `missing-error/a-variant-payload` (`Result<K,E>` with no parameter of type `K`) in `scalar-faults-v1`. From S11c-1 the first has a template (`["none","U8"]`, section 8.2), so the row is false. (a) *The dump.* S11c-1 removes the row from `make_scalar_faults.py` and regenerates; 52 rows remain and no other generated file's byte moves (the script and the directory's README change with it). This is the precedent of section 16.14 finding (5), where S11a regenerated the `tuple-forms-v1` row that it made false. The "new directory" trap of section 13 is about **adding** a dump, and it stands. R37's "a moved byte is a blocker" names the four corpus fixtures' `--check` lines and the `verify-v2` lines, not a parity dump: a parity refusal row that a slice makes false is regenerated by that slice. (b) *The generator's test.* `tests/scalar_faults_parity.rs` asserts the row count and that the generator refuses every row with the dump's message. Only the count changes (53 to 52, and its comment). The generator still refuses an `Option` payload until S11c-2, and with the row gone no assertion mentions the shape, so the test is green on both sides of S11c-2. (c) *`a-variant-payload` keeps its bytes.* A variant leaf with no parameter of exactly its type has no template (R31 (d)), **in every position**: as the payload, as a tuple component and as a record field. The reason is the same text everywhere, `missing-error has no template for this payload type`; `data-eval-v1` does not pin a second wording for the same refusal. (d) *One rule.* `fault_row` is always given the document's records and variants, by the witness search, by `make_scalar_faults.py` and by the kit's `instantiate_fault` alike. A document that declares none gives empty tables, which is not a second mode: `field-zero` then has no record to name, and `build` meets only tuples and scalars. (e) *A nested composite is built.* A tuple component or a record field that is itself a tuple or a record is built in turn, as `build` of section 8.2 recurses and as R31 (a) says a composite is always built. The pre-S11c refusal `a component of this type has no zero literal` is therefore unreachable for a tuple and for a record; no committed dump pins it for either. **S11c-2** makes the generator's `faults.rs` build a nested tuple and accept the `Option` payload, with `data-eval-v1` as its parity target.
* **R96. R94 and tuples (sections 8.1 and 8.2; the reviewer's draft, with its vector changed).** R94's relation has no tuple rule, and R51 makes a tuple invariant. Read together they refuse the normative `off-by-one-consumed` template (section 8.2: a `(T1,Int,..)` expression against a declared `(T1,Nat,..)`) and the committed wasm-leb128 row. **Added to R94's relation, for a fault row only:** two tuple types of one arity have a common type when their components do, pairwise, under this **narrower** relation: equal types; `Nat` and `Int` (to `Int`); two `Option` types, and two `Result` types, whose components are related the same way, a `Result` with the same error type or an undetermined one (R97); and a nested tuple likewise. **Nothing else lifts in a component.** Two `Bytes` types of different or forgotten width do not unify there, which section 16.17 R67's second test needs (a one-byte literal in a `Bytes<2>` component is refused), and records and variants are nominal. The narrower relation holds at every depth below a tuple: an `Option<Bytes<1>>` component does not unify with an `Option<Bytes<3>>` one, although the two unify at the top level by R94. R51 is unchanged for source and for every position that is not a fault row. The value is unchanged too: the widened component is a `Nat` value (section 8.2). No registry template changes the length of a bytes component (`drop-consumed`, `swap-components`, `off-by-one-consumed` and `missing-error` all keep component types), and a future template that does needs its own erratum. *Checked.* `_fault_component` and `_fault_unify` (`faults.py`, S11c-1) are this relation, and the kit's derivation calls the same function: S5e's `_require_common_types` (`conformance.py`), which uses R51's `unify` and so would refuse the `off-by-one-consumed` row, is deleted by S11c-1. *Vector (S10d, `v2-fault`):* `off-by-one-consumed` on an op whose result is `Result<(Bytes,Nat),E>`, accepted, with the derived result. The reviewer's second vector, the R67 literal refused in a `(Bytes<2>,Bool)` component, **cannot be a kit case**: `v2-fault` has no typing code (section 11; R94), so a row with no common type fails the kit build. It stays an oracle test and a `typing` row of `data-eval-v1` (R98) until the owner adds a code. **Amended by section 16.22 ([D74]):** the owner has added it, `ill-typed`; the second vector is a `v2-fault` case with that code (R110 (d)).
* **R97. An undetermined component unifies with whatever stands opposite it (R94; section 16.15 R41; question 3 of the S5e review).** A bare `ok` has an undetermined error type and a bare `err` an undetermined payload (R41). Under R94, and inside a tuple component under R96, such a component has a common type with **any** type in the opposite position, and the common type takes the determined side. A declared type never has one, so the common type of a fault expression and its declared type never has one either. This is unification and not "flows to": a bare `["ok", e]` with `e` of type plain `Bytes` has a common type with a declared `Result<Bytes<32>,E>`. S5e's kit derivation used the oracle's tolerant assignability for exactly this case, which is stricter than R94 (it refuses that example); S11c-1 replaces it with the one relation of R96. S11c-1's `make_conformance.py --check` line shows that no committed `v2-fault` case depends on the difference.
* **R98. Every branch of a fault expression is compared, and the `type` a typing row states (R94; findings 2 and 3 of the S11c-1 review).** (a) *Branches.* **Each** branch of an `if` and each arm of a `match` must have a common type with the declared type of its position (R94 with R96 and R97), **in every position to which R67 hands the declared type down**: the expression itself, a branch or an arm of such an `if` or `match`, the payload of an `ok` or a `some`, a tuple component and a `ctor` argument. So a tuple component `if .. then project(R, 0) else ok(true)` against a declared component `Result<U8,E>` is refused. Comparing the type of the whole expression is not enough when the typer builds that type from one branch. The oracle's IR typer merges two `Result` branches by keeping the more determined one and never compares their payloads (`_merge_result`, `ir_eval.py`), so `["if", ["is_err", R], R, ["ok", ["lit", true]]]` was accepted for a result `Result<U8,E>` (probed by the review), as was a wrong second `match` arm. **S11c-1** compares each branch as it compiles a fault row, wherever the declared type is handed down. An `if` or a `match` in a condition, count or operand position is typed by R51 alone. The same merge types a `v2-ir` tree; whether the kit's static pass refuses two `Result` branches whose payloads have no common type was not checked here and is under **CV2-F17**. (b) *The `type` of a typing row.* In the `typing` rows of `data_eval_v1.oracle-refusals.json`, the `type` of an accepted row is the **common type** of the expression and the declared type, with every undetermined component filled in: for the `missing-error` template, whose `["ok", P]` arm has no error type, it is `Result<(Bytes,Bool),E>` with the `E` of the other arm. It is never the oracle's placeholder `?`, which is an internal spelling and appears in no committed file and in no type text of this document (R41 already has the kit refuse it in `expect.type`).
* **R99. TFV depth (section 2.5, section 14; VER04j section 4.9; question 5 of the S11c-1 review).** (a) *The rule.* A writer of `cases.tfv` counts the containers open around each value it writes inside **one field value**: a tuple or a record is one (`L<n>`), an `Option` one (`L0` as well as `L1`), a `Result` one (`M1`, for `Ok` and for `Err`), and a variant **two** (`L2` and the `L<k>` of its fields). The outermost container of a field value is depth 1. A value that would open a container at depth 65 is refused, oracle code `limit` and generator code `limit` (the `W0709` of VER04j section 4.9), before anything is written. (b) *The line's `R<n>` does not count.* The limit is TFV's own (TF01 section 2.2): "lists and maps nest at most 64 deep: the outermost container is depth 1". `R<n>` is the record header, not a list or a map. The reader starts every field value at depth 0 (`parse_value` in `tfv.rs` of `rust/test-bootstrap`, whose test accepts 64 nested `L1` and refuses 65), and the v1 generator counts a value's own containers and refuses more than 64 (`container_depth` and `encode_checked`, VER04j section 12 item 19). The S11c-1 review suggested counting `R` too. That is not adopted: it would refuse a file that every TFV reader accepts and that the v1 generator writes, and would make the two profiles differ by one. (c) *Why type depth does not bound it.* Section 2.2 rule 1 bounds a type **expression** at 16 enclosing constructors, and a name is depth 0 whatever its declaration holds. Named types chain: a record whose field is a record, a variant whose constructor holds a variant, with no recursion and no wrapper in any type text. Each link adds one container, or two for a variant. The review's probe wrote a 40-link variant chain in an example result, 80 containers around the innermost field, and the oracle accepted it. (d) *Effect.* **S11c-1** adds the count to the oracle's writer and **S11c-2** to the generator's `tfv_value`, which has none. No committed byte moves: no committed example nests that deep. There is no kit vector, because no category holds TFV; each tool tests 64 and 65 containers, for a chain of records and for one of variants.
* **R100. A stand-in counts for `requires` and never causes `level-missing` (section 11; section 16.18 R69 (a) and (g); question 1 of the S5e review).** `subjects` binds an op of a `v2-ir` case to a pure model, and a lane runs that model when the op is called (section 11). R69 (a) names the call closure of `ir` and is silent on the stand-in. **The reading:** a stand-in that `subjects` binds to an op which `ir` reaches is **reached for `requires`**: the levels of its body's tags and of the models it calls are added to the case's `requires`. Its signature adds nothing, being the op's with each named width unstated (R70). A lane cannot run the case without compiling that model, and `requires` is the rule by which a lane skips. A stand-in bound to an op that `ir` does not reach is never run and counts for nothing. **A stand-in never causes `level-missing`.** It is the kit's machinery and stands in no corpus, so a refusal on its account would pin nothing about what a lane does with a corpus; `level-missing` is decided over the closure of `ir` alone, as R69 (g) has it. The two halves meet in one build rule: **a kit row whose stand-in needs a level outside the case's `levels` fails the kit build.** *The tools.* A lane does not compute `requires`; it reads it and skips by it. S6a's kit harness (`SubjectTable`, `v2/ir.py`) compiles the stand-in of every op the tree reaches, at the case's `levels`, and a refusal of that model, `level-missing` included, is a **kit error** and never the case's outcome; a stand-in bound to an op the tree does not reach is ignored. So the lane already needs the stand-in's levels to run the case and already never reports `level-missing` for one, which is this reading. The S5e oracle does neither half: `reach` (`conformance.py`) does not follow a stand-in, so `requires_of` omits its levels and `make_conformance.py` accepts such a row. **Queued for the oracle:** both changes, in or before S10d's PR. Whether a committed case's `requires` moves was not checked here; if one does it is a correction. [D62] does not apply to it yet: S6a regenerated the Python lane's **v1** manifest only (`python/ct-compare/evidence/test-manifest.json` on `main` has the schema `warrant-lane-evidence-v1`), and no v2 manifest is committed before S6b. Once one is, such a correction regenerates it under [D62]. *Vector (S10d, `v2-ir`):* at `levels` `["ir.core","ir.fixed"]`, a case whose `ir` holds `ir.core` tags only and whose stand-in body holds an `ir.fixed` tag: a value, with `requires` `["ir.core","ir.fixed"]`.
* **R101. The kit build refuses a row whose outcome depends on a model no lane at its `levels` compiles (section 16.18 R69 (c); finding S4 of the S5e review).** By tier 2 of R69 (c) a lane neither types nor costs a model that needs a level it lacks. The oracle's derivation compiles **every** model of a case (`_models`, `conformance.py`). So for a model that `ir` does not reach and that needs a level outside the case's `levels`, the oracle can derive `ill-typed` or `cost` where a lane at those `levels` returns the value. **The reading:** such a row is not a case. The kit build refuses a row whose derived outcome would differ if the models that need a level outside `levels` were left uncompiled. In practice a row may hold such a model only if it types and stays inside the cost bound. The checks that read tag names alone (`unknown-tag`, `subject-forbidden`, `model-order`, the expression depth and the chain depth of R77) still apply to that model and may be the row's outcome, as tier 2 says. **Queued for the oracle:** `make_conformance.py` enforces this, with R100's change. Whether a committed row breaks it was not checked here; if one does, that PR removes or corrects it. **Amended by section 16.23 R124:** a row for which the oracle derives `level-missing` from an unreached model whose types or tags need a missing level is such a row too; the lanes' value is right, the fix is in the oracle (`_models`), and until it lands the build refusal raises a kit error for the row.
* **R102.
