The product bound and the chunk bound cannot be told apart in the kit (section 16.18 R75; question 2 of the S5e review; a note).** R75's vector "a product over `2^28`" is `corpus` in `v2-chunk`. `chunk_size` is at most `2^16` (section 14), so a product over `2^28` has more than `2^28 / 2^16 = 4096` chunks at every legal `chunk_size`, which is over `MAX_CHUNKS` as well. A consumer that checks either bound first gives `corpus`, the kit pins the code and not the reason, and no vector can separate the two. This is harmless, and the vector stands as it is.
* **R103. `ctors` and the arity of `of` (section 5.1, the `descriptor` row of section 11; question 4 of the S11c-1 review; an extension of CV2-F17, in the manner of R72).** (a) *`ctors`* is the constructor list of the binder's variant type: **2 to 64** entries, each `[name, [descriptor, ..]]` with `name` a JSON string matching the name grammar (section 2.2 rule 4), names **without duplicates**, in declaration order, and **0 to 8** field descriptors per constructor. When a `types` table is present, the names, their order and each field count equal the declared constructors. (b) *`of` of a `tuple` descriptor* has **1 to 32** entries, the record bound of section 2.2 rule 3, and exactly the component or field count of the binder type: 2 to 8 for a tuple type (section 14). Anything else is `descriptor`. The bounds are those of section 2.2 rule 3, which a consumer of a corpus enforces whatever a source document could reach (R56). **The oracle is too lenient today:** `descriptor_size` (`domains.py`) requires of a `variant` only a non-empty list of two-element entries whose second element is a list, so it accepts one constructor, more than 64, a name that is not a string or is outside the grammar, a repeated name and more than 8 fields; of a `tuple` it requires only a non-empty list. No committed byte is wrong: every row of the consumer table of the `data-eval-v1` domains dump is right under this reading (the review's check). The fix is under **CV2-F17** and lands **before S11d derives the `v2-domain` variant vectors**, in S11d's PR [D70] or earlier. *Vectors (S11d, `v2-domain`), each `descriptor`:* a `variant` with one constructor, with 65, with a repeated name, with a name outside the grammar, with a name that is not a string, and with a constructor of 9 fields; a `tuple` whose `of` is empty, and one with 33 entries.
* **R104. Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is S11c-1-0. It lands **before S11c-1 merges**, and S11c-1 now depends on it (section 13). S11c-1 carries what the review asked for: the regenerated dump, the one rule and the generator's count ([D71], R95), the tuple relation and the per-branch comparison (R96 to R98), the `type` of a typing row (R98 (b)) and the TFV count (R99). [D70] amends the rows of S5e, S10d and S11d and R78's "two PRs". **The reach of [D70] (author refinements; the owner said only that kit slices ship the oracle tables and the regenerated kit in one PR, as a standing listed exception):** it covers every later slice of the same kind, the `ir.list` fixture PR among them; such a PR carries the oracle and the kit and nothing else (no generator, no lane runner, no adopter); and when it corrects a case that a committed v2 manifest pins, [D62] applies to it as well. **Every file a generator slice must match is byte parity, the `typing` rows included** (an author reading, settled in the review of this erratum): S11c-2 reproduces `data_eval_v1.oracle-refusals.json` whole, so it types fault rows under R67 and under R94 with R96 to R98. That is a scope increase over section 16.17 R67, which had S11c-2 do nothing beyond byte parity when no dump held a typing row; the section 13 row of S11c-2 says so, and S11c-1's README no longer calls these rows oracle-only. Where sections 16.18 and 16.19 say "S5e's oracle PR" and "the fixture PR", read the oracle part and the fixture part of S5e's one PR. S5c and S5d are unchanged: they stay two PRs because `make_conformance.py --check` passes while `v2-binding.json` is not committed, the branch R93 has S5d remove. **CV2-F17** gains R103, the two queued kit-build changes of R100 and R101, and the open check of R98 (a). Its empty-widths item (R70, R78) is done: S5e made the oracle's `ir_eval.py` refuse a widths list on an op without `width_params`, for a document as well as for the kit. The differing wording of two `limit` messages that the S11b review left (match arms, type nesting) is aligned when a dump row needs it; no row does today. The VER-BACKLOG rows for S11c-1-0 and for CV2-F17 are added by the separate docs PR, as for S5-0.

**Not closed by this section.** Whether `v2-fault` gains a typing code, so that a fault row with no common type (R96, R98) can be a kit case and not only an oracle test; that changes a closed code set ([D41]) and is the owner's (closed by [D74], section 16.22: it gains `ill-typed`). Whether an `omit_example` line may omit an example that R99 refuses (`value-over-limit` names the `Bytes` bound today, [D34]); until it is decided the refusal is whole and nothing is skipped. What a lane does with a `pre` rewrite whose value is not a value of the parameter's declared type, as section 16.19 left it.

### 16.21 Owner decisions of 2026-11-26 (dual-lane review, S11c-2 review)

Slice 16.21-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what three reviews needed from this document: the dual-lane comparison of the two lane libraries (S6a, the Python lane, and S7a, the TypeScript lane, merged as #20130, with the Python lane's fixes of #20131), the review of S11c-2 (the generator's `ir.data` evaluation, merged as #20133), and the review of the oracle's CV2-F17 change. Two items needed the owner: [D72] and [D73]. The others are the author's readings **R105** to **R109** (erratum candidates, not rulings). Each was checked against `main` at `e3d93b7d109`: the Python lane (`v2/chunks.py`, `v2/ir.py`, `v2/wtypes.py` of `python/warrant-corpus`), the TypeScript lane (`src/v2/chunks.ts`, `corpus.ts`, `ir.ts`, `types.ts` of `typescript/warrant-corpus`), the oracle (`conformance.py`, `corpus_v2.py`, `suites.py`, `lower.py`, `stream.py`) and the generator's `domains.rs`, `suite.rs` and `stream.rs`; and against two branches that were open when this was written: the oracle's CV2-F17 PR (#20144, `claude/cv2-f17-descriptor-checks-int-refusal` at `2d1c8bc82c5`: R103 and the `Int` refusal) and the Python lane's follow-ups (#20145, `claude/cv2-s6a-lane-followups` at `9e9f893b288`). Where a reading says a tool does something "in #20144" or "in #20145", it is true of that branch and not yet of `main`. The lanes and the oracle were run on small probes for R105 to R108; the generator was read and not run. Sections 2.1, 5.5, 9.3, 11, 13 and 14 were amended in the same change. This erratum moves no committed byte.

**[D72] A demoted suite is `bounded-set` (section 9.3).** The clause of section 9.3 that put "every demoted binder" under `sample` is dropped. A demoted suite's label follows its leaves, like every other suite's: demotion replaces a leaf by `fx_edge` (section 5.1), `fx_edge` is `bounded-set`, so a demoted suite is `bounded-set`. Why: the same sentence of section 9.3 listed `fx_edge` under `bounded-set` and every demoted binder under `sample`, and the two cannot both hold for a binder demoted to `fx_edge`. The decision matches both tools and the committed fixture: the oracle labels an `fx_edge` leaf `bounded-set` (`domains.py`) and so does the generator (`Label::BoundedSet`, `domains.rs`), and the two demoted suites of the committed `lzw-warrant-v2` (`decompress_two_byte_payload_model` and `decompress_missing_clear_rejected`, each `demoted: ["1"]`, a `U16` binder as `fx_edge` of 281 values) carry `domain_label` `bounded-set`. **No byte moves.** "A suite is as weak as its weakest binder" is unchanged, so a demoted suite that also holds a `bytes_n(N>=2)` leaf is `sample` on that leaf's account. The `demoted` list keeps saying which leaves were demoted (section 5.3), which is what makes the label honest.

**[D73] The chunks' `calls` sum to the row's `expected_calls`.** In a chunk file, the sum of the chunks' `calls` equals the suite row's `expected_calls`. It is a **consumer check**, made with the other cross-checks of section 5.5, and a violation is `chunk-file`. Sections 5.5 and 14 and the `chunk-file` row of section 11 now list it. Why: section 5.4 defines a line's `calls` as the `expected_calls` contributed by its chunk, but no check tied the column to the row; `inputs_sha256` and `expected_sha256` frame the per-chunk digests and do not cover `calls`, so `chunks_sha256` alone bound the column, and a row and a file that disagree with each other hash just as well. It is to `calls` what `sum(cases)` is to `cases`. *The tools.* The TypeScript lane already checks it (`readChunkFile`, `chunks.ts`: "the sum of calls is not expected_calls"). The Python lane does not (`check_chunk_file`, `chunks.py`, on `main` and in #20145) and neither does the oracle's kit reader (`check_chunk_file`, `conformance.py`, on `main` and in #20144): **both must add it.** The oracle's `verify-v2` is not affected, since it recomputes every chunk line and compares the file byte for byte (section 10.3). *No committed file violates it.* All 108 suites of the five committed v2 corpora (`ct-compare`, `logic-gates-basic`, `lzw`, `wasm-leb128`, `x25519`) have a chunk file whose `calls` sum to the row's `expected_calls`. Of the 74 committed `v2-chunk` cases, 69 hold a file that sums to its row; the other five (`chunk-file-missing-line`, `chunk-file-extra-line`, `chunk-file-double-space`, `chunk-file-empty-file` and `chunk-file-not-ascii`) expect `chunk-file` already, so no committed outcome moves. Where the check stands among the other `chunk-file` checks is not pinned, as the code is the same. *Vector (an author suggestion, optional; a later kit slice under [D70]):* a `v2-chunk` case whose file is right except that one line's `calls` is one more, with the row's `chunks_sha256` recomputed for the altered file, so that the sum is the only check that fails: `chunk-file`.

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R105. `Int` is refused at generation, not at lowering (section 2.1, [D23]; a coordinator reading, recorded here).** Section 2.1 says `Int` "MUST NOT appear in an op signature, a binder, a stream context, an `examples` row or any other value: a generator meeting it there fails with `W0701`". **"A generator meeting it there" is read as:** lowering **accepts** `Int` in those places, and **derive/generate refuses** it, `W0701` (oracle code `generation`), before anything is written. Refused: `Int` in a **record or variant field** (the `types` table would carry it), in an **op signature** (a parameter or the result) and in a **binder**, looking through aliases, `Option`, `Result`, tuples and lists, so `Option<Int>` and `(Int,Bool)` are refused as `Int` is. *Why generation:* the committed `data-forms-v1` is a **lowering-only** fixture whose document uses `Option<Int>`, and its dump must keep lowering; a lowering refusal would turn a committed parity fixture red for a rule about what a corpus may write. *The oracle, in #20144:* `refuse_int_in_corpus` (`corpus_v2.py`) runs in `derive` after lowering and before anything is built. It is a check of the document in `derive` only: **`emit-v2` keeps its per-suite reports** with the same status and code as before (one reason string, for a suite whose result type is `Int`, now carries the `W0701` text), as it does for the 8-binder bound. A value of type `Int` reaching the JSON writer, the TFV writer or the stream encoder is `W0701` as well. Before, on `main`, `record R(i: Int)` derived a corpus whose `types` row read `"Int"` (probed). The PR's changelog states that no committed byte moves. *What is not refused.* The `type` of a `lit` node and the argument of a `none` are IR and stay (section 2.1, Appendix A.2). A model's **result** is never written to a corpus, so an `Int` result is fine. A model's **parameters are written today** (`models[name].params`), **contrary to [D23]** ("it never appears in an op or model signature"): the models `fits` and `fits16` of `arithmetic.warrant` and six models of `fixed-forms-v1` take an `Int` parameter, and both tools write them. This erratum does not settle that; see "Not closed". **Amended by section 16.22:** [D75] settles it (a model parameter may have the type `Int`), and R111 corrects the count of six: the committed `fixed_forms_v1.oracle-lowering.json` has 31 such models. *The generator* mirrors the oracle after #20144 lands (queued: the same refusal after lowering and before emission, `W0701`); as the note of 2026-11-26 records, it accepts `Int` in a record or variant field and in an op signature today and refuses an `Int` binder late, with a generic `W0701`. That was not re-run here.
* **R106. A `Bytes<N>` value of another length is `W0701` at generation (section 2.3; a CV2-F17 follow-up).** Section 2.3's value table already says it: "an `expected` value ... of a fixed-width type outside its range, or a `Bytes<N>` of another length, cannot be encoded and is `W0701` at generation". The reading adds only that it holds for **a claim and an example alike**, and for an argument as for an expected value, at any depth (under an `ok`, in a tuple, in a record field). The generator already refuses it: its stream encoder returns `W0701`, "a stream value does not have the declared `Bytes<N>` length" (`stream.rs`; read, not run, and whether an example value takes that path was not checked). **The oracle must add the check.** With `op f(a: U8) -> Bytes<2>`, the claim `f(a) == b"aabbcc"` derives a corpus, and so does an example with that expected value, on `main` and in #20144 (probed). #20144 gives the oracle the range half of the same sentence (a `Nat` or fixed-width value outside its range is `W0701` where it was an `OverflowError` or a silently masked value); the length half is the follow-up, under **CV2-F17**, with S11d or in the next oracle PR. No committed byte is expected to move, since the generator's `--check` lines pass on every committed fixture.
* **R107. Readings the two lanes share (sections 1.4, 2.2, 4.3, 5.5; the dual-lane review).** Running the same crafted inputs through both lanes found inputs that one accepted and the other refused. The readings below are the ones both are now built to; each is an author reading, and none changes a committed kit outcome (the Python lane's kit run is unchanged in #20145: 782 cases, 0 failed). At suite level each refusal is `corpus`; in kit IR the same defect is `ill-typed`. (a) **Names are not repeated.** Two binders of one suite, two parameters of one model and two parameters of one op may not share a name: the later would silently shadow the earlier. The S7a review asked for a ruling on model parameters, the document being silent; the reading is to refuse. Both lanes refuse a repeated binder and a repeated model parameter on `main`; a repeated op parameter is refused by the TypeScript lane on `main` and by the Python lane in #20145. (b) **A `reference` suite's body is `eq(subject, expected)`, in either order (section 4.3).** `body` must be the `eq` node over the two sides of `oracle`; `ne`, or any other shape, with `oracle.kind` `reference` is `corpus`. The sides are compared as trees, so `true` and `1` stay different. A `predicate` suite's body is unconstrained. TypeScript on `main`, Python in #20145. (c) **`levels` equals the union of every `requires` (section 1.4, section 16.6 item 2), no more and no less.** A corpus whose `levels` names a level no `requires` holds, or omits one, is `corpus`. The TypeScript lane's corpus reader checks it on `main` (`corpus.ts`). The Python lane has no v2 corpus reader before S6b, and **S6b implements it**. (d) **`unknown-tag` and `subject-forbidden` take precedence over `level-missing` at suite level (section 14; section 16.18 R69 (c) and (d)).** The checks that read tag names alone run over every expression a suite holds (`admit`, `body`, and the two sides of a `reference` oracle) before the suite is recorded as needing a missing level, so an unknown tag or a misplaced `subject_op` cannot hide inside a not-live suite. Within one fault expression the order of the `v2-fault` table is unchanged. The TypeScript lane does this on `main` (`scanTags` in `loadSuite`). **The Python lane does not, at suite level:** it gives `unknown-tag` its precedence within one expression and in a model body, but it compiles a suite's expressions in order, so a suite whose `admit` holds an `ir.fixed` tag and whose `body` holds an unknown tag is `level-missing` in `build_suite`, and so is one whose `admit` holds an `ir.fixed` tag and a `subject_op`, on `main` and in #20145, where the TypeScript lane gives `corpus` for both (probed in both lanes). That is a lane follow-up, listed below. (e) **An error case name follows the name grammar.** Each case of an `error` type matches `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`, the grammar of section 2.2 rule 4, which names fields and constructors only; R72 already requires it of an `errs` entry. TypeScript on `main`, Python in #20145. (f) **The device-name check of a claim applies wherever a suite row is read (section 5.5).** A claim any of whose file-name segments is a Windows reserved device name is `corpus` when the row is loaded, and not only when a chunk file name is derived, so that a row is not half-accepted by a consumer that never opens the file. Both lanes on `main` (`loadSuite`; `build_suite`). What the oracle's kit reader does with (a) to (f) was not checked here.
* **R108. A gap no consumer closes yet: three `types` checks (section 2.2 rules 1 and 4).** Rule 1 forbids a record or variant that contains itself, directly or through any chain, and rule 4 gives the names of fields and constructors; the rules are "all enforced at generation and again by every consumer". Three things follow that no consumer of a `types` table checks today: **a recursive or mutually recursive record or variant**, also through `Option`, a tuple or a `Result`; **a duplicate field name in one record, or a duplicate constructor name in one variant** (rule 4 is read with "names are unique", as rule 2 says of error cases and R103 of `ctors`); and **a field whose type names a type the table does not declare**. The table check of both lanes accepts all three (`check_types_table`, `wtypes.py`; `readTypeTable`, `types.ts`; probed on `main` and in #20145); the Python lane refuses a self-containing record only as `type` when it is the value's type, and the TypeScript lane holds no `ir.data` values to probe. **The oracle refuses all three in a source document**, as `type` (`lower.py`: "a record or variant contains itself", "duplicate field name", "a name is declared twice", "an unknown type name"; probed), so no generated corpus holds such a table. **As a consumer the oracle does not:** its kit reader (`parse_type` and `decode_value`, `conformance.py`) takes the `types` object as given. Probed on `main` and in #20144, a record that contains itself through an `Option` ends a `v2-value` derivation in a Python `RecursionError`, a record with two fields of one name is accepted, and an undeclared field type gives `type` when the record is the value's type but, in a record the value does not use, ends `derive_value` in an uncaught `Refused` at encode time (a crash, not an outcome). So **both lanes and the oracle's consumer side must refuse these**, whole and before any value or descriptor is read, with the code of the context as for the rest of the table (`type` in `v2-value`, `descriptor` in `v2-domain`, `ill-typed` in kit IR and, from [D74], in `v2-fault`, `corpus` in a corpus and in `v2-chunk`). The recursion check matters most: a table that contains itself is a hostile input that makes a type walk not terminate (section 14). It lands with `ir.data` in each lane (S11e, S11g) and in the oracle under **CV2-F17**, before S11d derives vectors for it. Whether the generator checks a `types` object it reads was not looked at; it writes the table from a checked document. *Vectors (an author suggestion, S11d, `v2-value`), each `type`:* a record with a field of its own type; two records each holding the other under `Option`; a record with a repeated field name; a variant with a repeated constructor name; a record whose field type is not in `types`. **Amended by section 16.22:** [D74] gives `v2-fault` its code (R110); R113 and R114 place the check in `v2-binding` and `v2-domain`; R115 pins its order in `v2-ir`; R117 adds a fourth defect, a field naming an error type; and R118 records that `List<Self>` is recursion, which adds a vector: a record with a `List` of itself.
* **R109. The 9-binder claim of `arithmetic.warrant` (section 5.3, section 16.18 R75; the S11c-2 review).** The claim `ripple_carry_adder_with_carry_4_bit_exhaustive` has **nine** `Bool` binders (`a0` to `a3`, `b0` to `b3` and the carry `c`), 512 cases. A claim has at most 8. Both tools now refuse it at derive: the generator with `limit`, "a claim has more than 8 binders" (`domains.rs`, `suite.rs`), and the oracle with the same words (`suites.py`, on `main`; when the S11c-2 review ran, its document path did not enforce the bound and only its kit reader did). So `arithmetic.warrant` as it stands cannot be generated, and the **arithmetic adopter row of section 13 ("5 ops, 63 claims") and the arithmetic row of the section 0 table (63 claims, 2,655,437 cases, measured on the Rust manifest, which has no such bound) are inconsistent with section 5.3 until the adopter changes the claim.** A known fix: two 8-binder claims over `a0` to `a3` and `b0` to `b3` with the carry written as a literal, one for each value, which is 256 + 256 = 512 cases, so the case count stays 2,655,437 and the claim count becomes 64. Raising the bound instead is the other way, and it would be an erratum to section 5.3 and R75 with a kit vector to change. **This is the adopter's to decide, with the owner; it is not decided here**, and the two rows keep their numbers until then. **Resolved by [D76] (section 16.22):** the adopter splits the claim into claims of at most 8 binders, and the bound of 8 stays.

**Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is 16.21-0 (the name is the author's; it has no slice of its own to precede). **CV2-F17** gains the oracle's [D73] check, R106 and the consumer side of R108. The Python lane's [D73] check and R107 (d) go in a lane follow-up PR after #20145, and R107 (c) in S6b. The generator's mirror of R105 follows #20144. The VER-BACKLOG rows (this erratum, the model-parameter question, the lane follow-up) are added by the separate docs PR, as for S5-0.

**Not closed by this section.** **Whether a model parameter may have the type `Int`** (R105): [D23] says no and both tools write such parameters today; either an erratum allows `Int` as a model parameter type, a model row being a signature and never a value, or the `arithmetic` and `fixed-forms-v1` models change and both tools refuse it. It is the owner's, and it is tied to the arithmetic adopter. **How the 9-binder claim is resolved** (R109): split or a higher bound; the adopter's and the owner's. **The checks that are not built yet:** [D73] in the Python lane and in the oracle; R106 in the oracle; R108 in both lanes and in the oracle's consumer side; R107 (d) in the Python lane and R107 (c) in S6b; what the oracle's kit reader does with R107 (a) to (f), not checked; R105 in the generator. Two questions of the S7a review that this erratum does not take up: what a `tuple` descriptor against a scalar type is in `v2-domain`, whose code set has no level code, and what a consumer does with a chunk file that agrees with its row and not with the enumeration. The three items section 16.20 left open stay open. **Amended by section 16.22:** [D75] settles the first item of this paragraph and [D76] the second; the checks that were not built are restated there, with the ones that #20160 and #20161 build removed, and of the three items of section 16.20 the first is closed by [D74].

### 16.22 Owner decisions of 2026-11-26 (the `v2-fault` typing code, `Int` as a model parameter, the 9-binder claim)

Slice 16.22-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what the follow-up of section 16.21 needed from this document. Three items needed the owner: [D74], [D75] and [D76]. Each closes an item that section 16.20 or 16.21 left open (the typing code of `v2-fault`; `Int` as a model parameter, R105; the 9-binder claim, R109). The others are the author's readings **R110** to **R118** (erratum candidates, not rulings). Each was checked against `main` at `cf6f4bf284b`: the oracle (`conformance.py`, `corpus_v2.py`, `bindings_v2.py`, `lower.py` of `python/warrant-oracle`), the Python lane (`v2/chunks.py`, `ir.py`, `faults.py`, `domains.py`, `wtypes.py` of `python/warrant-corpus`), the TypeScript lane (`src/v2/chunks.ts`, `ir.ts`, `faults.ts`, `kit.ts`, `types.ts` of `typescript/warrant-corpus`), the generator (`lower.rs`, `emit.rs`, `view.rs`), the committed kit, the committed corpora and the oracle's committed fixtures; and against three branches that were open when this was written: the oracle's #20160 (`claude/cv2-16-21-orc21` at `d70bbf987a5`: [D73], R106 and R108 in the oracle), the Python lane's #20161 (`claude/cv2-16-21-py21` at `1a05a0198c5`: [D73], R107 (d) and R108 in the lane) and the generator's #20157 (`claude/cv2-gen-int-and-bytes-len-parity`: R105 in the generator, read on its branch for what it leaves alone; it has since merged as `469c48c809d`). Where a reading says a tool does something "in #20160" or "in #20161", it is true of that branch and not yet of `main`. (#20144 and #20145, which section 16.21 described as open, are merged and are on `main`.) The oracle and the Python lane (from the two branches, and from `main` where a reading says so) and the TypeScript lane (from `main`) were run on small probes for R110 to R118; the generator was read and not run. Sections 2.1, 11, 13 and 14 and, in place, [D23], R94, R96, R105, R108 and R109 and the "Not closed" paragraphs of sections 16.20 and 16.21 were amended in the same change. This erratum moves no committed byte.

**[D74] `v2-fault` gains `ill-typed` in its closed code set.** The closed set of `v2-fault` is now `ill-typed`, `level-missing` and `subject-forbidden` (section 11). **Amended 2026-11-26 by [D77] (section 16.23):** the set also holds `unknown-tag`, `depth` and `model-order`. The code covers two refusals: **a bad `types` table**, which is refused whole and first, before any value or expression is read, as `v2-ir` does (a record or variant that contains itself, a repeated field or constructor name, a field naming a type the table does not declare: R108); and **a fault row with no common type** with the parameter or result it replaces, which section 16.20 R96 and R98 refuse (R110 gives the order and the consumers). Why: a `v2-fault` case carries a non-empty `types` (6 of the 41 committed cases do: `missing-error-on-err`, `missing-error-on-ok`, `missing-error-empty-first-argument`, `swap-choice-true`, `swap-choice-false` and `level-missing-ok-form-needs-tuple`), but its code set was `level-missing` and `subject-forbidden` only, so a bad table had no code. The oracle accepts all three defects, and for a field of an undeclared type its `instantiate_fault` ends in an uncaught `Refused` and not in an outcome (`_encoder_spec`, `conformance.py`; probed). The same gap is why R96's second vector and the typing refusals of R98 have no kit vectors (section 16.20, first "not closed" item). **No byte moves:** none of the 41 committed `v2-fault` cases has a bad table (33 are accepted, five are `level-missing` and three `subject-forbidden`) or a typing refusal. Rejected: keeping the closed set and having the kit build refuse such a case. It is smaller, but it leaves R96 and R98 without vectors and gives a lane's table check in a fault case no code. Sections 11 and 14 and R94, R96 and R108 now say so.

**[D75] A model parameter may have the type `Int`.** This amends [D23]. A model row is a signature and never a value: `models[name].params` states a type for each parameter and no value of that type is written. **An op signature, a binder, a stream context, a record or variant field, an `examples` row and every value still refuse `Int`** (section 2.1, R105); only the parameter of a model changes. Why: no byte needs to move, because both tools already write such rows. `arithmetic.warrant` has two models with an `Int` parameter (`fits(s: Int, n: Nat)` and `fits16(s: Int)`) and the committed `fixed_forms_v1.oracle-lowering.json` has **31** (R111 corrects the count of six that R105 gave), the oracle's `corpus_v2.py` writes `params` for every model and so does the generator's `emit.rs`; no committed corpus and no committed kit case holds one, so no committed byte moves either way (R111 has the counts). The other way section 16.21 named, rewriting those models off `Int` and having both tools refuse it, was not taken: it would change the arithmetic adopter's document and move the committed `fixed-forms-v1` lowering dump, which the generator reproduces byte for byte (`view.rs`). Whether a model's *result* may be `Int` is not settled by this decision: R112.

**[D76] The arithmetic claim `ripple_carry_adder_with_carry_4_bit_exhaustive` is split by the adopter.** The adopter splits it into claims of at most 8 binders, for example one with the carry-in written as the literal `0u8` and one with `1u8`, each over `a0` to `a3` and `b0` to `b3` (256 cases each, so the 512 cases and the total of 2,655,437 stay, and the claim count goes from 63 to 64). **The bound of 8 (section 5.3, section 16.18 R75) stays.** This resolves R109 (section 16.21). Why: R109 named two ways, a split or a higher bound, and the owner chose the split. Rejected: raising the bound, which would be an erratum to section 5.3 and R75 with a kit vector to change. *Where the claim is.* In `code/specs/arithmetic.warrant` (the claim after `ripple_carry_adder_4_bit_exhaustive`: nine `Bool` binders `a0` to `a3`, `b0` to `b3` and `c`); its evidence is in `code/packages/rust/arithmetic` (`evidence/test-manifest.json`, the three rows `warrant/arithmetic/{exhaustive,property,fuzz}/ripple_carry_adder_with_carry_4_bit_exhaustive`, and the generated `tests/generated/warrant_evidence.rs`); the Rust harness has no 8-binder bound, so it runs the claim today. The adopter row of section 13 and the arithmetic row of the section 0 table kept 63 until the adopter landed; **it landed as #20171** (`_cin_0` and `_cin_1`), and both now say 64. *The claim name is a stable identifier.* The crate's README says so and `AR01-arithmetic-contract.md` lists the claim with "9 Bool binders"; the adopter's PR decides whether one half keeps the old name and updates both documents with the evidence it regenerates. The split also changes what two generator tests rely on (`tests/fixed_lowering.rs` and `tests/check_cli.rs` of `rust/warrant-corpus-gen`: `arithmetic.warrant` is their refused document for more than 8 binders, so they need another), `rust/arithmetic/assurance_report.json` (it lists the claim's three evidence rows), and the 62-suite and 2,654,925-case figures in the oracle's README and in the `data-eval-v1` README. This erratum edits none of these.

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R110. What `ill-typed` is in `v2-fault`, in what order, and who builds it ([D74]; sections 8.1, 8.2 and 11; section 16.18 R94; section 16.20 R96 to R98).** (a) *The two refusals.* The table: a `types` object that breaks section 2.2 is `ill-typed`, whole, before any `args`, `result` or expression is read; that is the three defects of R108, the fourth of R117 and every other defect of the table that the lanes already refuse in `v2-ir` as `ill-typed` (a malformed row, a name outside the grammar, a count outside rule 3). The row: a `pre` rewrite, or a `post` or `replace` expression, whose type has no common type with what it replaces, by R94 with the tuple, undetermined-component and per-branch relations of R96 to R98. **Reading (R110's, not [D74]'s):** `ill-typed` also covers a fault expression that is ill-typed as a tree, as in `v2-ir`; the refused row `r67/a-one-byte-literal-in-a-record-field-of-bytes-2`, oracle code `ir`, is of this kind. These are the six refused rows among the 11 `typing` rows of `data_eval_v1.oracle-refusals.json`, which the oracle states as `generation` or `ir` and the kit now states as `ill-typed`. (b) *Order.* One error per case (R25), so no vector depends on it. For an implementation: the table (1), then `level-missing` (2), then `subject-forbidden` (3), then the typing of the row (4). A table is read first, as in `v2-ir` (R115); a row that reaches a level the lane lacks is not typed (section 16.18 R69 (c)), and the subject check reads tag names alone; typing needs the compiled expression. This is what the oracle's `derive_fault` does after the table check is added (it returns `level-missing` and `subject-forbidden` before `_compile_fault`, `conformance.py`), what the TypeScript lane does (`runFault` reads the table in the `Program` constructor, then `compileFault`, `faults.ts`: levels, the expressions with the subject check, then `unify`), and what the Python lane does with the table second (`compile_fault`, `faults.py`: the row's shape, the table, `requires`, the compile, then `_may_replace`); so "the table first" is true of the oracle once it is added and of the TypeScript lane, and of the Python lane after its shape check. The two codes of section 11 that were 1 and 2 move to 2 and 3; no committed case holds two codes. **Amended by section 16.23 R119 and R121:** the op row's types are read with the table (1), `unknown-tag`, `depth` and `model-order` ([D77]) come after `level-missing` (3), `subject-forbidden` is 4 and the typing of the row is 5. (c) *Consumers to build.* **The oracle:** `derive_fault` runs the table check first (#20160's `check_type_table`, with `ill-typed`; it does not call it for `v2-fault` today) and gives `ill-typed` where it now raises `KitError("the oracle cannot compile the fault")` for a row with no common type. With the check first, `instantiate_fault` and `op_sig` are never reached with a table that has an undeclared field. **Both lanes** already return `ill-typed` for the typing of a row (`faults.py`; `faults.ts`) and refuse the table of a fault case as **`corpus`**, which is outside the closed set: the Python lane calls `check_types_table(types, "corpus")` (`compile_fault`, `faults.py`; on `main` and in #20161) and the TypeScript lane builds its `Program` with `structuralCode: "corpus"` (`runFault`, `kit.ts`). On `main` the Python lane's call refuses only a malformed row; the three defects of R108 are caught only in #20161. **Both change to `ill-typed`.** (d) *Vectors (an author suggestion; S10d and S11d, one PR each under [D70]).* S11d, with R108's: one `v2-fault` case per table defect (a record with a field of its own type, one holding itself under `List`, two records that hold each other under `Option`, a repeated field name, a repeated constructor name, a field of an undeclared type, a field of an error type), each `ill-typed` on a row that is otherwise accepted. S10d: R96's second vector (the R67 literal of one byte in a `(Bytes<2>,Bool)` component) and the refusals of R98 (an `if` whose other branch is `ok(true)` against a `Result<U8,E>` result; with `ir.tuple`, a wrong second `match` arm), each `ill-typed`. The table vectors need no level beyond `ir.core`: a `types` row that the row does not reach never causes `level-missing` (R69).
* **R111. What [D75] changes for the consumers, and the count it corrects (sections 2.1, 6; [D23]; R105).** (a) *The count.* R105 says six models of `fixed-forms-v1` take an `Int` parameter. The committed `fixed_forms_v1.oracle-lowering.json` has **31** (`m_at_int`, three `m_div_*`, four `m_imax_*`, three `m_imin_*`, four `m_rem_*`, eight `m_try_int_*` and eight `m_wrap_int_*`) and 30 with an `Int` result; `arithmetic.warrant` has two with an `Int` parameter (`fits`, `fits16`) and four with an `Int` result (`sgn`, `sg16`, `tot`, `tot16`). The committed `data_forms_v1.oracle-lowering.json` also has two models whose parameter holds `Int` under `Option` (`opt_widen_eq`, `opt_widen_unwrap`), and five with `Int` inside the result. R105 is corrected in place. (b) *Nothing committed holds one in a corpus.* Of the models of the 248 committed `v2-ir` cases, the 74 `v2-chunk` cases and the five committed corpora (`ct-compare`, `logic-gates-basic`, `lzw`, `wasm-leb128`, `x25519`), none has an `Int` parameter (probed: 0 of the model rows of each file). The rows that do are in the lowering dump, which is not a corpus. (c) *The kit readers refuse it today.* A `v2-ir` case with a model `m(a: Int)` called with an `Int` literal is `Refused("type")` in the oracle (`_models`, `conformance.py`, calls `parse_type` without `allow_int`; `type` is not in the closed set of `v2-ir`, so the build fails) and `ill-typed` in both lanes (`wtypes.py`: "Int is a name inside the IR only"; `types.ts`: "Int is IR-internal"), probed in all three. **All three accept an `Int` parameter in a model row from now on**, in `v2-ir` `models`, in `v2-chunk` `corpus.models` and in a corpus reader's `models`. An `Int` in an op row stays `ill-typed` in both lanes (section 2.1, R105). An `env` entry of type `Int` is no vector: the oracle refuses it as `type`, the Python lane raises a kit error and the TypeScript lane says `ill-typed` (probed); a binder exists only in `v2-chunk` and a corpus, where it is `corpus`. **Amended by section 16.23 R120:** an `env` entry whose type text the reader refuses, `Int` included, is `ill-typed`, and so a vector. **Reading (the author's, not part of [D75]):** [D75] is read through `Option`, `Result`, tuples and `List`, as R105 reads the refusal: a model parameter of type `Option<Int>` or `(Int,Bool)` is accepted as `Int` is. All three kit readers refuse `Option<Int>` today (probed). (d) *Vector (an author suggestion, S10d).* A `v2-ir` case with a model that takes an `Int` parameter and compares it with an `Int` literal, called with an `Int` literal: accepted, with a `Bool` result. The suggested vector has a `Bool` result by choice; an `Int` result is also expressible (14 committed `v2-ir` cases have `expect.type` `Int`). (e) *The generator.* #20157 does not touch a model parameter (its `corpus_check.rs` looks at records, variants, op signatures and binders), so no generator change is needed for [D75]; its comment that calls the parameter case "an open owner question" is closed by it.
* **R112. A model's result type may be `Int`: a reading ([D75] does not say; section 2.1; [D23]).** [D23] says `Int` "never appears in an op or model signature", which includes a result; section 2.1 says it "exists inside the model IR only: as the type of an intermediate". [D75] speaks of parameters. *What the tools do.* A model row of a corpus is `{"params", "body"}` and states no result: the result is the type of the body (`corpus_v2.py`; `emit.rs`). The lowering dumps carry `ret`, and 30 models of `fixed-forms-v1` and four of `arithmetic.warrant` have `Int` there. All three consumers accept a model whose body has type `Int` (probed: a model with no parameter and the body `1 - 2` over `Nat` literals, compared with an `Int` literal: the oracle, the Python lane and the TypeScript lane each accept). **Reading:** a model's result may be `Int`, being the type of an intermediate; no consumer can refuse it, since no row states it. It is a reading, and [D23]'s words are left as they are; the one-line erratum that says so is under "Not closed".
* **R113. R108 reaches `corpus.types` of a `v2-binding` case (section 7, section 11; section 16.19 R85 (a); section 16.21 R108).** The binding validator reads the `types` of the `corpus` it is given. The oracle's `read_corpus` (`bindings_v2.py`) checks that each row has a kind and a non-empty `cases`, `fields` or `ctors`, and parses every field type; it does **not** check recursion or a repeated field or constructor name (it accepts them, probed on `main`), and an undeclared field type is a `BindingKitError`, a kit error and not an outcome. **Reading:** R108 applies to a `v2-binding` case's `corpus.types` with the code of a corpus, `corpus`. `v2-binding` has no such code: its closed set is `BindingInvalid` with a `reason` from the 39 of R85 (d), and none of them is a table defect, so **a case with a bad table cannot be a vector**: it breaks its own schema and is a kit error (section 16.15 R45), which is what the oracle's `BindingKitError` already says for the undeclared type. What changes is that the oracle's reader must raise it for the other two defects and not accept them (a field naming an error type, R117, is already a `BindingKitError` there). A lane's validator reads a real `corpus.json`, whose reader (S6b, S7b) has refused such a table as `corpus` before any binding line is read. Built: in the oracle under CV2-F17 (S5d, which committed `v2-binding`, is merged) and in the lanes with S6b and S7b.
* **R114. R108 and `types` in `v2-domain` (section 11, `v2-domain` row; section 16.15 R44).** No committed `v2-domain` case has a `types` field (0 of 162) and the oracle's `derive_domain` reads none (it parses the binder type with no table, `conformance.py`); section 11 says a later level may add it, optional, absent meaning `{}`. **Reading:** when it is added (S11d: the `variant` and `option` descriptors with R103's refusals), R108 applies with the code `descriptor`, whole and before any descriptor node is read. Both lanes already read a present table with that code (`domains.py`: `check_types_table(types, "descriptor")`; `kit.ts`: `readTypeTable(c["types"], "descriptor")`), and #20161 gives the Python lane's check the three defects; the oracle's side comes with S11d.
* **R115. The order of the checks inside `v2-ir` (section 11; R110 (b); section 16.21 R108).** (a) *What the oracle and both lanes agree on.* The table check comes before every check that reads a tree, a model, an op or a type: `unknown-tag`, `level-missing`, `model-order`, `subject-forbidden`, `depth`, `impure-guard`, the `ill-typed` of a tree, and the cost of a body. #20160 runs `check_type_table(case["types"], "ill-typed")` first in `compile_ir_case`, before `static_refusal` (probed: a table with a repeated field name and the tree `["bogus"]`: `ill-typed`). The Python lane's `Program.__init__` (`ir.py`) reads the table before the `model_order` check and the per-model scan (probed in #20161, the same case: `ill-typed`). The TypeScript lane's constructor (`ir.ts`) reads it at the same place, before the ops, the models and the tree; the lane has not R108 yet, so with a bad table it gives `unknown-tag` today (probed on `main`). (b) *Where they differ.* A `max_ir_cost` outside `2,000..=65,536` together with a bad table: both lanes say `ir-cost`, because the constructor checks the bound before it reads the table (`ir.py`; `ir.ts`), and #20160 says `ill-typed`, because it reads the table first and the bound after `static_refusal` and `undeclared_err_case` (`conformance.py`; probed in the oracle and the Python lane). **The reading pins the lanes' order** because the two lanes on `main` agree and the oracle's order exists only in the unmerged #20160; the bound is one integer of the case and needs no table, so section 14's hostile-table argument is untouched. No vector can observe it (R25); it is pinned so that a lane and the oracle agree on every input. **The order:** the bound on `max_ir_cost`, then the table, then everything else; the oracle follows under **CV2-F17**. No kit case holds both (R25), so no outcome moves. Section 11's sentence that no order among the eight static codes is normative is amended: the table's `ill-typed` comes before the other seven, after the bound on `max_ir_cost` itself. **Amended by section 16.23 R122:** the signature of every op in `ops` (its parameter and result types) is read right after the table, and before every check that reads a tree, a model or `levels`; both lanes do, and the oracle follows under CV2-F17.
* **R116. The Python lane hides a model's unknown tag behind a `list` binder; a reading (section 16.18 R69 (c) and (d); section 16.21 R107 (d)).** (a) *The defect.* `_assemble_suite` (`chunks.py`) builds each binder's descriptor with `build_descriptor` before it builds the `Program` that checks every model whole, and the `list` descriptor is `level-missing` at once (`domains.py`). So a suite with a `List<U8>` binder and a `list` descriptor is `level-missing`, although R69 (d) makes an unknown tag in a model `corpus` wherever the model stands. Probed with a corpus whose one model has the body `["bogus_tag"]`: with the list binder, `level-missing`; with no binder, `unknown-tag` (which the lane maps to `corpus`); probed in #20161, and `main` builds the descriptors before the program in the same way. #20161's `scan_tags` runs over the suite's expressions before the binders and does not reach the models. (b) *The TypeScript lane does not have it:* `readCorpusPart` (`chunks.ts`) builds the `Program`, and so reads every model, before any suite row (probed: `corpus`), and `loadSuite` skips building a descriptor whose binder type needs a level the lane lacks. **Reading:** it is fixed in S6b, whose corpus reader builds the program first, as the TypeScript lane does; S7b needs a test and no change. No kit case reaches it: the kit's suites come from `ir.core` documents that have no list (section 16.15 R43). (c) *What stays open.* A descriptor of a level the lane lacks against a binder type that needs none (a `list` descriptor for `Bytes`) is `level-missing` in both lanes before any tree is scanned (`readNode`, `domains.ts`; `build`, `domains.py`); it belongs to the open question of the `tuple` descriptor against a scalar type (section 16.21, "Not closed").
* **R117. A field that names an error type: not a value type (sections 2.1, 2.2 and 2.3; [D55]; section 16.21 R108).** (a) *The gap.* Section 2.1's grammar says `Name` is "a record, variant or error type from `types`"; section 2.3 gives no value form to a bare error type (an error occurs only as the `{"err":"CASE"}` of a `Result`), and [D55] says no level has an error value. The oracle accepts the table `{"E": {"kind":"error",...}, "R": {"kind":"record","fields":[["a","E"]]}}` (`check_type_table` of #20160 and `check_types_table` of #20161, probed; the TypeScript lane's `readTypeTable` as well) and accepts `record R(e: E)` in a source document, where `_resolve` (`lower.py`) takes `head in spec.errors` as a type; `derive` then writes the field `["e","E"]`. Its decoder rejects every input of type `E` as `value` (probed), so the record has no value, and its own binding reader already says so (`parse_ty` in `read_corpus`, `bindings_v2.py`: "'E' is not a value type", probed). The generator refuses it: `resolve` (`lower.rs`) answers an error name with the type error "an error type is not a value type" (read, not run). (b) *Reading.* An error type's name is not a type of a value. It occurs **only as the second argument of `Result`** (section 2.1: `Result<type, ErrName>`), and nowhere else a type is read as the type of a value: not as the type of a record or constructor field, alone or under `Option`, a tuple or `List`; not as an op parameter or result, a binder, a model parameter or an `env` entry; not as a `v2-value` `type`. The grammar's comment is read to mean a record or variant, and an error type only inside `Result`. (c) *The fix.* It is a fourth defect of R108, refused in every consumer with the code of its context (R110 for `v2-fault`): the oracle's `check_type_table` and `lower._resolve` (`type`, as the generator already says), both lanes' table checks and their type readers. (d) *No committed byte moves.* Every committed `types` table (286 real ones in the five corpora and the kit, and the three non-empty ones in the oracle's fixtures) and every type text of a field, an op, a model, a binder or a result in the five corpora and the kit (5,857 of them) was read: none names an error type outside `Result`'s second argument (the one text that looks like it, `Result<Bool,E` in the `v2-value` case `type-unclosed`, is an unclosed type, a `type` vector).
* **R118. `List<Self>` is recursion (section 2.2 rule 1; section 16.21 R108; a reading the oracle and the Python lane share).** Rule 1 forbids a record or variant that contains itself "directly or through any chain". #20160 (`check_type_table`) and #20161 (`_check_references`) both count an edge through `Option`, `Result` payloads, tuples and `List`, so `record R(a: List<R>)` is refused although an empty list would be a finite value (probed in both). The rule is about the type, not about which values exist. The TypeScript lane does not refuse it yet (probed on `main`); it follows with R108. R108's vector list gains it: a record with a `List` of itself, `type` in `v2-value`.

**Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is 16.22-0 (the name is the author's; it has no slice of its own to precede). **CV2-F17** gains the oracle's `ill-typed` in `v2-fault` (the table first, a typing refusal as the code: R110), the lanes' order of R115 (b), the refusal of an error-named type (R117) in `lower._resolve` and in the table check, the table check in `read_corpus` (R113), and the acceptance of an `Int` model parameter in the kit reader (R111). The lane follow-up gains `ill-typed` for a fault case's table in both lanes (R110), R108 with R118 in the TypeScript lane and R117 in both lanes, the `Int` model parameter in both lanes (R111) and, in S6b, R116. The S10d and S11d vectors of R110 and R111 follow [D70]: they ship with the oracle's tables in one PR. The arithmetic adopter's split ([D76]) is the adopter's PR. The VER-BACKLOG rows (this erratum, the CV2-F17 additions, the lane follow-up, the adopter's split) are added by the separate docs PR, as for 16.21-0.

**Not closed by this section.** **Whether `Int` as a model result needs a line in [D23]** (R112): the tools accept it and no row states it. **The checks that are not built yet:** the oracle's `ill-typed` in `v2-fault`, the order of R115 (b) in the oracle, the table check of R108 in the oracle's `v2-binding` reader (R113) and in `v2-domain` when it gets a `types` field (R114), R117 in the oracle; `ill-typed` for a fault case's table, R108 with R118 in the TypeScript lane and R117 in both lanes, the `Int` model parameter in the oracle's kit reader and in both lanes; R107 (c) in S6b and R116 in S6b. R105 in the generator is built (#20157, merged as `469c48c809d`). [D73], R106, R107 (d) and R108's oracle and Python-lane checks are built in the open #20160 and #20161 and are not listed again. **What the oracle's kit reader does with R107 (a) to (f)**, checked here by reading `check_corpus_and_row` (`conformance.py`): it does not check (a) a repeated binder name, (b) the shape of a `reference` body, (e) the grammar of an `error` case name in the table, or (f) a device name in the claim, and (c) and (d) do not apply to it (it reads no `levels` and no tag); whether it should is not decided. **Whether the generator checks a `types` object it reads** was not looked at. Two questions of the S7a review stay open: what a `tuple` descriptor against a scalar type is in `v2-domain`, whose code set has no level code (R116 (c) is the same question for `list`), and what a consumer does with a chunk file that agrees with its row and not with the enumeration. *(Updated by section 16.23, whose "Not closed" paragraph carries this list forward and removes what #20169, #20170 and #20172 built.)* Of the three items section 16.20 left open, [D74] closes the first (the typing code); **whether an `omit_example` line may omit an example that R99 refuses** and **what a lane does with a `pre` rewrite whose value is not a value of the parameter's declared type** stay open.

### 16.23 Owner decision of 2026-11-26 (the static codes of `v2-fault`) and the readings that follow

Slice 16.23-0 (this erratum, spec text only; no code and no emitted byte changes) records what the dual-lane comparison of the oracle, the Python lane and the TypeScript lane left undecided once section 16.22 was built. One item needed the owner: [D77]. The others are the author's readings **R119** to **R127** (erratum candidates, not rulings). Each was checked against `main` at `6d02c654fea`: the oracle (`conformance.py` and `lower.py` of `python/warrant-oracle`), the Python lane (`v2/faults.py`, `ir.py`, `chunks.py`, `wtypes.py` and `kit.py` of `python/warrant-corpus`), the TypeScript lane (`src/v2/kit.ts`, `faults.ts`, `ir.ts`, `types.ts` and `chunks.ts`) and the grammar checker's reserved lists (`checker.rs` of `rust/warrant-grammar`). Where a reading says "probed", the three tools were run on one small hand-written case (the oracle's `derive_fault`, `derive_ir` and `check_corpus_and_row`; each lane's kit runner), the Python lane and the oracle as libraries and the TypeScript lane under `node`; the generator was not read for this erratum. **What had landed since section 16.22** (context, not readings): #20172 (the oracle: `ill-typed` in `v2-fault`, the `Int` model parameter, the whole-table checks of R113, R115 (b) and R117), #20170 (the Python lane: `ill-typed` in `v2-fault`, the `Int` model parameter, error-type fields, the eager member parse and the list-binder order of R116), #20169 (the TypeScript lane: the table read whole with R108, R117 and R118, `ill-typed` in `v2-fault`, `Int` model parameters) and #20173 (the Python lane: the R58 reserved names, and a non-object `types` refused in `v2-ir` and `v2-domain`). #20174 (the TypeScript lane's counterpart of #20173: `types: null` in `v2-value` and the R58 names; branch `claude/cv2-lane-ts-followups` at `d2fae5d5995`) had **not merged** when this was written, so where a reading says "on `main`" it is true of the TypeScript lane without it; R123 says what the branch does. Section 11 and, in place, R45, R58, R101, R110, R111 and R115, the "Not closed" paragraph of section 16.22 were amended in the same change, and the table of section 13 gains its row 16.23-0. This erratum moves no committed byte: of the 41 committed `v2-fault` cases, 33 are accepted, five are `level-missing` and three `subject-forbidden`, none holds an unknown tag or a `model_call`, and the deepest fault expression is 7 nodes (read from `v2-fault.json` on `main`); none of the 248 committed `v2-ir` cases has an `Int` in an `env` type or an op row.

**[D77] `v2-fault`'s closed code set gains `unknown-tag`, `depth` and `model-order`, with the same meaning as in `v2-ir`.** The closed set of `v2-fault` is now `ill-typed`, `level-missing`, `subject-forbidden`, `unknown-tag`, `depth` and `model-order` (section 11). Why: a fault expression can hold an unknown tag, be nested over 64 deep, or call a model that is not in `model_order`. Both lanes already return those three codes there, but they were outside the closed set, so a lane's answer could not be a kit outcome. **The oracle does not return them** (R119 (b): two end in an exception and the third is written as `ill-typed`). The owner was asked on the statement that all three tools already return the three codes there; drafting this section found that only the lanes do. The decision stands as given; what it adds is oracle work (R119 (d), CV2-F17), not only vectors; with this decision the kit can carry the vectors once the oracle does (S10d, S11d). Rejected (the other options put to the owner): folding them into `ill-typed`, which would make every tool remap them inside `v2-fault` only and make the code of one defect depend on the category; and treating such a case as a kit error with no vector (R45), which would leave a defect that two lanes already detect without a vector. *The order* is not part of this decision: it is the author's reading in R119 (c), with what each tool does today in R119 (b); *the undefined level name in `fault.requires`*, which is outside the closed set, is read as a kit error in R126, and the owner's call, if the reading is not accepted, is listed under "Not closed".

**Author refinements (not owner decisions).**

* **R119. The three codes in `v2-fault`: meaning, what the tools do, the order ([D77]; sections 4.2, 8.1, 11 and 14; section 16.22 R110).** (a) *Meaning.* **`unknown-tag`:** a tag of no level this document defines, in a `post` or `replace` `expr` or in the `expr` of a `pre` rewrite. **`depth`:** an expression nested more than 64 deep (section 14): an expression 64 nodes deep is accepted and one of 65 refused (63 and 64 nested `concat`s over a `var`, probed in all three tools); the second limit of the code, more than 64 active `model_call` frames, cannot occur here. **`model-order`:** a `v2-fault` case has no `models` field (section 11), so every `model_call` in a fault expression names a model that is missing from `models` and from `model_order`. This is a statement about the kit case, which has no `models` (section 11: "an S5 fault expression calls no model"); section 8.1 does not forbid a `model_call` in a corpus fault row, where the expression is compiled against the corpus's models. (b) *What the tools do today* (probed with `post` and `pre` rows over `echo(a: Bytes) -> Bytes`). **Both lanes** return the three codes (`scan_tags` and the compile of `ir.py` in the Python lane; `fail("depth", ...)`, `fail("model-order", ...)` in `ir.ts`). **The oracle does not.** `derive_fault` calls `fault_levels` (`L.ir_levels`) outside any handler, so an unknown tag ends in an uncaught `OracleError` ("an unknown IR tag"); a nesting over 64 reaches its `except OracleError` clause with the code `limit`, which is not in the clause's list of typing codes, and ends in `KitError("the oracle cannot compile the fault: limit: IR nesting exceeds 64 ...")`; and a `model_call` is **written as `ill-typed`** (the compile fails with the code `ir`, which the clause maps to `ill-typed`: `conformance.py`, `derive_fault`). The third is the worse of the three: a vector derived today would carry an expectation that both lanes fail. `derive_ir` has what is needed (`static_refusal` for `unknown-tag` and `model-order`, `classify_ir_refusal` for `depth`); `derive_fault` calls neither. (c) *The order.* One error per case (R25), so no vector depends on it. For an implementation: the table **and the op row's types** (1: R121), then `level-missing` (2), then `unknown-tag`, `depth` and `model-order` (3, no order among the three), then `subject-forbidden` (4), then the typing of the row (5). It is where each code falls in `v2-ir`'s static order, with one difference: `v2-ir` reads the tree before it decides the levels (section 11 pins no order there), and a fault row states its `requires` as a field, so both lanes decide `level-missing` from it before they read an expression (`compile_fault`, `faults.py`: the `requires` check before the compile; `compileFault`, `faults.ts`: `program.lacks` before the expressions; probed: an unknown tag with `levels` `[]` is `level-missing` in both). This keeps the order R107 (d) left to the `v2-fault` table. It is not the suite-level rule of R69 (d) and section 14, under which a tag-name check still applies to a control that needs a missing level; whether a corpus reader must refuse an unknown tag in a not-live control's fault row is listed under "Not closed". *Why the three come before `subject-forbidden`:* **`unknown-tag`** in both lanes (the oracle raises on the tag before its subject scan; probed with a `subject_op` and an unknown tag in one expression); **`depth`** in both lanes (the oracle gives `subject-forbidden`: its `has_subject` scan precedes the compile); **`model-order`** in the TypeScript lane always, in the Python lane when the `model_call` is met before the `subject_op`, and never in the oracle. *Why they come before the typing of the row:* both lanes (probed: an ill-typed `not` beside a nesting of 70, or beside a `model_call`, gives `depth` and `model-order`); the oracle answers `ill-typed` when the ill-typed node comes first (with the nesting first it raises the `KitError` of (b)). **So the oracle moves** (all three of its answers above), and the Python lane's `model-order` after a `subject_op` is a lane follow-up. **Not pinned:** the order among the three (probed: an unknown tag beside a nesting of 70 is `depth` in the Python lane whichever comes first, `unknown-tag` or `depth` by position in the TypeScript lane, the oracle raises on the unknown tag), as for the eight static codes of `v2-ir`. (d) *Built how.* The oracle's `derive_fault` reads the table and the op row, then compares `requires` with `levels` (it levels only the tags it knows, as `static_refusal` does, and not through `ir_levels`, which raises), then runs `static_refusal`'s two tag checks and the depth mapping of `classify_ir_refusal`, then the subject scan, then the typing; **CV2-F17**. (e) *Vectors (an author suggestion; S10d, with S11d where the row holds an `ir.data` tag, one PR each under [D70]).* Three `v2-fault` cases at `levels` `["ir.core"]` and row `requires` `["ir.core"]`, each otherwise accepted: `["bogus", ["var", "a"]]`, an expression 65 nodes deep (64 `concat`s over `["var", "a"]`), and `["model_call", "m", [["var", "a"]]]`. Their envelope `requires`: for the `unknown-tag` case it is the case's `levels` (R24), for the other two the row's `requires`; `requires_of` gains that branch with R119 (d).
* **R120. An `env` entry whose type the reader refuses is `ill-typed` in `v2-ir` (sections 2.1, 4.2 and 11; section 16.22 R111 (c), R117).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed, an entry `["x", {"type": T, "value": ...}]`). For `T` = `Int`, `Option<Int>`, `List<Int>`, a name `types` does not declare, `Result<U8,Q>` with `Q` a record, `Bytes<07>`, `Bytes<5000>`, `U8<` or the empty text: the oracle refuses with the code `type`, which is outside the closed set of `v2-ir` (the build fails: `compile_ir_case`, `conformance.py`, re-raises everything but `ERROR_NOT_VALUE`); the Python lane raises a `KitError` ("an env type is malformed", `outcome_ir`, `kit.py`); the TypeScript lane says `ill-typed`. An entry naming an error type is `ill-typed` in all three since R117. Every other type text of `v2-ir` is `ill-typed` in all three (probed: the written type of a `lit` for `U8<`, `Bytes<07>`, an undeclared name and `Bytes<5000>`; an op row; a model parameter). (b) *Reading.* The type of an `env` entry is part of the case's typing, as a `lit`'s written type is, and `v2-ir` has no `type` code: **any refusal of an `env` type text is `ill-typed`**, and so a vector. A kit error stays for an entry that is not `[name, {"type": text, "value": V}]` (a missing `type`, a non-text type) and for a `value` that does not decode as its type: both are breaks of the case's own schema (R45; probed: a missing `type` is a `KeyError` in the oracle and the Python lane and a harness error in the TypeScript lane; a value of 999 for `U8` is `value` in the oracle and the TypeScript lane, outside the closed set, and a kit error in the Python lane). R111 (c)'s "no vector" for an `Int` entry is amended. (c) *Who moves.* The oracle (`type` becomes `ill-typed` in the `env` clause of `compile_ir_case`) and the Python lane (`outcome_ir` turns its `KitError` into the refusal `ill-typed`); the TypeScript lane does not. (d) *Vectors (an author suggestion, S10d):* an entry of type `Int`, one of an undeclared type and one of `Result<U8,Q>` with `Q` a record, each with `ir` `["lit", true]` and each `ill-typed`. (e) *Not pinned.* The order of an `env` type refusal against a tree defect: the oracle reads the tree first (`unknown-tag`), the Python lane raises whatever the tree is, and the TypeScript lane is itself two-faced (with a `["bogus"]` tree an `Int` entry gives `ill-typed` and an undeclared name `unknown-tag`). No vector can hold both (R25).
* **R121. An op row of a `v2-fault` case is read with the table: order 1 (sections 6, 11 and 16.22 R110 (b)).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed: an op parameter or result naming `Int`, an undeclared type, an error type, or `Result<U8,Q>` with `Q` a record, with an otherwise accepted `post`). The oracle (`op_sig` in `derive_fault`, with `type` mapped to `ill-typed`) and the TypeScript lane (`checkNames(..., "ill-typed")`, `runFault`, `kit.ts`) answer `ill-typed` even when the row's `requires` names a level the case's `levels` lacks; the Python lane answers `level-missing` there (`compile_fault` checks `requires` at `faults.py` before it builds the `Program` that reads the op row; probed with `levels` `[]` and with `requires` `["ir.fixed"]` at `["ir.core"]`). All three answer `ill-typed` before `subject-forbidden` and before the three codes of R119 (probed with a `subject_op`, an unknown tag and a `model_call`). A type that needs a level (`Option<U8>` as a parameter at `["ir.core"]`) is no defect of the row: `level-missing` in all three. (b) *Reading.* The op row's parameter and result types are part of what the table check reads: `ill-typed`, order 1, before `level-missing`. This is the majority (the oracle and the TypeScript lane); **the Python lane moves**: it builds the `Program`, or reads the op row, before its `requires` check. (c) *Observability.* R25 means no vector holds a bad op row together with a level defect, so the order against `level-missing` cannot be seen; the refusal itself can: a `v2-fault` case whose op row names `Int`, an error type or an undeclared type, otherwise accepted, is `ill-typed` (S10d, an author suggestion). R110 (b)'s order is amended.
* **R122. In `v2-ir` the signature of every op is read after the table and before everything else (sections 6 and 11; section 16.22 R115).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed: an op that `ir` never calls, with a parameter or result naming `Int`, an error type, an undeclared type or `Result<U8,Q>`, together with each of: a `["bogus"]` tree, `levels` `[]`, a `model_call` to a missing model, a nesting of 70, a `subject_op` under role `fault`). **Both lanes** answer `ill-typed` every time. **The oracle** answers `unknown-tag`, `level-missing`, `model-order` and `subject-forbidden` for the four tree defects and `ill-typed` for the nesting: `compile_ir_case` runs `static_refusal` before it reads `ops` (`_ops`). R115 pinned the bound and the table only. (b) *Reading.* R115's order gains a step: the bound on `max_ir_cost`, the table, **the parameter and result types of every op in `ops`** (a type text the reader refuses, `Int`, an error type outside `Result`'s second argument, an undeclared name: `ill-typed`, reached or not), then everything else. The lanes agree and the oracle follows under **CV2-F17**. A signature that needs a level the case lacks stays `level-missing` scoped by reach (R69), so an unreached op row with `Option<U8>` is accepted (probed in all three). (c) *What is left open.* A **model's** signature: with a `["bogus"]` tree both lanes give `ill-typed` for a model parameter naming an error type or an undeclared name; with `levels` `[]` the Python lane gives `level-missing` (the tree needs `ir.core`) and the TypeScript lane `ill-typed`. The oracle gives the tree's code in both. No vector can observe it (R25).
* **R123. R58's reserved set for a `types` table (section 2.2; R58; section 16.10 R2; backlog CV2-F8).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed as record and constructor names in a `v2-ir` table). The oracle's table check refuses `_DECLARED_RESERVED_V1` (`conformance.py` for a row name and for a constructor name; `lower.py`): `V0_BUILTINS`, the v1 types and the v1 functions (`rem`, `div_trunc`, `checked_add`, ...), but not `take`, `drop`, `imin`, `imax` or the lowerer's level names (`slice`, `count`, `fold`). The Python lane refuses `RESERVED_DECLARED_NAMES` (`wtypes.py`): `V0_BUILTINS` and every primitive type name, so `Some`, `len`, `fixed`, `at` and `U16` but not `rem`. The TypeScript lane on `main` has no R58 check (the type names are refused as a record name, `Some`, `len`, `fixed`, `at` are accepted and so is `U16` as a constructor); the open #20174 gives it the Python lane's set, with the comment that the v1 names are read in every document. (b) *Reading.* R58 and CV2-F8 are version scoped because the checker's `is_reserved_builtin(version, name)` is (`checker.rs`): `V0_BUILTINS` in every document, the v1 types and functions in a v1 document. **A `types` table carries no document version**, so a version-scoped set cannot be applied to it. The v1 *function* names cannot be refused in a table: Probed: `error rem = A | B` and `error div_trunc = A | B` lower in a v0 document and are refused in a v1 one, so a corpus generated from a v0 document may hold an error row called `rem`, and the oracle's consumer-side check would refuse it. (Records and variants are v1 constructs, probed: "`record` is a v1 construct in a v0 document", so for those rows the v1 set was exact; one set for the whole table is simpler, and the looseness costs nothing, because no producer can write such a table.) **The set for a table:** `V0_BUILTINS` plus the six v1 type names (`U16`, `U32`, `I8`, `I16`, `I32`, `I64`; the lanes' set holds exactly these), for a record, variant or error row name and for a constructor name. The six are kept although the checker reserves them in a v1 document only, because section 2.1's type grammar reads them as primitive types in every corpus. **This does refuse a table a valid v0 document produces:** `error U16 = A | B` lowers in a v0 document and the oracle's `derive` writes the row `"U16"` (probed), which the oracle's own table check and both lanes refuse today. No committed table has such a row. Whether a producer refuses such a name in a v0 document or the consumers drop the six names is not settled here ("Not closed"); a field name is not covered (R58 is about declarations). This is the lanes' set and #20174's. (c) *Who moves.* The oracle's **consumer-side** `check_type_table` takes this set in place of `_DECLARED_RESERVED_V1`; its lowering keeps the version-scoped set for a source document. The Python lane does not move; the TypeScript lane gets it with #20174. No committed byte moves: no name in the kit's tables (8 distinct names) or in the nine committed corpus and oracle-fixture tables read (6 distinct names) is reserved under either set.
* **R124. An unreached model whose types or tags need a missing level is accepted, and the oracle is wrong (section 16.18 R69 (c) tiers 2 and 3, (g), R101; [D64], [D75]).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed: `levels` `["ir.core"]`, a model nothing calls). A parameter `Option<U8>`, `Option<Int>`, `List<U8>` or `List<Int>`, or a body with `some`: both lanes accept (a value); the oracle answers `level-missing`. With `levels` `["ir.core", "ir.list"]` the oracle's `List<Int>` ends in `KitError("an oracle failure with no kit code: level: a type that needs a reserved level")`, since its evaluator has no `ir.list`; with `ir.data` in `levels` it accepts `Option<U8>` and `Option<Int>`. A parameter `(U8,Bool)`, `(Int,Bool)` or `Result<Int,E>` is accepted by all three; the same `Option<U8>` parameter on a model that `ir` calls is `level-missing` in all three. (b) *Who is right: the lanes.* Tier 2 of R69 (c): a model that needs a level outside `levels` is not compiled; (g) and tier 3: only `level-missing` is scoped by reach, and a model that `ir` does not reach causes none. R101 names the cause: the oracle compiles **every** model (`_models`, `conformance.py`: the `probe` compile of each body, and `prog.model` of each name), so it derives `level-missing` or a kit error where a lane at those `levels` returns the value, and says a row of that kind is not a case. R101's build refusal is not built on `main`; `derive_ir` returns `level-missing`, an expectation that both lanes fail. [D75] changes nothing here: the oracle accepts an `Option<Int>` model parameter when the levels hold `ir.data` (probed; the lanes lack the level). (c) *The fix.* The oracle's `_models` leaves uncompiled a model whose parameter types or tags need a level outside the case's `levels` and that `ir` does not reach, and applies to it the tag-name checks of tier 2 only. Until then R101's refusal must raise a kit error for the row; no lane changes. (d) *Vector (an author suggestion, S11d, with the `ir.data` vectors):* a `v2-ir` case at `["ir.core"]` with an uncalled model whose parameter is `Option<U8>`: a value.
* **R125. The oracle's `v2-chunk` reader never reads `corpus.ops`, `corpus.models` or `model_order` (section 11, `v2-chunk` row; R117 (c); section 16.22 R111).** `check_corpus_and_row` (`conformance.py`) reads `types`, `chunk_size`, `max_ir_cost`, the suite row and its binders; the three fields are in `CHUNK_CORPUS_KEYS` and never read. Probed on the `kit-small` corpus part of the oracle's fixtures: an op parameter or result naming `Int`, an error type, an undeclared type or `Result<U8,Q>`, unparseable text (`U8<`), a model parameter naming an error type or an undeclared name, an unknown tag in a model body, a `model_order` naming a model that is not in `models`, and an `ops` or `models` that is not an object are **accepted by the oracle** and `corpus` in both lanes. A model parameter `Int`, `Option<Int>` or `(Int,Bool)` on a model the suite does not call is accepted by all three ([D75]). **Reading:** R117 (c) wants these refused with the code of the context, here `corpus`, so the oracle is the one that moves. It is a **CV2-F17** item: the oracle's reader reads `corpus.ops`, `corpus.models` and `model_order` as a lane's corpus reader does, which is the same check as R122's (b) and R124's for an unreached model needing a level. Once it does, a vector is possible (S10d, an author suggestion): a `v2-chunk` case whose `corpus.ops` has an op with an `Int` parameter, `corpus`.
* **R126. A malformed fault row and an undefined level name are kit errors (section 8.1; section 4.2 rule 7; R45 (f); [D77]).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed: a `post` row with an extra key, a `kind` of `swap`, a missing `expr`, a `requires` that is not a list, an op that is not a name, a `pre` row with no rewrites, with a rewrite that lacks `expr`, that names no parameter or names one twice). The Python lane answers `ill-typed` (`_check_fault_shape`, `_check_rewrite_rows`, `faults.py`), the TypeScript lane `corpus` (`compileFault`, `faults.ts`). The oracle does not check the row's schema: an extra key is ignored, a `kind` that is not `pre` is run as `post`, a missing key is a `KeyError`, a rewrite of no parameter is accepted. A `requires` naming an undefined level (`["ir.bogus"]`) is `corpus` in both lanes (`check_requires(requires, "corpus", ...)`; `DEFINED_LEVELS`) and a `KitError` in the oracle (its computed levels differ from the row's `requires`). With a bad table as well: the Python lane gives `corpus` for an undefined level (it reads the row's shape first) and `ill-typed` for an extra key, the TypeScript lane `ill-typed` for both (it reads the table first), the oracle `ill-typed` for both. (b) *Reading.* The row is a field of the case and section 8.1 gives it as a closed object of one of three kinds; a case whose `fault` is not one breaks its own schema, which R45 (f) keeps out of the kit for good: **a kit error, never a vector, with no code.** An undefined level name in `fault.requires` is the same (section 4.2 rule 7 makes it a corpus error in a corpus; in a kit case it is a schema break). `ill-typed` in section 11 covers the types table and the typing of the row's expressions: the "malformed row" of R110 (a) is a row of `types`. Neither lane's code for the schema is pinned, because the kit holds no such case; a fault row in a real `corpus.json` is `corpus` (S6b, S7b). The order with a bad table needs no pin for the same reason, **except in the oracle**: its `derive_fault` reads the table first and *returns* `ill-typed`, so a case with a bad table and a malformed row would be written as an `ill-typed` vector that the Python lane fails (an undefined level) or passes by accident. It reads the row's schema first and raises. (c) *The owner's call.* A code for these (they are outside the closed set) is the owner's, if the reading is not accepted; it would be a change to a closed set ([D41]), with a vector per defect. Listed under "Not closed".
* **R127. A not-live suite in `v2-chunk` (sections 5.5 and 11; section 16.18 R69 (d), (e); section 16.21 R107 (d)).** (a) *What the tools do* (probed with the oracle's `kit-small` corpus and a suite row whose `requires` holds `ir.list` and whose one binder is a `list` descriptor, the lanes' `levels` being `ir.core`). The Python lane returns `level-missing`, which is not a `v2-chunk` code; the TypeScript lane maps it to `corpus` (`runChunk`, `kit.ts`). With a repeated binder name or a row `kind` other than `exhaustive` added, the Python lane still returns `level-missing` (it builds the descriptors first, as R116 (a) found for a model) and the TypeScript lane `corpus`; with an extra or a missing key, or an unknown tag in the body, both return `corpus`. (b) *Reading, for the kit.* None of these is an outcome. A `v2-chunk` case's envelope `requires` is the suite row's (`requires_of`, `conformance.py`), so a lane without `ir.list` skips the case and counts it skipped (section 9.1); no harness is given one, `level-missing` is not a code of the category, and the Python lane's raw code and the TypeScript lane's mapping are both unobservable. R69 (e)'s `corpus` is for a suite whose `requires` is *inside* the lane's levels; the TypeScript lane's mapping of every `level-missing` goes beyond it, harmlessly. (c) *Reading, for a corpus reader (S6b, S7b).* A suite that needs a missing level is recorded `not-live` (R69, [D64]). R69 (d) makes the key sets corpus errors "wherever they stand", and its reason (a corpus must not hide a defect behind a level) covers the defects that read no node shape: **a repeated binder name (R107 (a)) and a row `kind` other than `exhaustive` are `corpus` in a not-live suite too.** The TypeScript lane does this; the Python lane's corpus reader (S6b) validates the row's own fields before it builds a descriptor, the fix R116 made for a model.

**Names, order and the backlog.** This PR is 16.23-0 (the name is the author's; it has no slice of its own to precede). **CV2-F17** gains the oracle's three codes in `v2-fault` with their order (R119), `ill-typed` for an `env` type (R120), the schema check of a fault row before the table (R126), the op signatures read after the table in `v2-ir` (R122), the version-independent R58 set in its table check (R123), the unreached model that needs a missing level (R124), and the reading of `corpus.ops`, `corpus.models` and `model_order` in `v2-chunk` (R125). The lane follow-up gains, for the Python lane, the op row before the `requires` check (R121), `ill-typed` for an `env` type (R120), `model-order` before a later `subject_op` (R119) and, in S6b, the row's fields before the descriptors (R127); for the TypeScript lane, #20174's R58 names (R123), and nothing else. The vectors of R119 (e), R120 (d), R121 (c), R124 (d) and R125 follow [D70]: they ship with the oracle's change in one PR (S10d for `ir.core`, S11d for the `ir.data` ones). The VER-BACKLOG rows (this erratum, the CV2-F17 additions, the lane follow-up) are added by the separate docs PR, as for 16.22-0.
